## Acts 17:22–32

*June 7, 1936*

So Paul, standing in the middle of the Areopagus, said: "Men of Athens, I perceive that in every way you are very religious. For as I passed along, and observed the objects of your worship, I found also an altar with this inscription, "To an unknown god.' What therefore you worship as unknown, this I proclaim to you. The God who made the world and everything in it, being Lord of heaven and earth, does not live in shrines made by man, nor is he served by human hands, as though he needed anything, since he himself gives to all men life and breath and everything. And he made from one every nation of men to live on all the face of the earth, having determined allotted periods and the boundaries of their habitation, that they should seek God, in the hope that they might feel after him and find him. Yet he is not far from each one of us, for

> In him we live and move and have our being ;

as even some of your poets have said,

> 'For we are indeed his offspring'

Being then God's offspring, we ought not to think that the Deity is like gold, or silver, or stone, a representation by the art and imagination of man. The times of ignorance God overlooked, but now he commands all men everywhere to repent, because he has fixed a day on which he will judge the world in righteousness by a man whom he has appointed, and of this he has given assurance to all men by raising him from the dead."

Now when they heard of the resurrection of the dead, some mocked; but others said, "We will hear you again about this."

### I

PAUL's speech consists in an attack on heathendom; he does not attack it, however, because of its immorality or impiety, but rather because of its pious and religious sentiment. He assesses it as so much superstition. Why?

Wandering through Athens he had seen many temples and altars where worship and sacrifice were given to many gods, and "his spirit was provoked within him as he saw that the city was full of idols" (v. 16).

What is the really superstitious element in this polytheistic worship? Might it for example be merely this, namely, that heathen worship was based on false conceptions, springing from a childish mode of thought, whereas Christianity, with its teaching about the unity of God, brought enlightenment and swept away all such false conceptions by its essential truth and maturity of thought? Certainly not. Paul could not have meant this, for the simple reason that he knew that Greek culture had long ago transcended the stage of polytheism, and that. Greek philosophers taught, like Christianity, the unity of the God who is immanent in the world, in whom "we live and move and have our being"

', and to whom man is related in the depths of his inner life. In fact he quotes a line from one of the Greek philosophical poets, "For we are indeed His offspring", which implies the offspring of the one God. Those philosophers taught that the gods who are worshipped in the various temples according to ancient custom are but energies and emanations flowing from the primal Godhead which is everywhere worshipped under a diversity of names. Hence the struggle of Christianity against idolatry did not consist in the fact that it had to remove, by its superior enlightenment, false heathenish notions of God.

Hence what precisely is the superstitious element in heathen religion? It consists in two factors.

1. Polytheism is an expression of man's fundamental anxiety. The worship of gods arose of course from the fact that the world is mysterious and uncanny for mankind. Mysterious and uncanny are the forces of nature and the caprices of fate, which sometimes lavish on man their blessing, sometimes bring ruin and destruction; now promoting his efforts, now frustrating them. Mysterious and uncanny again are the forces which break out from the depths of man's mind, his impulses and passions, leading him sometimes to good, sometimes to evil; now inspiring him to

IO accomplish great deeds, now enticing him and fettering him in the dark regions of guilt where he becomes terrified at himself. Man who feels himself surrounded thus by the mysterious and the uncanny, who is not secure of himself, who is aware that his life is both driven onward and thwarted by forces which are more powerful than himself, lives in anxiety; and in his anxiety he worships these transcendent forces as gods. And even when finally he concludes that the various powers are one and the same, he is still not freed from this anxiety; for he still does not know the one God who is Lord of heaven and earth.

The more gods or the more altars there are, so many more the symbols of human fear. The altar consecrated "to an unknown god" was also such a symbol. For such altars (and they bear too the inscription: "To the god whom it concerns") arise from this enveloping fear: here is a god at work whom I do not yet know and whose name I do not yet know! Hence he must have an altar at which he may be so to speak anonymously worshipped.

2. The second factor emerges from the words of Paul: "The God who made the world and everything in it... does not live in shrines made by man" (v. 24ff.), "and we ought not to think that the Deity is like gold or silver or stone, a representation by the art and imagination of man" (v. 29). Here again the superstitious element does not reside in false conceptions. For by the time of Paul those old conceptions had been superseded, according to which God had a human form, was bound to a particular place, and needed to be sustained by a human worship and culture.

The real superstitious element resides rather in the intention which lies at the basis of the sensuous representation and cultic worship of the Deity. That intention is to secure control of the divine. Religion is the attempt to conquer the fear of those dark powers which, springing from nature and fate and the depths of the human soul, stand threateningly across man's path; it is the attempt to exorcize these powers by embodying them in the form of images, by making their nearer acquaintance, by establishing a regular means of communion with them, almost as one gets to know other human beings. Man who subjugates the threatening world more and more to his own purposes, seeks also to integrate into the structure of his ordered world that mystery which once peered at him with a thousand eyes out of the surrounding darkness and lurked around his footsteps. He seeks to give it a place which, though still imparting awe and fear, shines with the clear light of daily life and becomes the centre of a regularized cult. In fact, in proportion as man devotes himself with efficiency and enthusiasm to such ordered worship, his primal fear is benumbed and the mystery of his existence concealed by his religion.

This is illustrated by the story of the very place on which Paul delivered his discourse, for it was the shrine at which those dread demons of blood vengeance, the Erinnyes, were worshipped. Yet their cult resulted in their being given the name of the Eumenides, the gracious ones.

This then is the superstitious aspect of heathen religion; it breathes, not the true fear of the living God, but deep-rooted anxiety in face of the strange mystery of the world, and such religion spells also the attempt to master this anxiety through a human effort to secure control of the mystery and to integrate it with the norm of human life. Thus man tries to calm his anxious mind and to attain a secure mastery of his existence.

### II

In all this, have we been speaking of a remote past, which may be of interest to us but which fundamentally no longer concerns us? Or have we been speaking essentially of ourselves also? Have modern men ceased to feel that anxiety? Is the world and human existence no longer a dark mystery for them? Have they ceased to practise the art of trying to control the mystery? Of trying to control it precisely through the means of religion?

Yes, it is true that modern man seems no longer to be aware of the unknown God; he has ceased to build altars to this unknown God. The world is no longer for him a puzzling and threateningly mysterious place, it is light and clear and familiar; he has illuminated it with the light of his thought and moulded it by his achievements. In the dark primaeval forest in which man as it were first finds himself, he begins to adapt things to his basic needs; he introduces light and order into nature whose powers he observes and subdues to his own purposes. The circle widens itself more and more until it at last becomes so extensive that the darkness of the world recedes into remotest distance and man loses sight of the element of mystery. Thus he has no longer any reason to erect an altar to the "unknown God". But has he been able at the same time to efface the fear of life which lay behind the belief in the "unknown God"? Or does it only lurk secretly at the heart of his life, stilled by his busy activities or hidden by the veil of a stupendous illusion, only to cry out more loudly at times? Has then man really become master of his world and his life? Have the world and existence in truth lost their uncanny and mysterious elements in consequence of the powerful organization of human culture and civilization? And do there not still remain, in the inner life of man, mysterious forces which as it were are only waiting for the moment when they can spring forth with daemonic energy?

When Paul says: "What therefore you worship as un-

he is admitting that in the known, this I proclaim to you" cultic worship springing from anxiety an element of truth is contained. Such a cult implies the search for the one true God, because it implies the awareness that man is not his own master. Man will never be freed from anxiety by seeking to illuminate the world, by seeking to control and organize it in his own interests, but only by facing the question which his anxiety suggests, by giving due recognition to his religious awareness, so that it may ultimatély lead him to the knowledge of the one God who is Lord of heaven and earth. This question is well illustrated by a poem of the young Nietzsche:

> "Noch einmal, eh ich weiter ziehe  
> und meine Blicke vorwärts sende,  
> heb ich vereinsamt meine Hände  
> zu dir empor, zu dem ich fliehe,  
> dem ich in tiefster Herzenstiefe  
> Altäre feierlich geweiht,  
> dass allezeit  
> mich deine Stimme wieder riefe.  
> "Darauf erglüht tief eingeschrieben  
> das Wort: dem unbekannten Gotte.  
> Sein bin ich, ob ich in der Frevler Rotte  
> auch bis zur Stunde bin geblieben:  
> sein bin ich—und ich fühl die Schlingen,  
> die mich im Kampf darniederziehn  
> und, mag ich fliehn,  
> mich doch zu seinem Dienste zwingen.  
> "Ich will dich kennen, Unbekannter,  
> du tief in meine Seele greifender,  
> mein Leben wie ein Sturm durchschweifender!  
> Du Unfassbarer, mir Verwandter!  
> Ich will dich kennen, selbst dir dienen."1

When man has ceased to build an altar to the unknown God, he is unlikely any longer to acknowledge the revealed God, the Lord of heaven and earth, for such a one wishes to become his own master.

> **[1]** "Once more, ere I travel on,  
> Straining forward eagerly,  
> In lonely wrestling, to Thee I lift my hands,  
> To Thee I flee.  
> Thou, to whom in deepest faith  
> In solemn awe, I have hallowed altars,  
> That Thy voice may ever echo in my soul.  
> "Thereon, deep engraved in letters of flame,  
> • Stands written: to the unknown God.  
> His I am, e'en though in wanton shame  
> I tarried to the present hour:  
> His I am and I feel the snares  
> Which grip and twine and hold me low,  
> And-though fain I would flee-  
> Compel me to His service.  
> "Thou unknown God, Thee will I know,  
> Thou who piercest my heart,  
> And in stormy anger dost sweep my soul.  
> Inapprehensible, yet to me akin!  
> Thee will I know, Thyself will I serve."

The effort of all men is directed towards the banishment of fear, towards making life transparently clear and controllable in the interests of our desires and plans, towards the ever-growing strength of human self-security. We are determined that nothing more shall disturb us or make us afraid. The modern world has witnessed an astonishing development of technics and has harnessed technics in the service of this constant aim. In this mastery of the world by technical means we see the ever-present temptation to regard our life as assured, to regard the world as the sphere of our domination, and to forget the original uncertainty and questionableness of the structure of our existence: just as the rich man whose land brought forth plentifully supposed that the mere possession of the means of subsistence had also made his life as a whole secure. But God said to him: "Fool! This night your soul is required of you!" In fact there is no need to dwell on the point that, in spite of all the perfection of our technology, nature itself is never fully within man's control, and that its forces again and again break forth with destructive violence in natural catastrophes and are constantly evident in tragedy and misfortune. Every newspaper we pick up bears witness to this. Nevertheless, the general sense of security is so great that the most insignificant reader, while being moved, says to himself: that sort of thing cannot happen to me.

But there is one fact in life which is not to be overcome by technical means: namely, death. We do not like to speak of it, as in general we anxiously avoid mentioning what is un-pleasant and repulsive to us: such things might disturb our complacency! For this reason we think and speak as little as possible of this greatest disturbance of all, death. Formerly it was regarded as a matter of course that man should prepare himself for death, when it became imminent, and that in such circumstances he should look to the Church for comfort and consolation. To-day we try to conceal from people as much as possible the approach of their end. Why did people behave differently in former times? Because they knew that in death, man is ineluctably confronted by God who demands from him an account of his life; because they realized that man cannot independently liquidate his existence and draw up his accounts. And although we have surrendered this thought to-day we have not been able to get rid of the implied fear. The fact that man will not speak of death and equip himself to meet it, shows only that he is afraid and is tensely closing his eyes in the face of this ultimate mystery.

Which of us to-day would think of growing angry when in a given case the approach of death was studiously concealed from a sick person? And who would take it upon himself to tell an unsuspecting person that he must soon die? But this only shows what we have at length come to, and how carefully we have concealed from ourselves the fundamental insecurity of our life; how little we take seriously the idea: "in Him we live and move and have our being". For the Lord of heaven and earth is also the Lord of life and death: and the thought "in Him we live and move and have our being" implies also media in vita in morte sumus (in the midst of life we are in death.

Thus the resources of technics do not suffice to make man master of death, nor of God. In fact he does not even thereby become master of himself, but only falls into the danger of losing his own. One of the means by which technics has influenced the human mind so seductively is its power of increasingly overcoming the boundaries of space and time, and thus of making the original limits, which reminded man forcibly of his finitude, shrink more and more, or at least appear to shrink; for the fact is that man never escapes these limits. But the less he feels them, so much the more he forgets all about them and forgets at the same time his finitude; so much the more riotously self-secure become his plans and calculations. In proportion as this happens, the hurry and fret of his life grow and the less time remains to him for consideration and reflection. Modern man who is swept away by the rush of life, which modern technology governs, only attains an acute awareness of time in those periods when his time is not filled by any specific content.

ı6

For it is the curse of a life which is so feverishly hurried that the pauses which do occasionally arise are void of content. At such times a terrible boredom may overcome him; he may suddenly be seized by a questioning as to the meaning of his life and feel mounting within him a disgust which can even lead to suicide.

"In Him we live and move and have our being." Whoever lives with the feeling of emptiness and tedium has ceased to realize this truth. If he is courageous enough to reflect, he will of course become aware that it is precisely in the sense of void which oppresses him that God is speaking to him and asking him questions: questioning him about himself and calling him to account as to what he has made of his life. But can man face such questions? The organization of life has discovered the means of enabling man to deal with these moments of emptiness and tedium: amusement and distraction, pleasure and noise! Man is to forget the shattering truth: "In Him we live and move and have our being!" But what is operative in this frenzied effort to become absorbed in bustle and noise but that original fear of life which man seeks so desperately to still?

Technology, which was intended to subjugate the energies of nature and harness to his service the forces which man once felt to be daemonic and crushing, has itself become a daemonic power compelling man to serve itself and thus bringing him to lose his own soul.

It would of course be a foolish mistake to try to wage war against the technical resources which we all need for our lives. It is rather a question of making it clear to ourselves that technics can be used for both good and evil, and that it confronts us with a powerful temptation. Against this temptation we must fight; if we succumb to it, then technology assumes the character of the daemonic and robs us of our true life in God. The temptation is that the very success of technology may lead us to believe that our life is secure and to lose the sense of its frailty; to regard ourselves as master of our life and to benumb man's original fear of life instead of allowing the latter to drive him on to seek his God.

Moreover we are not speaking of those outside our own circle, outside the Church and the Christian faith. No, we all belong integrally to modern life and are all subject to this temptation.

The declaration that God is Judge calls our attention to the fact that all the phenomena of life are ambiguous and hence sources of temptation, the fact that we may use them for good or lose our souls to them. But we do in fact lose our souls to them if we believe them to be unequivocal, thus ceasing to feel their power to tempt and lead astray. We owe an account as to what we make of them. This however applies above all to ourselves, to the energies and impulses, the gifts and the passions of our inner being. These latter are by no means unequivocal, and not everything that springs from the instincts of blood and nature is good. Primitive man is aware of the frailty and dubiety of his being, for he is aware of its dangers and does not suppose that he is master of himself. The German poet too knows this: "Ah! how little we know ourselves, for a divine force lives and moves within us" (Hölderlin). In a very popular book of our time, the Majorin of Ernst Wiechert, the author shows most convincingly and vividly that men know themselves so little and then are terrified at themselves when unsuspected forces burst forth from the inner heart of their lives.

But modern man thinks he knows himself, or he lives as if he did. He can do this only because he has forgotten the truth: "In Him we live and move and have our being." For this word warns him that he lives his life before the eyes of a judge, to whom he is always responsible, always transparent. "Whither shall I go from Thy Spirit, or whither shall I flee from Thy presence?"

Modern man supposes that he is not ambivalent in the impulses and stirrings of his blood. How seldom now are we terrified at ourselves, how seldom do we shudder to contemplate our own thoughts and deeds! We are rather accustomed to praise and extol our thoughts and deeds when they lead to achievements which are visible to all.

> **[1]** E. T. The Baroness, 1936.

Not so long ago humanity was proud of its culture and progress. The world war was required to make it realize, and in the most cruel way, what dark passions still slumber, ineffaceably present, in the depths of the human heart. Has the terror which this discovery aroused in us so quickly faded from our minds? Again we hear praise of what will-power and energy have accomplished in recent years, and proud words in view of the tasks which are still to be done. This is all very fine. But is modern man no longer threatened by the dark powers of evil which lurk in the secret recesses of his own heart? And has he not cause to think of them precisely in view of the immense tasks which it is incumbent on him now to achieve? And must we not recoil in dismay when in the boastings of the arrogant it seems that the awareness of the mystery of evil has been completely forgotten? And would it not appear that in such words the fear of evil has been forcibly suppressed? Is it not as if, in the pride which re-fuses to hear sin spoken of, this very fear was secretly lurking?

When we are told of outbreaks of evil in distant parts, no doubt we feel distressed and horrified. And we can under-stand how the reader of books which give an account of the cruelties of the communist reign of terror or of that un-forgettable little book by Dwinger, Zug durch Sibirien (Journey through Siberia) feels gripped by pity for the victims of these cruelties of which it is hard to believe that men have been able to perpetrate them on their fellow men. It is also understandable that in face of such horrors people ask: "How could God allow it?—if in fact there is a God at all!" Has every reader allowed these things to strike home to his conscience also? Or do we take it so much for granted that we ourselves could never be guilty of these atrocities, that the thought or question never occurs to us whether every man who participates in evil is not in part responsible for what the massed forces of evil are doing on this earth? When we have heard of these dark deeds, have we only called God to account, have we not realized that we ourselves were being called to account?

The modern man who no longer builds an altar to the unknown God has either forgotten God completely and cast aside the thought of God, or else he has a god who is only too well known. Just as Paul takes for his starting point the inscription on the altar, so also he makes use of the philosophical theism entertained in cultured circles, of the outlook which apprehends God as Him in whom we "live and move and have our being" and which can proudly affirm: "For we are indeed His offspring." This was the teaching of the stoics: the Deity inheres in, flows through, and moulds all things; it breathes in our breath and courses in our blood. In this sense the modern man too gladly accepts the statement: "In Him we live and move and have our being." And this truth moves him like ancient man to devotion and kindles him to worship the divine energies of the world. The theme becomes a solemn and soothing musical accompaniment to his life, and he does not notice that in this way, by his very religion, he is resisting God and relegating Him to a remote distance. For this kind of religion, which proposes to heap upon man all riches and abundance of life and to set him in the intricate pattern of a highly organized world, also wishes to regard the world as a beautiful work of art and man's veneration for the divine as an integral part of this living All. In fact it uses man's veneration for the life-forces which flow through the universe as a means of justifying the stirrings and strivings of the human will, and it speaks about the god within man's own breast.

But it does not wish to recognize the true God who confronts man not as a confirmation of his self-centred being but as a critical force; the living God who stands over against man and questions him in gift and fate, the Creator-God before whom man is as nothing, the Judge to whom man is accountable. God is not the unfateful current of power which streams through the life of nature, but the Lord of history. He is not the mysterious power which underlies and encircles all life, but the Creator and the Judge. And man cannot simply allow himself to be borne onward and supported by the lifeenergies of the All, or the blood and life energies of his race. The truth is that man stands before God alone. God as Judge tears man away from all his natural contexts and sets him in stark loneliness. Man cannot allow himself to be driven on by the natural instincts ingrained in his nature, but must choose his way in responsibility and decision. This is the truth behind the words: We are indeed His offspring." We are not nature but Spirit; man has a conscience which calls him away into solitude.

"In Him we live and move and have our being": for natural man this word, understood in its true sense, is a terrifying word, a word which shatters all peace and security as everyone can know who realizes what it means to live one's life as in the presence of God. But equally terrible is the ingenuity with which man, by the illusions of his piety, conceals from himself the living God who is Lord of heaven and earth. "O eternity, stupendous word!" we have been singing. But how ingenious is modern man to misuse the word "eternal" as a transfiguring description of the achievements of mankind. On the gate of the churchyard of my native town there stands an old inscription: "O eternity, ever abiding!" Well, recently I read something written on this motto as a theme, and interpreting it as a guiding and encouraging motif for our cultural achievements. What does this mean? It means that the eternity of God which limits and judges our human time-that this eternity is claimed by man for the religious enhancement of his doings and that man, forgetful of the judgment implied in the dimension of eternity, wishes to give to his own actions and achievements the honour which rightly belongs to God alone.

Modern man to whom the Deity is only too well known and familiar is further removed from the true God than the man who in his anxiety and awe of reality did homage to the unknown God. For God requires this of man, that he should not exploit the divine for the enhancement of his own human prestige and glory, but that he should submit to the Lord and give Him the honour which is His due. Whosoever proposes to draw near to God must first pass through the dark valley of self-surrender. Only out of the valley of the shadow of death, where the self-will of man dies, shines the light of that plenitude of life which God bestows.

And on what basis shall we dare to penetrate the darkness of this self-surrender? With what justification shall we allow ourselves to sink deep into this darkness trusting that God's hand will enfold and keep us with gracious strength? For this reason alone, but really for this reason, namely, that the unknown God has made Himself known to us in His word. For the reason that we have His word, which bids us believe; His word which Jesus Christ the Crucified and Risen Lord proclaims to us; His word which promises us the resurrection of the dead, His word which yields the riches of its truth only to him who believes, but really to him who believes.

The last verse of our text runs: "Now when they heard of the resurrection of the dead, some mocked; but others said, 'We will hear you again about this.? " To which of these two groups do we wish to belong?

For him who dares to make his self-surrender on the basis of the promise, the word: "In Him we live and move and have our being" is filled with a new meaning. The hymn in which we sang:

> "Thou alone shalt be  
> Our God and Lord,  
> To Thee all praise and honour we accord!"

continues thus:

> "O Breath that fills the whole, that works in all,  
> In whom we live and move,  
> Thou shoreless Sea, in thy strange depth I fall  
> Thy boundless life to prove:  
> Myself in Thee, Thyself in me to bind  
> Until Thyself alone I see, I find."

## St. Matthew 6:25–33

*November 15, 1936*

"Therefore I tell you, do not be anxious about your life, what you shall eat or what you shall drink, nor about your body, what you shall put on. Is not life more than food, and the body more than clothing? Look at the birds of the air: they neither sow nor reap nor gather into barns, and yet your heavenly Father feeds them. Are you not of more value than they? And which of you by being anxious can add one cubit to his span of life [margin, to his stature]? And why are you anxious about clothing? Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow; they neither toil nor spin; yet I tell you, even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these. But if God so clothes the grass of the field, which today is alive and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, will he not much more clothe you, O men of little faith? Therefore do not be anxious, saying, 'What shall we eat?' or 'What shall we drink?' or 'What shall we wear?' For the Gentiles seek all these things; and your heavenly Father knows that you need them all. But seek first his kingdom and his righteousness, and all these things shall be yours as well." THE text exhorts us thus: "Do not be anxious!"

I have chosen this text because the time in which we live is full of care and anxiety; but also because this text is difficult to understand. To some it is puzzling and offensive and to others it is even an occasion for mockery.

### I

Such an exhortation sounds strange and perplexing. For it seems inevitable that human life should be full of care, should be motivated by anxieties of one sort or another, to such an extent in fact that a philosopher of our day has gone so far as to describe care as the fundamental temper of human life. Man is indeed a creature immersed in time; his present is ever confronted by a future and is accordingly always inspired by the question: What will the future bring?

This is not always a question arousing joyful expectation; it is often one of distressing anxiety. Shall we pull through? our nation asks. Will my work be successful? This we ask when heavy duties lie upon us. Will my toil bear fruit? So asks the man who has to maintain himself and his family. Will the loved one about whom I am worrying be preserved for me? So we ask when the life of one we love is in danger. I suppose everyone knows the anxiety which robs our nights of sleep.

And not only so. Does not the future demand of us-even at times when the skies are brighter for us—that we should be anxious for the morrow? that we should be concerned about our food and drink, and as to how we shall be clothed? Unceasing care about ourselves and those we love!

Hence how can Jesus exhort us: "Do not be anxious"? Of course we should like very much to be able to follow that advice. We should like very much to be without a care in the world. As every man is urged by anxiety, so everyone knows the desire for a carefree life. In legend and fairy tale a fanciful imagination has always delighted to depict a wonderland in which no one needs to care any longer about sordid and material things.

For everyone feels how care, which directs our attention from the present to the future, impoverishes and devalues the present moment; how through care the centre of gravity of our life is transferred to something which has not yet sprung into existence, which may not be to-morrow or the day after, and which for ever eludes us until the "last syllable of recorded time". Hence Goethe makes "Care" address Faust thus:

> "Whom I make my own with loathing,  
> Counts the whole wide world as nothing:  
> His eternal gloom surpriseth,  
> With each outer sense excelling,  
> In his breast hath darkness dwelling.  
> He may not by any measures,  
> Make him lord of all his treasures.  
> Good and ill become caprices,  
> Him midst fullness famine seizes;  
> Puts him off unto the morrow,  
> On the future ever waiteth,  
> So that naught he consummateth."1

Of course care does not dominate every personality to the same degree, nor does it influence us equally at all times. There are "happy natures" which do not allow themselves to be assailed by anxiety; and no doubt we all have moments when we are able to forget our worries. Yes, just that: forget! Hence fundamentally they are still there! And is it not more honest not to banish them from our minds, but to look them straight in the face and to refuse to foster illusions about the realities of life? And if we cannot sustain this effort constantly but need hours of forgetfulness and refreshment-do we not need them precisely in order to strengthen ourselves that we may once more shoulder the burden of the cares which are waiting for us? And if we blame a man as being frivolous when he blinds himself to the future and its cares, do we not by implication recognize that care is an integral part of the genuinely human life?

Of human life! For we are not plants and animals which do not need to care like ourselves about food and clothing: which do not, like us, have to face the future clear-eyed, but live and grow according to natural instinct: which do not have to bear like us responsibility for others, anxiety and toil for others. Hence, how can Jesus exhort us: "Look at the birds of the air!" "Consider the lilies of the field!"? Yes, of course, they neither toil nor spin, nor do they need to; but how could we learn from them that we do not need to worry?

### II

More closely considered, however, our text shows that Jesus is by no means overlooking the distinction between man and the plant or animal kingdoms, and that He certainly does not mean that we should live a life like that of plants

1 E. T. Everyman's Library edition (Dent), tr. Latham.

or animals, should close our eyes to the future with its destiny and responsibility. For what does our text say exactly? "Look at the birds of the air; they neither sow nor reap nor gather into barns! ... Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow; they neither toil nor spin." They do not do any of the things which man does to secure his future. And naturally Jesus does not propose to say: "You must do the same, that is, neither sow nor reap nor work." Rather He is assuming that men do all these things. But with this pre-supposition in mind He wishes to say: "You should imitate them by not fretting and worrying!" For if plants and animals which cannot make provision for the future are yet without anxiety about it, how should men worry who are skilled in making provision? Precisely because you men sow and reap, toil and spin, for just that reason you should not be anxious!

But does not our sowing and reaping and all our toil take place because we are made anxious by the question: What shall we eat, what shall we drink, and what shall we put on? Now, if Jesus is assuming that by our work we make provision for our food and clothing, and yet says that we must not be anxious, it is obvious that He is making a distinction between different kinds of anxiety. Care for the needs of the future is not rejected but is taken for granted. And if Jesus says that we who are daily providing for the future ought not to be anxious, then He understands by this anxiety, from which we should be released, an anxiety of a different kind. Of what kind? Well, at bottom, it is not a different type of anxiety that is in question, but His meaning is simply this: we must not allow anxiety for the future, which of course impels us as human beings to make due provision, to become master of our lives. For as soon as that happens, its character changes and from being a natural providential care it becomes an anxious consuming care, and the question, what shall we...? becomes a question instinct with fretful anxiety.

Hence such questioning may be right and proper, but it also may be quite false and wrong. It is right and proper when it concerns our daily work, and our provision for future needs. That is a matter of obligation for us if we do not want to be a burden to others and leave them to do our work, which it is incumbent on us to do because we ourselves have to bear a burden of responsibility for others. But such questioning is false when we go further than this and question with fretful anxiety; when we do not simply allow our work to give an answer to our question; when in spite of everything we can find no rest and torture ourselves with the anxious question: What shall we...?

Hence the first thing that we should learn from plants and animals is this: we can do what they cannot-namely, provide for our future by our work; and hence we should not encumber ourselves with further tormenting worries when even plants and animals live free from care.

### III

But we must think more precisely about the words of Jesus. For if they said nothing more than this: you do not need to worry because you can work, there are many to whom His advice would be inapplicable. For there are many people who cannot work, and cannot make provision for their future; while there are others who indeed can and do work, but who in spite of all their labour have ill success and can only look to the future with distressing anxiety. May we reproach them with their anxiety and invoke the words: "Do not be anxious! Look at the birds of the air! Consider the lilies of the field!"? What are we to say in face of the situation of such people? Well, in the first place, it is quite obvious that Jesus is not addressing His exhortation to such unfortunate ones, who on the whole are the exceptions; He is speaking rather to average humanity of whom it is true that they can work and can thus provide for their future needs, just as He is speaking of the average case in the kingdom of plants and animals where it is true also that occasionally a beast perishes or a plant dies for want of nourishment.

And further, Jesus is taking it for granted that if a man is in need, others should come to his assistance. Had we said to Jesus: "How can you say in general terms, that we ought not to be anxious: do you not see the many people whose lives are inevitably clouded by anxiety?" He might well have replied: "I do indeed see them; but do not you see them too? And do you not realize what is your duty in the circumstances?" And He might have gone on to say: "Look at the birds: consider the lilies...! They have no laws and are not organized into a state: they do not live in a community in which each is responsible for other lives and each is dependent on others. And you who have your laws and your state organization, who live in fellowship and community, where each is responsible towards others and all are mutually interdependent, you cannot even banish needless anxiety from your community life which implies mutual help and support."

Hence if anyone would answer: the words of Jesus are pointless, because there is so much acute need in the context of which they are quite inappropriate, then these words of the Master become for such a one an admonition as to his responsibilities, or even an accusation suggesting that he has not done all that he might have done to ensure that such words can be suitably addressed to all mankind.

### IV

Yet having said so much, we have not said all. For if we who can work and make reasonable provision for the future consider in our minds whether we are really obeying the Master's injunction, and can really live a life free from fretful anxiety, we are beset by doubts. For do we know whether we shall always be able to work? Or whether the fruit of our labours may not be destroyed? Whether the resources which we have already garnered for the future may not be wiped out? Are we secure?

Of course if we understood the words of Jesus to imply that we can secure our future by our own active provision then we should be making a great mistake. For if we consider the matter well, we come to realize that we can never make ourselves secure by work and toil, and such a realization is apt to bring renewed anxiety to many people. There are no doubt others who do not reflect on the constant uncertainties of life or else soon forget them, who work with eager industry, assemble their resources and, looking with pride on what they have amassed, live with the feeling of absolute security. The words of Jesus apply to both groups: to those who are only too painfully aware of their fundamental insecurity, and to those who, always aiming at profit, live comfortably with the sense of long-term security. To both he says: "Abandon your anxieties! They cannot help you at all, neither the anxiety which springs from painful awareness of the truth, nor the anxieties of money-making. Both kinds of anxiety are useless." To both groups His ironic word applies: "Which of you by being anxious can add one cubit to his stature?" Hence something which to many people may perhaps seem desirable but at bottom is a ridiculously trivial matter, our height, cannot be altered in the smallest degree by any amount of anxiety. How much less can our busy carefulness avail to make our lives basically secure! What then is the point of living in fretful anxiety when such an attitude is from the outset certain to effect nothing? Or what is the point of worrying about money-making when alter all it can never bring ultimate security? You, who cannot add to your stature by one cubit, nor make one hair of your head black or white (Matt. 5: 36) —you imagine that you must or can make your future secure!

Both types of men are alluded to in this message; fundamentally both have the same way of thinking, that of the man who thinks he must at all costs make his future secure, and who is either distressed because he cannot do so or imagines that by his busy activities he can do so. In both cases, anxiety has become man's tyrant; in both cases, life is absorbed in care about the material means of life, while in reality life itself slips from their grasp.

"Therefore I tell you: do not be anxious about your life, what you shall eat or what you shall drink, nor about your body, what you shall put on! Is not life more than food and the body more than clothing?" Some, of course, will feel inclined to reply that it is precisely for life and body that they are caring when they toil for their food and clothing. But they do not notice that in fretful anxiety or busy care they are consuming themselves by concern about the material basis of life, that in one way or the other they are becoming mastered by the illusion that if only they are assured of material resources, they have no further need to worry. They are losing their souls and their true life because they are forgetting that there are far more serious concerns than those about material subsistence; concerns in comparison with which the concern for means of subsistence becomes trivial.

Jesus wishes to lead men's minds to realize the centrality of this most serious concern by impressing upon them that their anxieties about material resources are at bottom useless. No doubt man has this advantage over plants and animals that by a little forethought he can provide for his future. But in order that this concern may not assume control of his life, he must become aware of the truth that he can never make his life secure by his anxiety to provide for the material basis of living.

### V

Jesus, however, adopts another method of approach when He says: this fretful anxiety or busy care is not necessary either, since God cares for us: "Look at the birds of the air: they neither sow nor reap nor gather into barns, and yet your heavenly Father feeds them. Are you not of more value than they? ... Consider the lilies of the field, how they grow: they neither toil nor spin: yet I tell you, even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these. But if God so clothes the grass of the field, which today is alive and tomorrow is thrown into the oven, will He not much more clothe you, O men of little faith?" How are we to understand this thought? For many people it contains the most obnoxious element in this phase of the teaching of

Jesus. For is it really true that we can firmly rely on the providence of God to provide us always with food and clothing just as certainly as the bird finds its nourishment and the flower receives its garb of colour? Are we meant to learn from plants and animals that our life is secure through God's unceasing providence? Of course we are to learn such a lesson. But if it is clearly a mistake to suppose that our life is thus secured in the same way as that of the plants and animals, namely, by the constant provision of food and clothing without any care or trouble on our part—then our true life which is assured by God's merciful providence is obviously of a different kind from that of plants and animals; of a different kind, indeed, from a life whose wants are supplied by the provision of food and clothing. And what we are to learn is this: so certainly as God provides in the realm of nature for the real need and the good of His creatures, so certainly He provides for our needs on a deeper level of life.

For there is a difference between the life of nature and that of humanity. No doubt in nature too not every single living creature is made secure. There are birds which die of hunger and flowers which perish through frost. Jesus too knows that some sparrows fall to the ground (Matt. IO: 29) and that not every seed brings forth grain (Mark 4: 3ff.). Nevertheless He can say that the animals find their food and the flowers are clothed in splendour. He views nature as a whole as we also are accustomed to do. When we view the life of nature thus, we see that while there is decay, there is also a constant renewal of life, so that decay and growth form the rhythmic pulsation of the flow of natural life, in which destructive forces also have their appointed place, and promote the vitality of the whole. The life of nature thus appears as a purposefully ordered whole, within the pattern of which each part is provided for, so that no part need grow anxious.

Are we to understand the message of Jesus in the sense that human life is integrated with the structure of the natural life? It would be a great self-delusion to think so.

There was indeed a period of thought when it was considered that the life of man could be viewed as forming a unity with the life of nature, and the intelligent design which nature reveals could be equated with God's providential care for humanity. This type of piety was impressed by the orderly pattern and purpose which nature disclosed and believed that such design suggested a self-revelation of God, the revelation of His watchful care. Of course, such an outlook has by no means died out completely; but in the days when it was predominant it received a terrible shock from that great natural calamity, the earthquake of Lisbon, in which thousands of people perished. We can still see from Goethe's Poetry and Truth how this event disturbed and agitated the minds of men. The belief in a providential design, which had grown up from a consideration of the intelligent patterns of nature, collapsed

No; we cannot in this way arrive at an understanding of the working of God's providential care for humanity. For the life of man cannot be resolved into the processes of the life of nature, because it is simply not to be equated with the life of plants and animals. It is impossible for man to accept the facts of his transience and death without question, and to comfort himself by the consideration that in the course of nature new growth follows upon decay. For him transience and death mean real annihilation; and the fact that his life is so closely entwined with the processes of nature is no appeasement to his spirit but rather a torturing mystery. No doubt nature offers him the means of subsistence; but often too she denies them. No doubt she offers him the raw material of his labour; but also she often destroys his labour. No doubt she presents herself to his contemplation as an image of beauty and orderliness; but at the same time she may cruelly assemble her sinister forces and swallow up his life.

What then is the underlying implication when Jesus points to the birds of the air and the lilies of the field? He does not wish us to equate our life with the life of nature, but rather He insists on the qualitative distinction which differentiates us from nature: "Are you not of more value than they?" If God clearly cares for the life of nature so that what is necessary to sustain it is always there and it may live free from fear, how much more shall we not live without fear in the assurance that God will unfailingly supply what we need? The insecurity of our life should not tempt us to be anxious: for what is decisively necessary is not affected by this insecurity. Hence in the midst of our insecurity we should know ourselves to be secure, whatever may happen to us. We are to realize that even the hairs of our head are all numbered (Matt. 1o: 30). Jesus speaks this word in exhorting the disciples not to allow themselves to be dismayed by the prospect of persecution, imprisonment and death. The fact that the hairs of our head are numbered does not mean that nothing untoward can happen to us, but that it cannot happen without the will of God, just as certainly as no sparrow falls to the ground without the divine will and knowledge. Hence we must not be mastered by the tormenting fear: "What shall we ...?" "The Gentiles seek all these things; and your heavenly Father knows that you need them all." If therefore He sometimes withdraws such things from us, He also knows why.

Often we ourselves do not know why. But although we are puzzled, we must not be anxious but rejoice in the assurance that God cares about what is fundamentally necessary and right for us. And we shall have this trust and be free from the mastery of fear about the material things of life only when we are dominated by a far more important concern, when a very different question presses more closely on our minds. What question is that?

### VI

Jesus tells us that when in conclusion He enjoins us to seek first the kingdom of God and His righteousness. Our lives must be controlled by the concern for God's kingdom. The sovereignty of God and the will of God must be placed first in our lives. We are to learn that in the last resort our concern

CrwB should not be for the material aspect of life; that it is vain to try to make ourselves secure through material resources, and unnecessary because God cares for us, but that there is one supreme thing we should seek and care about with all the energies of our being—namely, that we might be able to stand in the presence of God. If this really becomes our controlling care then anxiety about sustenance and clothing will disappear.

On a purely human level we find it is true that material concerns take second place for anyone who is moved and mastered by higher thoughts and aims. For instance, the man who is gripped by the inspiration of some great task, some life work whose importance for him is so overwhelming and obvious that he is prepared to sacrifice everything for itcomfort and security and in certain circumstances life itself. Or one who has encountered friendship and love, the gift of which makes everything else seem a matter of indifference, and who is now moved by higher cares than material ones, by concern for the beloved-friend for his friend, husband for wife, mother for child—for the sake of which he is prepared for any deprivation or insecurity or self-sacrifice.

If we find this to be true in human relationships, must we not understand that it is still more true of our relationship to God? Shall we love and worship God only if He first guarantees us peace and security of life? Or does not belief in God mean abandoning the pressing care for peace and security of life? Does it not mean to be ready for self-devotion and self-sacrifice? We are only seeking the kingdom of God and His righteousness when we are asking what God requires of us, for what purpose He needs us, and when the anxious question "What shall we ...?" is silent.

And if we are really putting first this central aim, we shall understand when Jesus continues: "All these things shall be yours as well." Then God will provide what is necessary for our outward life too. This, of course, does not mean that material security is guaranteed us. But we can then leave the care of it to God with the consolation of trust. He needs us when and where He wills; and so long as He is using us, He will also maintain our lives. And if He sends us distress and want, He knows why even though we do not know. It is impossible for us to solve the riddles of life. Neither can we attain by a perspicacious solution of all the problems of the universe the proof that the reins of government are in the hands of God, who guides all things well; nor have we in our religious faith the means of solving these problems. The will of God suffers no question. But we can win the strength to bear the burden of these mysteries, if we do not allow ourselves to be mastered by tormenting fears about the material side of life, but rather seek with all our hearts and minds the kingdom of God and His righteousness. In that case we shall not only gather the strength to bear the burden of the mystery but more and more shall we come to realize what gift God wishes to bestow on us through the burden of the mystery: inner freedom and peace. And we shall become ever more aware what gift He has bestowed on us in Jesus Christ, who in His word calls us to this freedom of the spirit.

## Genesis 8:22

*May 9, 1937*

While the earth remains, seedtime and harvest, cold and heat, summer and winter, day and night, shall not cease.

### I

AFter many cold and depressing weeks, the sun of spring has begun to cheer us with its radiance and warmth. The vital forces of nature, long frozen and paralysed, have at last burst forth freely; trees and bushes have adorned themselves with the splendour of their blossom, and the trees are once more green. As the farmer in the surrounding country has been glad to be able to get down to his work in earnest, we town-dwellers too have felt an impulse of grateful joy. Our work, of course, unlike that of the farmer, is not dependent on the weather; nevertheless, we rejoice once more in the coming of the spring. We rejoice not merely because the prosperity of all of us depends on the undisturbed continuance of the processes of nature so that the earth may bring forth its fruit. Our own pleasure in the spring is much more immediate and instinctive, and needs no special justification. And, too, the more our lives are harnessed to work, responsibility and care, the more we feel it as a release to be able to open ourselves freely to the light and beauty of the spring, when there takes place a veritable renaissance in the world of nature which speaks to us with an audible voice.

No doubt there are people who do not hear this voice and do not feel things in this way. But feeling certain that our joy in the spring is right and healthy we know that there is something lacking to these people, that there is something morose and crabbed about them and that this lack infects the whole of their lives. And it is quite a healthy instinct in our modern State that it endeavours to facilitate for as many people as possible, both young and old, excursions which enable them to come into direct contact with nature, to enjoy the beauty of nature and from their enjoyment to gain renewed energy and power for their work.

This, to be sure, is how a wise administration calculates and we can be thankful for this development. But as Christians we must say still more. As Christians we believe that we have not deeply enough understood the meaning of our joy in nature which discloses its beauty to us in the spring if we regard this joy in biological terms, as it were, as something to be cultivated for the sake of the health of our nation. As Christians we must go still further. In what way? What is the real gift of nature which the generous hand of spring lavishes upon us as a shower of blossoms? What is the real meaning of the joy which awakens within us and fills our being? There can be no doubt as to the answer. The real gift of nature is that it speaks to us of God the Creator; and the real meaning of our joy is that here we feel ourselves coming into communion with the very ground of our being and perceiving its sustaining energies. Thus our joy passes into thankfulness and adoration, adoration of the presence of God in nature.

"While the earth remains..." It is the law of God which prevails in the rhythm of nature's life. And when we grow aware of the triumphant power and luxuriant abundance of nature, we are asked whether we realize that the law which governs its processes is the law of God.

This is a dangerous way of speaking! We know very well those misleading voices which in our time declare: it is in nature, in its infallible courses and eternal energies, that God reveals Himself to us. We should turn our attention to this revelation, not to an outmoded book which raises the un-justified claim of being God's self-revelation. We cannot doubt as Christians that we must not follow such teaching, that God has revealed Himself to man in His Word and does so still, that here, in the word of Scripture, He speaks to us and wishes us to hear His message, and that if we do not hear Him here we shall hear Him nowhere. To be sure, God is present in nature. But Luther's dictum remains true: "If God's word and sign is not there and is not recognized, then it avails nothing even though God Himself were there!" No, nature is not the sphere of God's self-revelation. Nevertheless, Scripture declares that God is effectually present in nature. "The heavens are telling the glory of God and the firmament proclaims His handiwork. Day to day pours forth speech, and night to night declares knowledge. There is no speech nor are there words; their voice is not heard" (Ps. I9: 2-4). He, the Omnipotent and Omnipresent, is also present in nature. We to whom He speaks in the word of Scripture and the gospel of Jesus Christ are not intended to come to know Him first from the world of nature. But as those who already know Him through Jesus Christ, we are intended to look for the witness to His presence which is implicit in nature, and to praise and worship Him with grateful hearts for the traces of His being which we see there. Hence we do not speak of God's self-disclosure in nature but we do speak of God's presence in nature. The question is: in what way do we encounter this presence?

It is unquestionable that the glad and grateful appreciation of the beauty and vitality of nature is not peculiar to Christians, but is characteristic of all men who are not inwardly consumed by care and grief. It is unquestionable too that this joy in nature is not a merely aesthetic attitude. Such joy is essentially different from the joy we feel in the contemplation of a beautiful picture or a great work of art. Nature does not invite us to merely recreative or cultural attitudes. The feeling with which we walk through the fields and woods in spring and delight in their deepening green, or with which the town-dweller cultivates his little garden plot and rejoices to see its growth, is a feeling which has a quite characteristic tone. It is a joy in which the whole man has a part, a joy which echoes in many a poem and song. It is unquestionable, and particularly so for Christians, that the sensitive feeling for the splendours and the abundant vitalities of nature is a feeling which conceals the sense of God's presence and working, that the astonished delight in the secret powers of nature really expresses our wonder at the power and glory of God, and that the thankful exultation in nature which rings out in poetry and song is at bottom praise of God the Creator. Hence it is not surprising that when such joy and gratitude are expressed in poetry, the poem also speaks of God. So it is in the austere Pilgrim's Song of Eichendorff: "When God wishes to bestow on you great favour"

••. and concluding thus: "I accept in simple faith the wise ruling of the good God; He who wills to maintain the streams, the larks, the woods and the fields, has also guided my life for the best." So it is too in the spring song of Goethe, Ganymede, which to many of us is so attractive in the musical setting of Schubert. The poem is penetrated with love and yearning directed to the "all-loving Father":

"Beloved spring, how you encircle me with your flame in the bright radiance of morning. O infinite beauty, with the manifold raptures of love the holy feeling of your everlasting warmth and glow pierces my heart." And so it goes on to the conclusion:

"Upwards, upwards soars my heart, while the hovering clouds sink down to meet the yearnings of love. To me they incline! Upwards I rise to their bosom! I return the embrace of love! Upwards I soar to your breast, O all-loving Father!"

Who would wish to deny that in such and countless similar songs the voice of genuine religious feeling is heard, and that the poet is feeling after God to find Him? And as Christians should we wish to deny that poetry of this kind redounds to the glory of the God whom we know as our Father in Jesus Christ our Lord? It would be appropriate to quote in this connexion what God declares through the prophet Malachi to those priests of Jerusalem who consider that their sacrifices are alone well-pleasing to Him:

"For from the rising of the sun to its setting, my name is great among the nations, and in every place incense is offered to my name, and a pure offering; for my name is great among the nations, says the Lord of hosts" (Mal. I: II). And we should remember too the truth of Paul's words (Rom. I: 20) to the effect that the invisible being of God, i.e. His eternal power and deity, is clearly perceptible in the works of God or the creation of the world.

If this were not so, how could we as Christians delight in this poetry which expresses the joy of springtime and the religious feelings it awakens, and how could we delight in the singing of these songs? On the contrary, as Christians we can do this all the more fervently, in spite of the fact that for the Christian man this kind of natural joy has a characteristic limit.

However, before we speak of this we will further consider why we can justifiably describe this feeling for nature as piety; why we feel justified in saying that the God to whom the human heart is here outpoured in yearning and thankfulness, is our God.

Man's characteristic estrangement from God consists in this: that he thinks he has the reins of his life in his own hands, that he thinks he can carve out his way in life through his own initiative and planning, and by his own integrity and achievement can securely lay the foundations of his worth. It is just this arrogant attitude which is dissolved when he opens his heart to the impact of nature.

Man can do many things. He can harness the forces of nature to his own service, although he can only do so in so far as he observes, and adapts himself to, the laws which govern them. But he can wield not the slightest influence over the great law of growth and ripening, birth and death, change and decay. "While the earth remains ..." such is the inflexible law of God which no man can alter. No man can either arrest or hasten the great rhythm of the seasons; no man can control the weather; no man is lord of life and death. Man knows this very well; but in the busy activities of daily life with its plans and its cares he usually forgets it and deals with nature, with its resources and energies, as though he were the ultimate controller of them. But when from time to time the fountain of his heart is unsealed as he observes the beauties of nature, then he once more comes clearly to realize that his life stands under higher laws and springs from a deeper foundation on which it remains dependent. Then there steals into his heart some surmise of the true dimensions of his being. And when he seeks refuge from the noise and bustle of the world in the silent spaces of nature, then the sense of eternity may once more emerge into his consciousness:

"When over the silent plain the moon's sickle swings, the heart that shudders in anguish is released from its pain. Eternity is silence. Change is feverish clamour. Silently sweeps God's will over the distant spaces of earth."1

Man can do many things. He can create great beauty in painting and sculpture, he can raise mighty buildings to embody his soaring fancies. And yet when he opens his being to the majesty of the mountains or the mysterious power of the sea, he may be swept by the sense of the pettiness of the artistic works which he creates in comparison with the over-awing splendour and sublimity of the works of nature. And considering the beauty of the flowers of the field he may grow deeply aware that even Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these (Matt. 6: 29).

Man can do many things. He can raise monuments of his skill which last for decades and even for centuries. And the greatest of his monuments retain an abiding worth for the successive generations. But they do not escape the law of transience and decay. Whole civilizations come to maturity and die, and nothing abides eternally. The work of man's hand passes away; but nature goes on its unvarying course throughout all ages, renewing its life with every spring and scattering lavishly its riches without ever becoming poorer. The sun of Homer smiles on us too, and the angels praise the Lord:

"And all your noble works are grand

As if on that first day, creation's dawn, they stand." When now man becomes deeply aware that he is not master

> **[1]** Wilhelm Raabe.

of his fate, that his life is subject to great laws, that his works are small and the creations of his mind and art pass away, when thus his pride and boasting fade-then there comes to him a sense of his smallness, a sense of humility, but also at the same time a feeling of release and redemption. It spells release for man not only when he feels his anxieties dissipated but also when the pressure of responsibility slackens. He feels emancipated when control is taken out of his own hands and he knows himself to be sustained by a mightier hand, when he no longer needs to give his life its law, but knows himselt to be subject to a greater law, which relieves him of ultimate care and responsibility, when he becomes aware that his lite too is governed by the divine ordinance: "While the earth remains..." And it is just this feeling of release through being controlled and enclasped by an infinite power, which man experiences-even if only momentarily—when he opens his heart to the subduing beauty of nature. For the infinite power which is here perceptible to him in sensuous embodiment—can he call it anything else but God? And further, it is no accident that the human imagination, when it delights to picture to itself the bliss which surrounds the Deity, represents paradise as a gloriously beautiful garden.

Hence does not man speak with justice about God meeting him in the world of nature? Certainly, it is with justice that he speaks thus, and in fact with far greater justice than he himself realizes. And precisely because he does not himself know with what justification he can speak thus, his argument is at bottom unjustified. Why and to what extent is it unjustified?

### III

"While the earth remains ..." Let us not forget the beginning of this text: "While, so long as ...", which implies that the earth will not last for ever and that neither has it eternally existed. In such a word it is affirmed that the world and nature is God's creation, and that over this created world stands the Creator and His sovereign will which is not fettered to the laws of nature. It is certain that all the religious feelings of gratitude which man experiences in response to the beauty of nature are really awakened by the presence of God and are directed towards Him. For nature is His creation, and if with sensitive and true hearts we honour the beauty of creation, we are in fact honouring God the Creator. And yet if our devout thankfulness stops at nature herself and does not go beyond her to the vision of the Creator, then our religious response to nature is no more than idolatry.

What is the real nature of the wrong which inheres in such an attitude? It lies in the fact that by this attitude the majesty of God is violated. God dwells in unapproachable light (I Tim. 6: 16), and if He is present in nature He is also concealed in nature. He does not allow Himself to be directly seen by man—we can only believe in Him; and all the splendours of nature and her teeming energies are not God Himself, they do but point to God who, Himself unseen, wraps Himself in the garb of nature. But the error of man is this, that he wishes to look upon God and to hold un-mediated communion with Him. And thus for him creation becomes the Creator, the time of the world is equated with eternity, and he forgets the significance of the word "While the earth remains"

> , and the limit it implies.

The fruits of this error are soon seen in the life of men, for every wrong is avenged. We do not propose to dwell on the way in which the error becomes most plainly visible. For in fact man, who perhaps at first fostered genuinely religious feelings of delight in nature, soon came to think that the energies of nature whose inexhaustible power and abundance he admires are divine, and when he remembers that his own human being has its natural side-birth and growth, eating and drinking, waking and sleeping—he easily succumbs to the delusion that he too, man, is also a divine being. He feels that a divine energy and a sacred will are expressed in his blood, and in the impulses to which it gives rise. God lives within him, he thinks, inasmuch as he belongs to the community of his people with its natural roots in race and soil, and its solidarity flowing from oneness of blood and one sacred energy throbbing within it. God in the race, God in man! For Christians it is a matter of course that a belief such as this violates the majesty of God, and the Christian recoils in horror from the consequences to which such a blasphemous doctrine leads. But these errors have often been denounced in our day and we do not purpose to enlarge on them. Nor do we need to dwell on the point that nature herself, just because God is present within her, ruthlessly corrects these errors. Whenever man forgets the confines which separate him from nature, when he tries to ascribe to his own creations the same value as that of natural force and growth, then nature reminds him ever afresh that these limits remain, and that the work of man's hand is weak and liable to decay.

Nature reminds him of this by the mere fact of the passing of time, of incessant change, through which nature ever renews herself, whereas man grows old and ceases to be. She reminds him of it again in that she repeatedly hurls herself on man's structures with destructive force in events which we call natural catastrophes, but which in truth are not catastrophes of nature at all but catastrophes for the proud edifices of man which violate the sphere of nature. For nature herself these events are only a powerful demonstration of her inviolable, irrefragable laws; for man and his works they are catastrophes, "for the elements hate the structures which man's hand has reared"

No! nature resists too close an intimacy such as man seeks with her. She resists it again in that she remains for man a mysterious equivocal force. "Mysteriously nature refuses to unveil herself to the light of day." Man's religious adoration of the powers of nature constantly runs the risk of interpreting nature too simply and unambiguously, of idealizing her falsely as though he saw only her springtime aspect. But is this the whole of nature, the aspect which in spring delights us with its sweet charms and its triumphant vitalities? Does nature not also conceal sinister mysteries? Does not a mere glance at the animal world show that cruel voracity and fear also belong to the forces which she needs for the maintenance of her tumultuous life? Does she not also disclose decay and death? We may certainly affirm that all decay and death are swallowed up in the great, heedless circling of her life, that every death in her midst only marks the transition to new life, and that in the total sphere of nature there prevails order, peace and intelligibility of pattern. But do we see deep enough into her to know whether this circling of her life, in which each individual specimen constantly falls as a sacrifice to the whole, has ultimate meaning and purpose?

Again, the worship of the powers of nature constantly runs the risk of being transformed into its opposite: into awe and terror of her unfathomable and sinister secrets. To many a questing mind it has seemed that the gay colours in which her laws bedeck themselves grew pale, and that these laws took on the appearance of an impassive, compulsive force: to them the order of the cosmos was imprisoning. No doubt Jesus can speak of the heavenly Father who causes His sun to shine on the evil and the good, and His rain to fall on the just and the unjust, but the poet who does not know this heavenly Father enthroned above nature sees the matter otherwise and says: "For unfeeling is nature: the sun shines on the evil and the good, and the moon and the stars illuminate the path of the evil-doer as of the upright."

At best there is operative in nature an uncanny, un-fathomable power which can kindle no response in our heart. And Klopstock, who was not only a great German poet, but also a great Christian poet, addresses God present in nature thus: "Without Him who has loved me and sacrificed Himself for me, I could not be Thine! Without Him the thought of Thine all-pervading preserice were terrible, an anguished fear of the all-powerful Unknown."

Further, this simple faith in nature fails to recognize not only the sinister mystery of nature herself, but also the characteristic essence of man. For man too, of course, is a part of nature; yet not merely a part of nature. As the Scripture expresses it, he was created in the image of God, i.e. in his inmost self he is not nature but spirit. Unlike the plants and the animals, he is not merely an exemplar of his species, but each man has his own individual life to live and his own individual death to die in sole responsibility before God.

When man forgets this he violates his own dignity just as much as he violates the dignity of nature in equating his own structures with the powers and manifestations of nature. But if he violates the dignity of his humanity he also violates the honour of God who created man in His own image. And then he falls under the shadow of annihilating judgment. For if he forgets that he has to live his life in responsibility before God, his life becomes a dark mystery to him and his death a terror. He cannot of course truly understand his life in terms of the natural growth and development of plants and animals, for his life is always intertwined with responsibility and decision, temptation and guilt. Neither can he under-stand his death in terms of the withering and decay of vegetable life, as something utterly natural and self-explanatory. Rather to grow old and die is something that is repugnant and incomprehensible to him; it is fraught with terror. And though at all times loud voices have been raised in the endeavour to soothe and delude man's troubled spirit as it recoils in horror from his mysterious destiny, anguish breaks out in him ever afresh. For the man who knows God only in the realm of nature, death implies destruction, a destruction which makes his whole life meaningless.

Finally, a last word. If man goes no further than to be stirred by nature to feelings of devout gratitude, if he is not driven onwards to seek the face of the Creator, then his piety, however genuine, remains merely episodic and fragmentary. It remains as something which from time to time is awakened to life, which gives a religious tone to certain hours, which may indeed become vividly felt in the spring but which fades away again with the withering of the flowers. For the God whose presence man experiences in the brightness of nature, ceases to be felt in the grey light of common day, in the pressures of responsibility, in the storms of fate. To gaze in rapture and awe at the mighty forces of nature may well release him from the tensions of working life with its self-imposed discipline, but it cannot help him to bear the responsibilities and anxieties of this life itself. He can see no reflection of the calm and undeviating processes of nature in the struggles of passion and the tragic climaxes of history. For in this sphere it is not true as it is in the world of nature that all maturity and decay bear the seeds of new life, that death is only a transition to a new upsurge of life, that the face of the earth constantly renews itself and at each new springtime is arrayed with the undimmed brilliance with which it shone on the first morning of creation. It cannot be said of man's history:

"And all your noble works are grand As if on that first day, creation's dawn, they stand." It is very significant that the great German poet who was most genuinely and deeply sensitive to the lavish beauties and mighty energies of nature, who felt most intensely the deity present in nature and whose poetry expresses a mystical pantheism, that Goethe was only able to achieve these exalted feelings by consciously averting his gaze from the terrible abysses in the nature of man and from the madness and folly which everywhere runs like a dark thread through the course of human history; that he wrote Iphigenia as if no stocking-weavers hungered in Apolda; that he did not feel himself equal to the task of writing a true tragedy because he felt that it would shatter the calm of his own life.

Goethe's nature-piety had seriousness and sincerity. He was aware of the abyss whose edge he skirted. But for most men nature-piety means nothing more than a mood of sentimentality, because it is but the enjoyment of an hour which remains without any abiding repercussions on their life.

For in truth the history of man is woven with tragedy, passion and guilt, it is stained with every horror and un-cleanness. And in contrast with it, nature is a sphere of peace, luminous, clear and innocent. If we identify God and nature, then the rest of life necessarily becomes for us void of God, surrendered to a mysterious fate, to godless passions or to the choking dust of the commonplace. Whoever sees God in nature only, is acquainted with a type of piety which is episodic merely. And this means that in reality he does not know God, the Almighty and All-present, at all.
