For to know this God means, of course, to experience His liberating and sustaining power and His controlling guidance not only when we step forth out of the hum of daily life, in infrequent, alas! too infrequent pauses, which stand out as islands of repose in the roaring torrent of everyday affairs; but to have the assurance of His liberating and sustaining power and to feel the security of His guiding hand in life as a whole, with its care and responsibility, its sorrow and its guilt. And if already in our surrender to nature we experience a sense of liberation through which our cares vanish and anxieties become silent, it is that nature gives us an intimation of God who bestows upon us that freedom which enables us in the midst of the cares of life to live without fretful care and in the responsibilities of life to feel the burden fall from our shoulders, since He cares for us and stands forth as the One who is ultimately responsible for us.

CIV

But such faith is the Christian faith which as such is awakened by the message of the grace of God in Jesus Christ. Now this message is a message of forgiveness—a forgiveness which does not simply mean that God forgives us this or that moral failing, as among ourselves we can and should forgive each other our lapses. It declares rather that God forgives and has forgiven the fundamental sin of our whole existence, the one essential sin which is that we have separated ourselves from God and have as it were cut ourselves off from Him in order to live independently in our own strength. In Christ He has created a new humanity; the humanity of those who allow Christ the Good Shepherd to lead them back to God, the Omnipotent and Omnipresent, trom whom, to whom, and through whom, are all things. This means to believe in forgiveness and in the midst of care and responsibility to be free from care and the burden of responsibility, in the presence of fate to be free from the power of fate, and so free from the stresses of fear. When we have this faith, then God is present for us not only in nature but in the world and life as a whole, in history as a whole with its heights and depths, in the humdrum world of every day as well as in the relaxation of festival days. The consequence is that the presence of God in nature takes on for us a new meaning. What is that?

1. He who has this faith understands the presence of God in nature as the invitation of the God who is calling man unto Himself, who is driving man to seek Himself. In this light he understands too that nature-piety of which we have been speaking. He understands it as a flight to God, which is not aware of its own true nature and meaning. For if naturepiety is genuine and true, it is a witness to the sin which is the controlling factor in human history and the life of responsibility and anxiety. As an escape from the humdrum world of everyday life and work, it bears witness to the fact that this life is empty of God, that God is not felt to be expressed within it. But precisely as such a flight, nature-piety attests also that man is driven on every side to seek God and is impelled by the yearning to attain the presence of God, which embraces man with sustaining and liberating power.

2. The Christian believer rejoices in the presence of God in nature; the word which calls him to believe causes him also to be certain of the nearness of God in nature, and to receive with thankfulness and praise all the manifold gifts of nature as the gifts of God. And again, the divine in nature constantly leads him back to the divine presence which speaks out in the Word; for he does not defy nature, he sees that in spite of all her gifts she remains ambiguous for man; it is the hidden God who is present in her.

3. The Christian believer who is assured of God's presence in nature, even though he cannot contemplate the truth of God in nature, praises God for His presence therein—a presence which is both bestowing and questioning. And nature becomes ever afresh for him the occasion to render thanks and praise to the Creator who stands as sovereign Lord over His creation, and has declared: "While the earth

.": remains...

"Wenige nur, ach wenige sind,

Deren Aug' in der Schöpfung Den Schöpfer sieht! wenige deren Ohr Ihn in dem mächtigen Rauschen des Sturmwinds hört, Im Donner, der rollt, oder im lispelnden Bache, Unerschaffener, Dich vernimmt! Weniger Herzen erfüllt mit Ehrfurcht und Schauer Gottes Allegegenwart. "Du, den Worte nicht nennen,

Deine noch ungeschaute Gegenwart Erleucht' und erhebe jeden meiner Gedanken! Leit ihn, Unerschaffner, zu Dir! Deiner Gottheit Gegenwart Entflamm' und beflügle Jede meiner Empfindungen! Leit sie, Unerschaffner, zu Dir! Wer bin ich, o Erster! Und wer bist Du! Stärke, kräftige, gründe mich, Dass ich auf ewig Dein sei!"1

1 Klopstock, Dem Allgegenwärligen (To the Omnipresent) :

> "How few there are, how few  
> Whose eye pierces the veil of creatior  
> To behold the Creator! whose ear hears Him  
> In the mighty roar of wind and tempest,  
> Who in the rolling thunder or the whispering brook  
> Can hear Thy voice, Thou Uncreated!  
> Few there are whose hearts are filled with awe and adoration  
> By him whose presence moves and breathes in all things!  
> "Thou whom words may not name,  
> May Thy yet unseen presence  
> Inspire and inflame my every thought!  
> Guide my mind to Thy uncreated wisdom!  
> May Thy divinity kindle all my feelings  
> That they may soar aloft to feel and find Thee!  
> Primal Creator, who am I  
> And who art Thou, ineffable One?  
> Strengthen and uphold me with Thine enfolding might,  
> That I may be for ever Thine!"

## Philippians 3:7–14

*June 27, 1937*

But whatever gain I had, I counted as loss for the sake of Christ. Indeed I count everything as loss because of the surpassing worth of knowing Christ Jesus my Lord. For his sake I have suffered the loss of all things, and count them as refuse, in order that I may gain Christ and be found in him, not having a righteousness of my own, based on law, but that which is through faith in Christ, the righteousness from God that depends on faith; that I may know him and the power of his resurrection, and may share his sufferings, becoming like him in his death, that if possible I may attain the resurrection from the dead.

Not that I have already obtained this or am already perfect; but I press on to make it my own, because Christ Jesus has made me his own. Brethren, I do not consider that I have made it my own; but one thing I do, forgetting what lies behind and straining forward to what lies ahead, I press on toward the goal for the prize of the upward call of God in Christ Jesus.

### I

At the end of a term we look both backwards and forwards.

... forgetting Yet, are we right to look back? Paul says: " what lies behind and straining forward to what lies ahead. ..." Yet Paul too looks back. He remembers the great things that God has done for him, that he was gripped by the vision of Jesus Christ and that in consequence he counted his former gain as loss.

In what sense then shall we feel it right to look back, and in what sense shall we feel that we ought not to? The "forgetting" of which Paul speaks does not mean that he banishes the past from his mind, letting bygones be bygones; it means that he makes a discriminating judgment with regard to the past. He surrenders everything that the past, as such, contained which might tempt him to be proud, to

5 I rest on his oars and to appease his mind; everything on which he might base his confidence and in which he might find his security, whether the gifts of fate, the achievements wrought by his own abilities, or the attainments of his knowledge. In other words, the forgetting of the past means passing judgment on the old Adam, as it ever emerges out of the matrix of the past, and especially of the old Adam who satisfies himself that he is secure.

But there is one thing in his past which Paul does not forget, namely the experience which shattered his sense of self-security, which tore him apart from all that he called his own, bringing a great unrest into his life and yet freeing him from the fetters of self; one thing he does not forget, the painful thrust by which Christ gripped his life and cast it down, and the overwhelming vision of Christ which came to him in consequence. To this experience he remains faithful; and thus he remains faithful to that crisis in his past which liberated him from the dead weight of the past and set him free to look forward to the boundless horizons of the future.

We are now taking a retrospective survey of that bit of past time which is closed by the term now ending. We hope that it may have brought both to those who teach and those who learn an increase of knowledge and understanding and an enrichment of life through friendship and fellowship, through joys of one kind or another, and through deepening experience and awareness.

What is to be our attitude to all this? Ought we not to remember it with gratitude? Without a doubt! But the more we recall these things with sincere gratitude, the more we shall also become aware that they represent a gift which has been bestowed on us. And as we say the words: "Lord, I am too small for all the mercies and all the faithfulness which Thou hast shown to Thy servant" we shall realize that we are expressing a judgment on ourselves. Then we shall see in all the gifts which the term has showered upon us the grip of

Christ on our lives, of Him who wishes to release us from our self-concern by bestowing these mercies upon us. And we shall understand too that what was bestowed on us was not bestowed to make us linger over it but to make us look forward to the future, that it might bear fruit in the future.

The purpose of the gift was to make us more mature and capable for the carrying out of future tasks. In a twofold way. Firstly everything that has come our way should inspire us with courage and strength to meet what the future holds, should fill us with aspiration to live a nobler and purer life, and should make us ready both for action and sacrifice. But in such a way that we never forget that "in our own strength we can accomplish nothing, soon we stumble and fall". What we now have and are, is His gift, and also what we shall become will be His gift. And our readiness for action and sacrifice will only be maintained and come to good effect if it implies readiness to live by the gift of His grace, to allow His grace, to allow Him to act upon us and within us, as He wills; and this means readiness to abandon ourselves as it were to that void in which there is no support and no security, in which we are held only by the grip of Him who in our recent past has thrust with power and grace into our lives.

### III

But perhaps there are some among us who do not dare to say that they can detect the grip of Christ upon them in the experiences which the term now ending has brought. Perhaps there are some who do not look back with glad and grateful hearts, but with disillusionment and dismay. What has the term brought? Increase of knowledge perhaps, but knowledge without inspiration or power. Perhaps insight, yes, but a painful insight, the discovery of the insecurity of what seemed secure. Doubt about what seemed certain and in consequence doubt about the meaning and worthwhileness of our work. Or did the term bring neglected opportunities and wasted hours? Doubt about ourselves?

How could the words: "forgetting what lies behind and straining forward" be appropriate in such a case? Does not such a man find it easier to turn away from his past? Has not the temptation of self-complacency been spared him? No, he has encountered it in a far more dangerous guise. Precisely such a judgment about his past, a judgment made in the bitterness of disillusionment or despair, in a far more sinister way fetters a man to his past, to himself and to the old Adam. Such a one is tempted to cling tenaciously to his disillusionment, his bitterness, his self-condemnation as though it were a sour but precious fruit. He torments himself perversely as long as he does not realize that precisely in this disillusionment and doubt and despair Christ is thrusting into his life to free him from the bonds of self.

But how shall we show him that it is none other than the grip of Christ under which he suffers, and that this painful grip is aiming to free him from his tormented self? Is this the touch of Christ?" he will ask, and we shall counter by asking him, "How can it not be?" If you are honest with yourself, can you ever free yourself from this word which has pierced your life like a sword, and from this experience which has intruded upon it with disturbing force? Does it not awaken in you an unceasing unrest, as it awakens unrest in the world at large which can never be again as it once was, before the word and message of Christ resounded in it? in the world which again to-day is so obviously striving to resist the thrust of Christ who wishes to shatter its pride and so free it from its torment? Is not your dispeace due precisely to this-that you have encountered the disturbing force of the word of Christ, so that you are driven to confess: "It was easier and would be easier without this intrusive touch"? "Were it not for this intrusive word, then life might be mastered, for then it would be a closed and secure circle"? Do you look back with nostalgic regret to the time before this strange disturbance, as we look back with longing to the time of childhood? Now in this very state of mind you must and may realize that you are being gripped by the saving touch of Christ. In this very situation, allow yourself to be shattered by Him that so you may experience the power of His resurrection. Try in these circumstances to feel the power of His word: "My grace is sufficient for you; for my power is made perfect in weakness."

But is it really the case that some of us look back with gratitude and joy and others with disillusionment and doubt? Is it not rather true that all of us to some extent share in both these moods? Which of us could justifiably complain that the term has not brought him some degree of good, summoning him to give thanks? And who would not have to confess that neither is he at the goal of his hopes and aims, that not all of his desires and hopes have found fulfilment? Who must not confess that he himself has been found wanting at many points, again and again?

We ought all to realize that in whatever the term has brought us, both of joy and bitter sorrow, the pressure of Christ has been upon us to free us from the fetters of ourselves, to bring us the freedom of the spirit in which to meet the challenge of the future. And if we have not yet become conscious of this, then this hour of worship at the end of term should bring it home to us, this hour in which the word of Scripture meets and finds us, compelling us to understand our lives in its light, permitting us to understand our lives in its light.

Free to meet the challenge of the future! Are we sure of the future? Are we sure of ourselves in regard to the future? "Brethren, I do not consider that I have made it my own." The lives of us all are governed by this motif of the "not yet". The goal still lies before us and we are not yet what we shall be. "But one thing I do, forgetting what lies behind and straining forward to what lies ahead, I press on to the prize of the upward call of God in Christ Jesus."

Intertwined with the motif of the "not yet" runs that of the "no longer", and the "no longer"—i.e. our emancipation from the old Adam—enables us bravely to bear the "not yet" and to run the race towards the goal that beckons us. For if we are really bearing the painful grip of Christ which frees us from our bondage, then we know that the "not yet"

motif suggests all our weakness and half-heartedness. But the "no longer" theme is heard when we look upwards to Christ and to His grace which frees us and comes to redeem us in all our joy and bitter sorrow. And then the "not yet" theme merges into a triumphant "but already". "But already!" for divine grace accepts us as those whom we are not in our own strength, but whom we are to be and would wish to be; it accepts us as those who have been justified by faith. It fills our impoverished souls with its riches, it helps our weakness with its power, it stills our fears with its consolations, it forgives our guilt with its love. "But already" —against the triumphant background of the "but already" the "not yet" loses its pain. "Not yet" certainly, but "I press on to make it my own, because Jesus Christ has made me His own."

It is especially plain to us to-day that the future requires from us courage and calm. For however obscure it may be for us, we do know that it will demand sacrifices. Now when Christ has truly laid His grip upon us, we experience not only the power of His resurrection, but also the fellowship of His sufferings. Then in times of stress and crisis it will be seen whether we really endured the marks of His mastery of us, and were willing to allow Him to set us free from the burden of ourselves. Can we step out into the future as those who have been set free, "undismayed and without flinching"? Are we ready to receive His grace which liberates us in that it ever anew breaks our lives, shatters over and over again the old man which constantly clings to us? Shatters us with our pride and our indolence, our tense and gnawing self-concern and, on the other hand, our frivolity?

Looking back on the term that is now ended, may our experiences teach us to endure the pressure of the touch of Christ; may the gift of the term to us be that we who are weak are learning to let His grace make us strong, that we are coming to know the power of His resurrection and the fellowship of His sufferings, that we are pressing on "toward the goal for the prize of the upward call of God in Christ Jesus"

With such determination and such a prayer in our hearts let us approach in brotherly love the table of the Lord, who wishes to assure us of His liberating grace.

Lord, abide with us, for the day is far spent and evening has come. To-night we have gathered in Your presence, because the work of the term which in past weeks has pressed upon us is now coming to an end. Lord, we have indeed tried to carry out our tasks as in Your service. But You alone know whether we have done them well or ill; You know too how often our faithfulness turned into un-faithfulness, how often our good will failed to become effective, and also how often we were lacking in good will. In this evening hour of worship, allow us to place the past weeks in the light and splendour of Your grace, so that the work we have done may, in spite of everything, turn into blessing for us and bear fruit for the future. Our work has bound us together into a fellowship of love, in which you have allowed us again and again to feel the healing touch of Your grace. For many of us it is a fellowship which, from an external point of view, will last but a short time. Grant that for us all it may remain an inner communion of the spirit, and may become ever more and more a linking of heart and mind in devotion to the cause which we serve, and which is the cause of Your kingdom.

Lord God, who once commanded the light to shine forth out of darkness, grant that a bright light may also spring up in our hearts, and that through us the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Jesus Christ may increasingly illuminate the minds of men. O Lord, abide with us, for the day is far spent and the shadows of evening fall around us! Amen.

## St. John 16:5–15

*May 15, 1938*

"But now I am going to him who sent me; yet none of you asks me, 'Where are you going?" But because I have said these things to you, sorrow has filled your hearts. Nevertheless I tell you the truth: it is to your advantage that I go away, for if I do not go away, the Counsellor will not come to you; but if I go, I will send him to you. And when he comes, he will convince the world of sin and of righteousness and of judgment: of sin, because they do not believe in me; of righteousness, because I go to the Father, and you will see me no more; of judgment, because the ruler of this world is judged.

"I have yet many things to say to you, but you cannot bear them now. When the Spirit of truth comes, he will guide you into all the truth; for he will not speak on his own authority, but whatever he hears he will speak, and he will declare to you the things that are to come. He will glorify me, for he will take what is mine and declare it to you. All that the Father has is mine; therefore I said that he will take what is mine and declare it to you."

### I

My text is taken from the farewell discourses of St. John's Gospel. The scene is the last night of Jesus with His disciples; the solemn meal has been partaken of; the way to Gethsemane is now imminent. Thus the context is a scene from the passion narrative. Nevertheless, the Church has not seen fit to order the reading of this passage on any of the passion Sundays, but on this Sunday, the third Sunday before Whitsunday as in fact it has distributed the whole of this chapter of St. John among the Sundays before Whitsunday, for the reason that the words of Jesus here reported are intended to prepare the disciples for the pentecostal gift of the Holy Spirit. Thus our text also speaks of the Comforter, i.e. the Spirit, the Spirit of truth, whom His own are to receive, and who will guide them into all truth.

Of course at this moment of the story, when events are breaking over them, the disciples are not in a position to understand the inner meaning of those events. "I have yet many things to say to you, but you cannot bear them now." They have understood only one thing, namely that their Master is about to depart from them and leave them bereft; and their heart is filled with sadness. Their gaze is focused on what is visibly happening around them with a tangible impact on themselves; and therefore they are bewildered and in despair. The new insight which the Spirit of truth will bestow on them consists in this, that their eyes will be opened to the invisible aspect of events and to what constitutes the real and true happening of the present hour. This is the truth into which the Spirit is to lead them; the reality behind the visible, which alone gives to all that is visible its essential significance. The Spirit opens our eyes to the invisible working of God, which is veiled by the visible events taking place in the world. For the ultimately real and true is not what intrudes itself on our notice, what sets in motion our feeling, thinking and willing, what stuns us with its terror or perhaps may thrill us with its magnificence. No; only behind all that lies the fundamentally true and real, namely that which God is effecting, and which therefore alone has true meaning for our lives.

Once the spirit has opened our eyes to this inner truth and reality, then we shall no longer consider the visible and apparent as the fundamental but shall measure its significance and value in the light of what it means to God, and we shall be able to penetrate to its innocence or falsehood. For its significance might be much greater but also much more trivial than we first supposed; it may be more pleasing but also more terrifying than we surmised in the first instance.

### II

This is the general thought running through our text;

however, this truth is not expressed in its generality but from a quite special angle; it is a question of the history of the disciples of Jesus, of the first Christian community, of the visible and apparent aspect of this history as contrasted with its invisible fundamental truth and reality. The theme is the forsakenness of this little flock in the world, and, in conjunction with that, its superiority to the world. For in truth the evangelist does not propose to depict a farewell scene in a moving and poetical way, but in the situation of the disciples he is symbolizing the situation of the Christian fellowship as a whole. Their fate is ours; the promise made to them is likewise made to us.

The picture given of the disciples being left by Jesus to face, alone, a hostile world is not a picture of some transient episode but of the situation of the Christian community in the world at all times. Nor is it a scene which recurs only occasionally in periods of special crisis and stress, but it expresses the essential character of the universal Christian situation: namely, its abandonment in the great world. Why does this feature belong to the very essence of its life? The context of the passage we have chosen as our text makes this quite plain: believers in Him are the friends of Jesus whom He has chosen out of the world (15: I4ff.) and who belong to Him. This tie with Jesus means also however an indissoluble connexion with His fate. It is a link of a different kind from that which binds disciples to a great teacher or leader of humanity who brings to birth new insights and new ideals and initiates new tasks and programmes. Of course in this latter case too the disciples' association with their leader can involve a sharing of his fate, a participation not only in his ideals and aspirations, but also in his struggles, sacrifices and sufferings. Yet this is only true in a partial sense and usually only at the beginning of the movement when it is necessary to struggle for an acceptance of the new ideas in the world. Discipleship to Jesus, on the other hand, means necessarily and always a real sharing of His fate.

This fate implies facing the mistrust and enmity of the world. For Jesus is not a teacher and leader of humanity towards new ideals and goals. His word spells a radical challenge to the world's very existence and way of life; His word challenges the world with regard to its meaning and justification; indeed, it accuses the world of imagining that it possesses in itself independent meaning and justification; it accuses the world of having gone astray through the dream of its independence and self-sufficiency, and it calls the world back from the sphere of the visible to that of the invisible. Thus Jesus does not move in the world as belonging to it, but He confronts it as the One who wills to save it from the illusion of its self-sufficiency and who for that reason must be its accuser.

The word "world" here does not mean the world of nature, nor the world as the field of possibilities, gifts and tasks for human thinking and creative effort. "World" here is not the world as the creation of God, but "world" in a quite specific sense, i.e. the world in so far as it stands under the control of the "prince of this world". The "prince of this world" is the peculiar domination which this world of possibilities, gifts and tasks can achieve and exercise over the minds of men. Whoever surrenders himself to it experiences this peculiar domination which aspires to master him, which gains a tyrannous hold of him in work, in pleasure and in pain, with the result that his whole outlook, his thoughts and desires are fettered and can no longer gain freedom from this tyranny.

We must not deceive ourselves. It is not here a question of the so-called world which tempts man astray into the so-called paths of evil, into bad immoral actions. No, the world exercises its tyrannous sway precisely as much over moral men as over immoral men, and it must never be forgotten that the struggle of Jesus against the world was precisely a struggle against very earnestly moral men. "All things are lawful for me"

, says Paul (1 Cor. 6: 12), "but I will not be enslaved by anything." And the characteristic dominion and sway exercised by the world is to enslave men in good as well as in evil, in the pedestrian work of every day as in the most exalted strivings, in pleasures as in struggles to attain the highest ends:

"The son of earth who struggles in the flaming sun and fierily lashes the team of horses, who ardently wrestles to reach a distant goal, covered in a cloud of dust—how could he believe in the stars?'1

Such is the mysterious sway of the world that it presumes to claim us wholly, so that we are cheated into thinking it the ultimate reality; this is its uncanny tendency, to delude us into thinking that it can satisfy us completely with its gifts, can absorb us utterly with its duties. The consequence is that the world itself becomes a god, rounded in itself, closed and all-sufficient.

But if anyone sets his face against this claim made by the world and refuses to become absorbed in its gifts or demands, if anyone rejects the world's pretension to offer what is finally decisive and important, then the world pursues him with inexorable hatred. This hatred may take various forms: it can assume the mask of seductive amiability, and it can become open hostility and persecution. For if a man repudiates the world's claim to have the power to fill his life completely and utterly absorb his attention in the duties it imposes, then it attempts to make clear to him that at any rate it has the power to destroy him in infamy and torment.

These truths can be felt by anyone who still has an awareness that all the care, all the passion in the world does not in the last resort exhaust the potentiality of his being, anyone who is still capable of not being stunned by clamour and show, and in whom there still lives a nostalgic longing, as it were, for the stars. This the poets also know. The poem which we have just quoted about the fighter, the son of earth, who in the struggles of noonday forgets the stars, continues thus:

"Yet the team flags, the paths grow dark, the eternal stars begin to glimmer, the majesty of inviolable law pervades the gloom. The cries of battle cease. The day is judged."

Or consider the following, by Hebbel: "The most frightening dream is accompanied by a secret feeling that it all means nothing, howsoever we sweat with

> **[1]** C. F. Meyer.

ear. A loving smile shines gently upon our tears. But I would like to think that it must always be so."

And Goethe, who was particularly sensitive to the rich and many-hued beauties of the world, knows nevertheless that two souls dwell in our breast and so prays:

"Thou visitant from heaven, who dost soothe all pain and sorrow, and on him whose misery passes measure dost heap a twofold blessing: Ah! weary am I of earth's troubles! What meaneth pain and sorrow? Sweet peace, come gently down and steal into my breast!"

But the New Testament speaks otherwise than the poets about man's strangeness and homelessness in the world. It does not sound the note of longing, born of weariness and disillusionment or of surfeit, for there the challenge to the world is sounded as a result of the intervention of God's power. This challenge is not to be understood in the sense that the Christian faith intrudes to set the world and its problems to rights. But on the other hand it would also be a misunderstanding to suppose that the Christian gospel called men out of the world, so that they no longer had any share in either its gifts or its tasks. It is a question of one thing only: to say to the world that it is not the final power which can claim dominion over men.

Just as the hostility of the world was directed to the mission of Jesus who summoned men to surrender themselves to the sovereignty of God (for "no man can serve two masters") and to seek first His kingdom, so it is likewise aroused by the preaching of His Church. Nor does it need to be a formal preaching expressed in audible words. The very existence in the world of the Christian Church is a protest against the world; a protest against the presumption of the world to be able to impose ultimate duties and obligations, to give lasting satisfaction and fulfilment. A protest too against the world's claim to be holy: for one alone is holy, namely, God, the Lord of the world. For such reasons the world feels the Church to be an alien body in its midst, and if the Church refuses to yield to the sway of the world or to accept the gifts and the purposes of the world, then she is fated to experience the power of the world in its aspect of wrath.

This gives us the picture of the community in fate, implied by discipleship to Jesus Christ. And this is our situation as a Christian community, as it was that of the disciples in the hour when Jesus spoke to them His parting words. So long as He dwelt among them, they had not truly grasped the essence of this their situation; even in this solemn hour they still do not realize what it means to belong to Him. They will realize it only after He has left them. "None of you asks me: 'Where are you going?" " Their minds are fixed not on the whither, the goal of His journey, but on the here and now which He is leaving behind. They will be without Him, bereft and forsaken in the world.

"Nevertheless I tell you the truth: it is to your advantage that I go away." Why is it to their advantage? Because they will not become deeply aware of their inner strength and victory over the world so long as they have not experienced their forsakenness in the world and the world's hostility. Their victory? Ah, no! but the victory of Christ. Yet the victory of Christ is to become their victory in virtue of their discipleship to Him, their union with Him. "It is to your advantage that I go away; for if I do not go away, the Counsellor will not come to you; but if I go I will send Him to you."

The Counsellor is none other than the Spirit of Christ, the power which He bestows on those who belong to Him. But they can receive the Spirit only when they have tasted the bitterness of their abandonment. That divine power will make itself felt in the weakness of the desolate; only out of the sadness of their desolation in the world can they learn to know the full measure of the victory over the world which has been vouchsafed them. "When the Spirit of truth comes, He will guide you into all the truth." He will lead them to understand and be assured of their victory over the world by opening their eyes to the invisible reality which is veiled by the visible. The visible situation is their abandonment and distress. What is the invisible reality which stands behind that visible scene? Their triumphant ascendancy over the hostile world. For what invisibly takes place in the unfolding of those terrible visible events of the crucifixion of Jesus and the persecution of the Church, is in truth nothing other than the judgment of the world. "And when He comes He will convince the world of sin, and of righteousness, and of judgment." What is the meaning of this?

The title which in the Johannine farewell discourses is given to the Spirit has the meaning of helper and advocate; in fact what is really meant is an advocate in a judgment scene. And this is basically the meaning of the remarkable verse: the Spirit discloses to the mind the truth that the events which Jesus and His own experience constitute are in fact the great judgment before the judgment seat of God, in which the Spirit is their advocate. What is here in question is the divine judgment which will not first take place at the end of time, but which is even now being enacted; it is the divine judgment on the world.

The Spirit opens the eyes to this truth; for the world it remains hidden and that is the terrible thing, that the world itself does not know that its judgment is continually taking place.

How does this come about? The poet says: "World history implies world judgment." But this is not the meaning here. For the poet wishes to affirm that in the course of world history all wrong is avenged, all right finally triumphs, and that the development of world history is an upward progress towards the light. But the gospel does not suggest this. For the Christian faith (and not it alone) knows, or can know, that this belief is an illusion, and that the struggle between right and wrong, between truth and falsehood in world history is never decisively closed, but lasts as long as earth remains. Savagery that had apparently been mastered breaks out afresh one day; crimes of wanton wickedness, the like of which had long been forgotten, dare to sully human civilization once more. Again and again it happens that right is crushed and innocence is trampled under foot, truth is suppressed and falsehood is enthroned.

But in what sense is it meant that a true judgment takes place, to which the Spirit opens the eyes of the Christian? The Spirit will convict the world of "sin and righteousness and judgment"; i.e. the Spirit will show what these three words in truth mean, and, at the same time, who is the sinner, who the righteous man, and who is judged. The world also is of course familiar with these three words; it speaks about sin and justice and judgment and supposes it knows what they mean.

The world understands by sin, moral failings and guilty deeds. It does not hesitate to condemn sin; nevertheless it does not take sin seriously. It thinks of sin as of something occasional, fortuitous, a deviation which might possibly occur. It does not realize that if anyone sins he is the slave of sin (John 8: 34). And if anyone suggests that the world is sinful, it derides him or is angry. For it is of the very essence of the self-consciousness and need of the world, that it should consider itself to be right and good. It holds the question of sin to be a question which it has to decide and settle from within its own sphere, before its own forum and according to its own law. And it does not realize that the word "sin" presupposes a reference to a judge beyond the world; it is unfamiliar with the concept of God's consuming holiness.

The recognition inspired by the Spirit is this: it will convict the world of sin because "they do not believe on me".

The sin of the world consists in unbelief rather than in any particular wanton misdeed. Scripture does not for example say: "because they have crucified me". The sin of the world is not for example that it failed to recognize in Jesus a noble man and brought Him to the cross. The text says: "because they do not believe on me. It does not say: "because they have not believed" but "because they do not believe". It is not a question of any particular piece of conduct but of the characteristic basic attitude of the world, an attitude which may include good as well as bad deeds. "Because they do not believe on me" means that the world fails to recognize Him who, sent by God, stands over against it, accuses it, rejects its claim to be inherently powerful and self-sufficient, unmasks its limitedness, its questionableness, and its lack of sanctity. To refuse to believe on Jesus does not mean to reject as incredible some curious doctrines about Him, but to refuse to allow His validity as God's challenge to the world, to close one's eyes to the intrinsic finiteness of earthly things, to consider the world the final authority both in what it claims and in what it offers, to shut oneself off from the grace which flows from the holiness of God and is readily available for this world as soon as it confesses that it needs divine grace. This is sin.

Again, the Spirit convinces the world of the true meaning of righteousness. The world too speaks of righteousness. According to its own criteria it makes judgments, pronounces one man to be right, another to be wrong, honours one and defames another. And it makes especial use of the principle that the course of history discloses judgment in that it declares: in the successes and achievements of history, the right is manifested. Right is what triumphs in the world. In fact, the right and success are one and the same thing; it supposes that, in the language of the gospel, righteousness means the same thing as ascendancy and victory. But the criteria of what may be called victory differ. The judgment of the world presupposes that victory must be revealed in what is visible, and failure in the visible sphere spells defeat and destruction. But the Spirit teaches us thus: "Of righteousness because I go to the Father and you will see me no more." This is very remarkable: Jesus' failure in the visible sphere, His death and His departure from the world is His victory! He has overcome the world, and His death is the very confirmation and seal of His work which was to confront the world with God's challenge to it. Had he belonged to the world, then the world, which loves its own (John 15: 19) would gladly have welcomed Him.

In fact, it attempts to destroy the real meaning of His work by posthumously claiming Him for itself, as one of its own religious heroes. His death is, as Scripture here states, His going away to the Father, and (to use a term which often occurs elsewhere in St. John's gospel) His glorification. In setting Him aside by crucifying Him, the world confirms His own word that He does not belong to it, and without its own knowledge and desire it must thus glorify Him. All this of course is only visible to one whose eyes the Spirit has opened, and who in consequence sees righteousness and victory where the world sees defeat and condemnation. Christianity too has often fallen a prey to the temptation to detect its triumphs in the sphere of the visible and to insist on this tangible evidence. Thus people speak of the influence of Christ on the course of world history, of the effects which His spirit has had on human manners and morals, of its uplifting and civilizing influence on nations. The world war showed little of all this, and, since it was a war between nominally Christian peoples, many so-called Christians and many pagans became bewildered about Christianity. Rightly so! For we are disastrously wrong when we wish to see the victory and righteousness of Christ manifested in the visible sphere. The world which does not apprehend His true victory answers of course: "Imagination! Fancy!" It does not know that what it declares to be imagination and fancy can be the strongest, most abiding of all realities, and that the things which it deems great and secure are but dreams which the breath of eternity disperses.

And finally the Spirit will convince the world of "judgment". The world too speaks about judgment, and means thereby the judgment which world history pronounces, and which is embodied within the earthly sphere. But Scripture means something quite different: "of judgment, because the ruler of this world is judged." The world which fondly imagines that it is a closed circle, complete in itself, which supposes that it is responsible only to itself, and which subjects to its unconditioned claim all that belongs to itthis world as a whole is summoned before the judgment of God and is there judged, because it rejects the One who over against it voices the claim of the Eternal.

It is in the first place judged in that its claim, to be limited to itself and fulfilled in itself, works out its inexorable consequences. Inasmuch as it closes its mind to the challenge of the word of Jesus and fails to grasp that its own existence is being questioned, it remains abandoned to itself and lost; because it shuts out the divine light it remains in darkness, however little it realizes this and however much it supposes it has the light. But it is also judged in that its power is broken. The power of the world is not destroyed by the fact that there are courageous men of steadfast character who are ready to sacrifice themselves for Jesus. It is not we who execute the judgment on the world by resisting it.

> "With force of arms we nothing can,  
> Full soon were we down-ridden."

And moreover there are courageous men of steadfast character in the world itself.

Rather the power of the world is broken as a result of the fact that the word of Jesus is proclaimed in it and again and again endows weak men with the power to resist the world. Probably the most subtle seduction of the world lies in the fact that, in so far as it can respect human heroism, it also respects brave Christians. And of course the Christian by his courage must secure respect for the cause he represents. But for the cause itself! It is not a question of his own human qualities being respected. And the Christian knows that even the most ardent readiness for sacrifice does not in itself prove the rightness of his cause. Were it not so, then it would depend on the Christian himself to achieve victory over the prince of this world. But the Scripture says: "Of judgment because the ruler of this world is judged." He is already judged and his power already broken.

The very existence of the Christian Church, with its proclamation of the gospel of Christ in the world, proves that the power of the world is broken. The Church is a symbol of eternity in the midst of the self-sufficient world. The word of the gospel resounds in the world and does not allow it to rest in its illusions. That Word is intolerable to it and disturbs and vexes it constantly. Although its eyes are sealed and it does not see the judgment which is invisibly being executed upon it, it has an uneasy and vexing suspicion that this is so. It desires that this word, which assails its self-sufficiency and claimfulness, should cease to be heard and it attempts to suppress it.

By the word we do not mean only the word of the preacher which is heard in church; the witness to Christ has taken many various forms and whatever the form, it vexes the spirit of the world. It may take the form of works of Christian charity; such activity is a word which speaks and testifies to Jesus. But as such it is provoking to the world. Certainly the world admits the necessity of such works, but only in the guise of secular philanthropy. Or the word of Christ may have taken shape in art, poetry and music, in painting and sculpture. The world gladly admits the beauty of the artistic expression and claims that as its own. But the Word thus embodied is hateful to it. Nevertheless this word, which so vexes and disturbs the world, gives to the Christian disciple the power to challenge the world; for he knows that by the mighty Word the sway of the prince of this world is shattered.

> "And let the prince of ill  
> Look grim as e'er he will,  
> He harms us not a whit;  
> For why?—his doom is writ;  
> A word shall quickly slay him."1

> **[1]** Luther; tr. Thomas Carlyle.

## Romans 8:18–27

*July 2, 1938*

I consider that the sufferings of this present time are not worth comparing with the glory that is to be revealed to us. For the creation waits with eager longing for the revealing of the sons of God; for the creation was subjected to futility, not of its own will but by the will of him who subjected it in hope; because the creation itself will be set free from its bondage to decay and obtain the glorious liberty of the children of God. We know that the whole creation has been groaning in travail together until now; and not only the creation, but we ourselves, who have the first fruits of the Spirit, groan inwardly as we wait for adoption as sons, the redemption of our bodies. For in this hope we were saved. Now hope that is seen is not hope. For who hopes for what he sees? But if we hope for what we do not see, we wait for it with patience.

Likewise the Spirit helps us in our weakness; for we do not know how to pray as we ought, but the Spirit himself intercedes for us with sighs too deep for words. And he who searches the hearts of men knows what is the mind of the Spirit, because the Spirit intercedes for the saints according to the will of God.

THIs text is one of the most difficult in the New Testament. According to the ancient tradition of the Church it is the epistle chosen for to-day, and we do not propose to avoid it as a theme for meditation. Even though we may not under-stand it in every respect, we shall try to grasp its basic thought, which can be summarily formulated as follows: We are not what here and now we appear to be, but what we hope to become. We are not what we make of ourselves but what God designs to do with us. What we shall become gives to our present existence its true character, stamps it as something provisional and fills it with characteristic unrest.

The remarkable thing is that Paul illustrates his thesis that our present existence is provisional and that our true and fulfilled existence is yet to come, by a consideration of the world of creatures outside the human sphere: "For the creation waits with eager longing for the revealing of the sons of God; for the creation was subjected to futility not of its own will but by the will of him who subjected it in hope; because the creation itself will be set free from its bondage to decay, and obtain the glorious liberty of the children of God."

Even the creatures of the world of nature are not as they should be. They are filled with a longing for release from their bondage; against their own will, against what they truly should and want to be, they were subjected to futility, i.e. to transience. They cannot freely develop their forces of life; their freedom of growth is checked and they are maimed.

What is the meaning of this? Can we understand it? Have we not here a mythological mode of speech? Still more mythological is Paul's suggestion that nature was subjected to decay against its own will, and for the sake of Him who subjected it. In truth, Paul is here alluding to old mythological stories, elaborations of the old theme of Adam's fall: just as death was laid as a doom on man in consequence of his falling a victim to the seduction of Satan, so nature became doomed to the curse of decay and was thus involved in the tragic fate of man.

All this sounds very strange. For do we see the curse of decay at work in nature and do we hear its sighing for redemption? Does not nature seem to us on the contrary to be the sphere of inexhaustible vitality? In fact to such an extent is this the case that if we wish to picture plainly to ourselves what life is, we think straight away of the vital and creative forces ever operative in nature's life. To be sure, we see in nature also the process of coming to be and dying, of blooming and withering, birth and death. But in nature,

72 dying is something quite different from the implications of death in man; it is not destruction, extinction, but the transition to new forms of life. All growth and decay in nature is swallowed up in a vast, unceasing stream of life. In what sense then can it be said that nature stands under the curse of transience?

And yet this thought contains a deep truth. Certainly, nature, regarded in itself, does not seem to be burdened with the curse of transience; but it does not exist for its own sake, for the drama of man's history is enacted in its midst, and whether it wills or no, it is drawn into the complex of that drama, becomes involved in the fate of man, is violated and despoiled by man. Do we not see this to-day, sometimes with a feeling of horror?

It is difficult to say where this process of violation begins. Perhaps already in the work of the tiller of the soil? In the cultivation of garden and forest? This would not seem to be the case. It is doubtful if Paul is thinking of this, for in such dealings with nature man remains as it were in conformity with the laws of nature's life; he tends its growth and reaps its crops for the maintenance of his life and obviously is not yet acting contrary to nature. For in so far as he needs nourishment and seeks his nourishment in the realm of nature, he is himself a being integral to nature, no other than the animal, and with seed time and harvest his life is merged in the great process of nature's vitalities.

But where does the violation of nature begin? We do not know at what point specifically Paul himself saw man's forcible subjugation of nature. He might have been thinking of all kinds of phenomena of his time; but he gives us no precise details. But there is no need to ponder the matter. The fact of man's violation of nature is plain, and it is plainest where human life has become most removed from the sphere of nature, in big cities and wherever technics have gained an all-controlling ascendancy. The very longing of the city dweller to take refuge in nature from time to time is an indication that in his normal environment nature has been driven out, or trampled on and killed.

A further indication of this is the present popularity of the stories of Adalbert Stifter with their descriptions of nature and of a type of life that fulfils its destiny in the midst of nature. And is not nature becoming increasingly absorbed in city culture and the technological organization of human life? Is it not being increasingly despoiled of its original peace and tranquillity and the mysterious sway of its inherent vitalities disturbed? It was still possible for Schiller to write: "There is perfection in the world of nature wherever man with his torment does not intrude." But where is it that man does not nowadays intrude? What does he not disturb with his tormenting unrest? What sphere is still left within the framework of our civilization, one might almost say on the whole earth, where in truth the peace and stillness and purity of nature reign undisturbed? The noise of modern traffic and the wail of modern jazz destroy the peace of even the remotest spots. Modern motor roads and the building of huge industrial plants utterly efface the natural beauties of our landscape.

It may perhaps be retorted that this is a romantic, aesthetic point of view. Is not this development inevitable? Is it not a question of harsh necessities which are rooted in the evolution of modern life? No doubt we are faced here to a large extent by sheer necessity. But should that blind us to the fact that nature which is thus integrated with the historical development and fate of man is being disfigured and defaced in the process? And does it all rest on sheer necessity? Where does a reasonable requirement begin and where does it end? Is not the ancient myth justified which suggests that the curse resting on nature has its origin in the sin of man? How much of this destructive process is the consequence not of the real exigencies of life, but of the demand for elaborate forms of pleasure, and the divorce of human life from nature? How much flows from the sheer desperate striving after profits—a mentality which has lost all respect for nature and ruthlessly exploits it, which regards every forest from the point of view of the productive timber it contains, every stream as a source of power to be canalized in the service of technics? How much is rooted in the fact that human life is a struggle of all against all? If the armaments which a nation needs for its own security demand an ever fresh violation of nature, does not that prove clearly that this alleged necessity has its origin essentially in sin?

But of course what is necessary and what is sinful are so closely interwoven in human history that the skein can no longer be untangled. We are all of us immersed in a complex structure of life through which the power of evil winds in multifarious ways, whether we like it or not, and the individual cannot free himself from the muddle. He can of course make clear to himself the trend of things, and he ought not to look on with indifference at the destructive process which is violating nature; he must attempt to restore a due respect for nature, and he may be able to do his part in helping to ensure that the process of ruthless exploitation is limited to what is strictly essential. But our text does not draw our attention to the picture of nature in order to inculcate in us the right attitude towards it, but simply in order to open our eyes to the real situation with which we are confronted; nature involved against its will in the history of man and therefore subjected to the curse of ravage and destruction. We notice what she might and ought to be and what she is ever less and less. As we grow aware of these things we feel a sense of horror creeping over us, and we gain some surmise of the tragic doom which weighs upon our world, of the extent to which our life has become entangled in evil, and there arises in us a longing and a sighing for release and redemption.

### II

This survey of the world of nature, disturbed and defaced by human history, should bring us to consider the drama of human life. Is the life of a man as it should be? Is it not also distorted and corrupted? To what has the course of history led us? Do we not to-day perceive the world tremble and groan in all its joints? Our text of course does not speak of man in general, but of believers, who await with longing the end of this corrupt world and look forward to the prospect of a new world. They also and perhaps they above all are affected by the "sufferings of this present time"

', but-says Paul-they are not so affected in their innermost being because their true life is hidden and does not belong to this world but to the future. And so they know that the "sufferings of this present time are not worth comparing with the glory that is to be revealed to us"

Naturally this is not an easy panacea for our ills, as though everyone who is having a bad time here could wait to be richly compensated in the heavenly glory which shall be. Only he can be comforted and only he can confess the faith who has clearly understood that his present life is apparent and unreal if it is not even now determined and sustained by that future for which he hopes, and that his present life is imbued with power and truth only if he wills to be what he is intended to be, only if he really lives by the inspiration of the future. What does this mean? It may in some sense be said that all men live by the inspiration of the future, that they do not wish to be what they are in fact here and now, but rather what they hope to become. This is true of all those who are striving in human affairs after any kind of goal, whether great or small; whether it be the security of a profession, or the quiet of family life, whether it be recognition and honour, whether it be power in economic or political life, whether it be knowledge or the creative achievement of the artist. At bottom all these men live in the strength of what they have not yet, what they feel is yet to come, the future goal of their hopes and strivings; it is this which gives their life its true content and inspiring energy. None of them can say to the passing moment: "Do tarry, thou art so beautiful." And if perhaps in some moment of good fortune and bliss we feel inclined to say this, yet it is only because this moment is an earnest of the future. Now this means that all life which is aiming to reach a goal is always only a provisional, unfulfilled and relative existence. What man wishes essentially to be lies ever ahead of him.

His life is to a certain extent like the running of a race, in which he wishes to attain his own true being.

But this is also true of him who is not strivingor not essentially striving for any such particular goal, but who sees the meaning of life in the education of himself, in the formation of his own inner culture, in the perfecting of himself and his capacities; who wishes to make something of himself, who works at himself in order to be able to stand the test of his own self-examination. "Before each of us there hovers an ideal of what we must become, and so long as we have not reached it, our hearts are restless."

Such a one too lives in the strength of the future, of what he is not yet. He himself knows that he has not yet reached his goal. And the more frankly he admits this to himself, the more he becomes aware how often he has gone and goes astray, how often he has lost himself instead of finding himself, how often he has been guilty of falling short of his ideals. And just as little as he would care to stand exposed to the eyes of all in his present state, so much the more, in all his restlessness, is he comforted by the thought that his true being is not what he here and now is, but rather what he hopes one day to become. Hence no doubt it is generally true that all men live by the inspiration of the future, whether they are clearly aware of this or not. But it makes a decisive difference whether the future which animates and fashions the life of a man is a real and genuine future, i.e. whether it is only dreamed of, longed for, striven for, or whether it is in fact coming to me. For after all, the future is what is coming to us whether we hope for it, expect, and are prepared for it, or not.

The extent to which men are living in view of the real future is shown simply by this, namely, how far they are able to deal with what the future brings to them, or whether on the contrary they collapse and are crushed under it, be it joy or pain or the dreary round of humdrum duties. Hence to live in such a way as to be ready to meet the future means to be available for whatever the future brings, in the assurance and conviction that all must work together for good, and that every future is the gift and blessing of God. Hence the Christian does not allow his life to be determined by a self-chosen aim, to which all his energies and hopes are bent; but his life is rather characterized, in a certain sense, by lack of specific aim, by which we mean an inner freedom from self-chosen aims. The faith of the Christian is that the future will bring him his true self, which he can never capture by his own self-appointed courses. In other words, readiness for my fate, for that which God designs to do with me.

Can we affirm that such readiness is ours? Are we prepared to meet any destiny? Does our readiness to meet what the future may bring give us freedom from our own obsessive purposes? Are we, who aim at being Christians, characterized by this note of inner freedom as Paul describes it:

"Let those who have wives live as though they had none, and those who mourn as though they were not mourning, and those who rejoice as though they were not rejoicing, and those who buy as though they had no goods, and those who deal with the world as though they had no dealings with it."

Most men live, unconsciously to themselves, in a state of characteristic inner discord. Their hopes for the future without which they cannot live are combined with a fear of the future which can rob them of what they already possess. In so far as they strive to hold fast to what they already possess, they are clinging desperately to the past and not living towards the future but dying to it. What they hope for from the future is always only something which, once they have it, they will be eager to grasp and retain, so as to possess it, and secure themselves against the future. It is from such strivings of humanity, from such eagerness to hold fast and possess, that there arises the curse which weighs on the whole of human history; the strife of all against all, because each wishes to assert himself and his rights and to secure his future against others. And instead of living accessibly to the future, men really live on the basis of the past, which prescribes to them their aims, dictates their motives and the criteria of their conduct. So, like nature, human history offers us the picture of a humanity which is not as it should be and might be, but instead is disfigured and defaced.

Not readiness for the future, but fear of it, is the decisive motivating force in human life. Really to live availably for the future would mean to overcome the anguished fear of it in the certainty that whatever it brings can only serve our ultimate good. And the final proof of the Christian attitude would be not to shudder and recoil at the thought of the death which the future will assuredly bring to every one of us, but rather to go forward to meet it undismayed in the assured conviction that "neither death nor life, nor angels nor principalities, nor things present nor things to come, nor powers... Will be able to separate us from the love of God".

But who can speak with this degree of assurance? May perhaps he whose aims are not tangible worldly goods, but who strives only to attain the purity and perfection of his own true being? Does not such a one live with a view to a genuine future? Does he not look to a future which can rob him of nothing since he has not set his heart on transitory and tangible things? No doubt he lives by the inspiration of what he wishes to become, yet not in view of the real future which is coming to him. He is living in the strength of his own resources, not by the blessing which the future will bring to him and not in readiness for the purposes which God has for him. But the more he lives by his own strength, the more also he lives enslaved to the past, to what he has made of himself. And the more frankly he confesses that he is not yet what he wishes to become, the more he becomes conscious of the discord in which his life is rooted; he would like to be the man whom he sees before him as a serene and shining ideal, and yet he is ever entrapped by his own past with its half-heartedness and pettiness, its impurity and evil from which he can never free himself. And then his hope cleaves no longer to what he wishes to make of himself, but to the possibility of release and redemption from the fetters of self, and the basic disharmony of his being. He is not what he should be and wishes to be; only the future can impart to him his true life, and then only as a gift if he stands ready and available for the future, for what God designs with him.

But who is there who can be thus ready for the future which God gives? Who is so free from anxiety that he ceases to cling to the transitory and perishable? Who is free from his own past, from himself?

There is only one power which can free us from the bondage of self, and take away from us both our fear and our self-despair. It is the power of love.

Through our human intercourse and dealings with each other, whenever our common life is struck by the beams of divine love, we know what the spirit and sway of love can mean. And some of us are aware with shame and gratitude that we are not what we have made of ourselves but what the love that we have encountered, and that has been bestowed on us, has enabled us to become. This mysterious power and grace of genuine love rests upon this fact, namely that it sees and understands and deals with a human being whom it encounters, not as what he in effect at present is, but as what he can and should and would like to be, as the being whom he will be. It cheers and enheartens us when we perceive that others are treating us as those whom we shall be; it is an inspiration to us to see that the other can penetrate beyond all that is outward, beyond the grime of daily life, the petty and trivial, the imperfect, the half-hearted that adheres to each one of us; that the person with whom we are dealing trusts us. And to trust a man means of course to believe in his future. Such trust inspires the one who experiences it with new happiness and sense of freedom.

Thus love bestows a real creative future and at the same time belief in its unbounded horizons. But all our love is only a faint reflection of the love of God, which "moves sun and stars". How feeble and wavering is our power to give and awaken love! Yet the essential message of Christianity is that the love of God has been manifested in Jesus Christ. There it emerges from its hiddenness and in word and deed is proclaimed to all who in the torment of their distress and sorrow long for freedom. The love of God which takes us not as those who we now are and into whom we have made ourselves, but as those who we are not, who we ought and wish to be, who we shall be. And by this means the divine love makes that which we are, apparent only, and bestows in its stead our genuine and true life.

This love of God is not a goal towards which we strive— who could ever obtain it by striving?-but it is the power which already enclasps and enfolds us, and to which our eyes have only to be opened; and we are to turn our gaze and our meditation on Him in whom it has appeared incarnate, Jesus Christ. To know oneself to be sustained by this love means to be free from the bondage of the past, from the fetters of oneself, free for the future which God will bestow and for the glory which shall be revealed to us.

This freedom creates in us the hope of which Paul says: "For in this hope we were saved. Now hope that is seen is not hope. For who hopes for what he sees?" Hope focuses itself on the invisible, on that of which we can have no conception or imagination. But this hope must be entertained in patience: "But if we hope for what we do not see, we wait for it with patience." This no doubt requires always new power and self-conquest. But if we are aware of the love of God we belong to those who have the "first fruits of the Spirit", i.e. those in whose present life the power of the future is effectually shown. And the Spirit "helps us in our weakness". When we feel our own powers enteebled, then the power of God intervenes to help us, and our confession that in our own strength we can do nothing, becomes a prayer.

For what are we to pray? "We do not know how to pray as we ought"—we have of course all our multifarious wishes for the future, but in the presence of God they must be silent. And when all our conscious aims for the future are transcended and our way in life from a human point of view seems to be without a goal, when only our longing and aspiration remain, then in this very longing, unknown to ourselves, there speaks the voice of the Spirit. "The Spirit

8ı himself intercedes for us with sighs too deep for words. And he who searches the hearts of men knows what is the mind of the Spirit, because the Spirit intercedes for the saints according to the will of God."

We are not what we in our present state appear to be but what we hope to be, and this we truly are if we hope for what God in His love designs with us.

> "Darum zur Ruh, mein wild Gemüt!  
> Nicht alles wird hier Frucht, was blüht;  
> Du trägst, der Erde stummer Gast,  
> in dir, was nur der Himmel fasst.  
> Was für und für so ruhelos  
> dich dunkel treibt auf deinen Wegen,  
> Es ist das erste Flügelregen  
> des Falters in der Puppe Schoss;  
> Dir selbst bewusst kaum ist dein Leid  
> ein Heimweh nach der Ewigkeit."1

> **[1]** Geibel:  
> "Hence calm yourself, my wild tormented heart!  
> Not all that blossoms here bears fruit;  
> Dumb guest of earth, you carry within you  
> What only heaven can grasp.  
> What drives vou on and on so restlessly  
> In your dark ways  
> Is the first stirring of butterfly wings  
> In the breast of the chrysalis;  
> All unknown to you, your pain  
> Is your yearning for eternity, your heart's home."

## St. Matthew 11:28–30

*July 24, 1938*

"Come to me, all who labour and are heavy laden, and I will give you rest. Take my yoke upon you, and learn from me; for I am gentle and lowly in heart, and you will find rest for your souls. For my yoke is easy, and my burden is light." THe words of our Lord to which we have just been listening are among the most familiar in the whole of the New Testament. They bring vividly before us the figure of the Lord Jesus Christ who extends His arms invitingly and appeals to men: "Come to me!" The call of Jesus sounds in our life in many various ways. Let us think to-day of the way in which that appeal comes to us through the festival that we are celebrating, namely Sunday. To-day is Sunday, a day which the ancient Church described as the Lord's Day. On this, the Lord's Day, the bells make the voice of Jesus resound throughout the Christian world with the appeal: "Come to me!" What has Sunday to say to us, this day on which the appeal of Christ strikes home to us with peculiar power?

### I

The Christian Sunday is foreshadowed in the Jewish Sabbath. The latter was celebrated at the end of the week, Sunday at the beginning. This characteristic fact in itself suggests that Sunday should spell for us a new beginning, a new beginning after the week of toil and the cares of everyday life. What a blessing it is for man that again and again he is allowed to make such a new beginning! How many a time can a new day be a real gift to us! What a quickening refreshment it can be when the night is sub-merged in the west and the new day rises gently in the east, when the first beams of the morning sunshine greet the town and its dwellings, gardens and landscape! When we really take to our hearts the beauty of the morning sunlight, we can begin our day's work with renewed joy and vigour.

How many a morning of course steals upon us, grey with cloud and mist! And it is not always easy for us to assimilate the blessing of the new day. But how desirable it would be if we could do so always with a moment of silent meditation, a prayer or a hymn, whether alone or in fellowship with the other members of our family. It would be so salutary for our mood during the day's work, for the way in which we approach the people with whom we have dealings, if only each day began with quiet self-recollection in the presence of God, with thanksgiving and petition. How often do we begin the day quite thoughtlessly, and hardly have we awakened from sleep when we are again caught up in the cares and the business of life. We can see from the behaviour of people how they have begun their day; many a popular expression brings home the same truth. But even when we have had a time of quiet and prayer in the early morning, how often it is quickly forgotten in the bustle of the new day! How grateful we should be for Sunday which brings an interruption to the work of the week, which stands out as a day of rest from the agitation and tumult of our week's work, which invites us to pause and reflect in God's presence, when the bells ring out with their appeal: "Come to me, all who labour and are heavy laden."

Are we not all weary and heavy laden? Laden with the burdens of the week, and the grime of everyday toil? What a load of trouble and care each week brings, what petty and exhausting tasks, oftentimes frustrating labour! And this is the case even when we are engaged in work which delights us and expresses our sense of personal vocation. Even the most fruitful labour is in the long run undermining and fatiguing, and we notice this at the end of the week. And we notice it still more if at any time we have spent a Sunday without due rest, whether from frivolous motives, or because on Sunday there was so much to get done which in the week we had to set aside.

Work however not only makes the body tired, and is physically fatiguing, but it can also consume our inner strength. Those who are engaged in work which with its plans and prospects claims their whole attention, are always in danger of forgetting their souls through their absorption in such work. For such people the time for recollection, and self-examination in the presence of God, for realizing the primordial necessity of God's blessing on their work, becomes ever shorter and in the end threatens to vanish altogether. We say of course that "so and so is utterly taken up with his work" and more often than not we do not realize what a condemnation this implies. A man of this kind must lose the whole basis and poise of his life if he has the misfortune to meet with failure, for it was only his work which gave his life its support; and if such a one becomes incapable of work, then he loses the content of his life completely, for it was only his work which gave his life its content and meaning. Thus it appears that work can be not only a blessing but also a curse, and can, as it were, eat up and dissipate a man's life. We detect in the people of our acquaintance whether they are merely workers (rather like working animals) as opposed to human beings, for such people are incapable of taking part in a genuine community life, and sharing the joys and sorrows of others.

### II

Now it is just these things of which Sunday reminds us. The words of Jesus are a summons to all those who are weary and heavy laden. Sunday invites these tired and burdened souls to become refreshed and renewed. But Sunday can pour its healing balm only on those who realize their need. Woe to them it,

, in their preoccupation with toil, they forget their soul's need! In fact, Sunday is not intended to be simply a superficial day of rest for those who feel weary and run down by the labours of the week, but according to its true meaning and purpose it is a day of rest and refreshment for the soul, when we pause to consider that there is something other and bigger than earthly thoughts and the cares of everyday life. It is not work, however precious that may be, which can give our life exhaustive significance. It is not our everyday toil which endows the life of man with ultimate meaning and richest content. Life receives its true content and meaning from what is taking place in our inner man, our souls.

Sunday should lead us to meditate on this, among other things: what is the ultimate meaning of all our busy care and toil? Of this bustling activity in which we all seem to be endlessly engaged? It comes to an end of course with death, and what final significance has our work in view of this end to which we are moving? Have we become more mature, more prepared for eternity? Have we won an inner peace of soul? Can we thank God as we look back on this past week? Have we become more cheerful and kind? Or are we only tired and vexed? Or do we find it uncomfortable and repugnant to examine ourselves at all? Are we seeking in the midst of a feverish preoccupation with our work to flee from ourselves because we know how poor and empty we are? Yes, Sunday is intended to remind us of the fact that we are truly weary and heavy laden however much we are inclined to forget it. Its purpose is to draw our attention to those hidden things of the spirit which we most sorely need. "The kingdom of God is not eating and drinking"

, says Paul (neither is it toil and care), "but righteousness and peace and joy in the Holy Ghost."

"Come to me!" thus the peal of church bells on Sunday invites us. They call us to enter the house of God, and to surrender ourselves to the sway of God's word. If we do that, our Sunday will be rich in blessing, a real Day of the Lord. "Come to me!"—to the word of God which calls us away from our work with its thoughts and plans, its joys and sorrows, its successes and failures, to the silence of meditation; to serious and joyful self-recollection in the presence of God; to the sense of God's glory which shines forth on us in the face of Jesus Christ. Thus Sunday will always be for us a new beginning, so that, after feeling "power from on high" we can resume our weekly tasks with new joy and strength, with a new attitude of composure in face of anxiety and failure, and so also with new cheerfulness and kindness. We are best able to face our life of work and our weekly tasks when, with all our industry and conscientiousness, we have gained an inner detachment from them, when we know that our work is not the ultimate value and does not exhaust the potentialities of our being. Work is not our master. God is our Master!

### III

"Take my yoke upon you and learn from me." If the promise "I will refresh you" is to be valid for us, then of course we must submit ourselves to the law of Christ and take upon us His yoke.
