He says to us: "My yoke is easy and my burden is light." But only he experiences this who really takes upon him the yoke of Christ. For the truth is that those who do not at all feel themselves to be weary and heavy laden, recoil from taking the yoke of Christ and look upon it as harsh and crushing. The real meaning and blessing of Sunday is concealed from such people. They too celebrate Sunday but they misunderstand and abuse it.

We are of course aware that there are many people for whom the work in which they are engaged during the week does not constitute the meaning of their lives. They are not absorbed in their work. Their work remains something external to themselves; it is not for them a fulfilment of life but only a means of livelihood; and no doubt they feel their work to be a burden. For the real essence of life is for them the excitement of pleasure. There is no need to dwell on this misuse of Sunday which makes of Sunday not the Lord's Day, not a time of quiet and worship, but a time of noisy activity; not rest but sheer pleasure-seeking, not a time for self-recollection but a time for escaping from oneselt, not an opportunity for inner concentration of being, but an opportunity for distraction and amusement. Such people not only miss the real spiritual blessing of Sunday but also for the most part the gift which it brings of outward quiet; and Monday morning finds them prostrated and peevish.

But both sets of people, those who are entirely absorbed in their work and for whom Sunday is merely a day of outward relaxation, and the others who are indifferent to their work, and who misuse Sunday in chasing the pleasures of the world, are not always to be so clearly separated. Many men, in fact most, have something of both attitudes in themselves and sometimes behave in one way, sometimes in the other. Not one of us does not stand in danger of misusing Sunday and forgetting that it is the Lord's Day whose yoke we should take upon us and from whom we should learn.

There were once times, and even to-day there are still many places, where the Sabbath rest was most strictly carried out and where any kind of amusement or pleasure on Sunday was considered an abuse. The danger often is indeed that the easy yoke of the Lord may become oppressive; in which case the freedom for which it is the purpose of this day to liberate us, turns into a form of slavery. But for the majority of us, generally speaking, this danger is not great; on the contrary! And we should do our best to maintain sound and ancient Sunday customs. The latter of course seem harsh and oppressive to those who have not grasped the true meaning of Sunday. And the chief point is that we should keep alive the sense of this meaning and purpose among our people, or revive it if need be; that we should try to instil an under-standing of the truth that our life should not be wholly taken up in preoccupation with the things of time and space, whether it be a question of work or pleasure; that our souls belong to a higher world, to God's world. Then even Sunday customs which formerly perhaps may have seemed harsh and enslaving, will become a light burden and we shall be grateful to them because they have disciplined us and enabled us to taste to the full the healing blessings of Sunday.

If anyone should ask whether it is at all permissible for us to enjoy games and innocent pleasures on Sunday, we would feel inclined to ask him: what after all is true earthly enjoyment? "The earth is the Lord's and the fullness thereof!" We may surely enjoy what the Lord's earth gives us to enjoy; whether it be the beauty of nature, or the cheerful gathering of the family circle or the meeting of friends, or the gifts of noble art—all that we may enjoy on the Lord's Day also, if we enjoy it as the Lord's gift for which we thank Him. "For all that the Lord created is good and nothing is bad which s received with a grateful heart." Only the pleasure in the enjoyment of which we could not thank the Lord as Giver, nor indeed think of Him at all, but would rather have to avoid all religious thoughts, is "worldly pleasure" which we ought to shun not only on Sunday but on weekdays also. And even other pleasures which we might thankfully welcome as His gift become "worldly pleasures", if in enjoying them we forget Him and the thanks due to Him. "Whether then you eat or drink, or whatever you do, do all to the glory of God." It is this which we are to learn from the Lord; this is the meaning of His yoke, and this should stand as the guiding and testing rule over all that we do on the Lord's Day.

### IV

And finally, one last word! Sunday should mean for us a new beginning not merely in regard to our week-day work. It is not simply the humdrum work of the week which causes us to feel the blessing of a new beginning Sunday by Sunday.

"A new beginning"-how often is that the wish of our hearts when we look back on our past with all its working days and Sundays! For how often do we realize with a painful sense of oppression that we are caught in and enchained by our past, that we have lost our way in life and can find no means of escape from our difficulties. We have enslaved ourselves to one thing or another-obligations, customs, passions! We note that as a result we have become fettered and dishonoured, that we are perishing, and cannot get free from the toils. At such moments we are aware how weary and heavy laden we in fact are, not only through work and care but also-and still more oppressively-through guilt and impurity. Oh that we might begin all over again!

Oh that we might begin a new life! How different it would be, and how much better. No doubt many a time and in many things we can make a new start by taking a fierce resolution, and we should do so. But does this always succeed? And above all, we cannot do this in the most important matters. For who can silence the voice of conscience, which reminds us of past guilt? Which of us could cleanse ourselves of all the stains which sully our past? Which of us could restore so much that is grievously, irreparably, damaged in our life and community? For how many of us is it true that it is now too late!

Does Sunday spell a new beginning for us in these respects too? We must remember that the Lord's Day is the Day of the resurrection of Christ; the day on which the new world of life in the spirit victoriously broke in upon the old world of sin and death, and so initiated a new beginning for all who honour the Risen Lord as their Saviour and Master. "If any one is in Christ he is a new creation; the old has passed away, behold, the new has come. All this is from God who through Christ reconciled us to Himself."

It is not in our power to make a new beginning, but God has made a new beginning for us. And again and again we can allow this divine initiative to become a reality for us, we can repeatedly be strengthened and refreshed by it. This is the very meaning of God's word which each new Sunday proclaims to us; it warns us that we may and must do this, that our thoughts should not remain centred on our old life but on the grace which God bestows on us in Christ. The bells ring out the message that we are they whose life He has restored, whom He has reconciled to Himself, sinners whom He has forgiven, the impure whom He has made pure. Sunday as the Lord's Day is meant to give us the assurance that we in all our guilt and weakness belong even now to the new world, the world of God and of eternal life. Just as this day is excepted from the course of our working time which binds us to the old world, so through the grace of Christ we are lifted out of this world, freed from this world and from ourselves, in so far as we are still implicated in this old world. Just as Christ died to this world, so it is true of us to say: "You have died and your life is hid with Christ in God."

To believe in this hidden inner life is the very mark of the Christian, and Sunday assures us of the reality of this hidden life. But we are called to bear witness to this hidden life, and precisely by this means, namely, that we keep Sunday as the Lord's Day on which this new beginning, divinely bestowed, is again and again appropriated and honoured by us as a real new beginning in our earthly life. Let us with the poet! invoke Sunday thus:

> "To all who wander in dismay  
> Thou shalt be a home.  
> Receive them in thy house of light  
> From shadows where they roam.  
> "To all week-day wanderers,  
> Burdened though they be,  
> With thy seraph army bring  
> A day of victory.  
> "To all souls that sought for love  
> Six long wretched days  
> Thou shalt come with seven lights  
> And seven harps of praise.  
> "Everyone that longs for home  
> Lead with gentle hand.  
> Show to us the wondrous mountains  
> Of that other land."

> **[1]** Gustav Schüler.

## Another Season of the Year

*July 27, 1938*

> "Abermal ein Teil vom Fahre,  
> abermal ein Tag vollbracht:  
> Abermal ein Brett zur Bahre  
> und ein Schritt zum Grab gemacht.  
> Also nähert sich die Zeit  
> nach und nach der Ewigkeit;  
> also müssen wir auf Erden  
> zum dem Tode reifer werden."  
> J. Chr. Günther  
> "Another season of the year,  
> Another day is spent:  
> Another board to make the bier,  
> Steps to the graveyard bent.  
> As Time draws ever closer  
> To touch Eternity,  
> For death we should be ripening  
> While this world still we see.

### I

IT is very appropriate that the last service of the term should be an evening service, for this makes clearer to us the fact that our term's work on which we are looking back is immersed in the stream of time which is ever flowing out to the seas of eternity. These divisions of time which are established by the exigencies of our working life and its organization do not form an architectonic pattern built into time as an immovable dimension. It would be truer to think of them as swallowed up in the rush of an onward-sweeping time stream. No doubt it pleases us to look back on the work accomplished during the term as a piece of work by which the building of our life as a whole has been advanced, and which will furnish a basis for further building; and with our plans and projects in mind we like to look forward to the time ahead and think about further building. But the past term and the future are not only periods of time which stand at our disposal for the realization of our plans, they are also waves in the onward sweep of the time stream.

If we carefully consider this, we shall also begin to realize that the chief point is not what we have accomplished by our trouble in the past term and how we propose to build further on this basis, but what, while doing our work, has been happening to our selves and what is to emerge from these hidden selves of ours, in the sense which the poet has in mind when he speaks of our growing ripe for death. But how could we become ripe for death and eternity if eternity itself did not already determine our life in time, did not limit and judge our work in time? If in the flux of time we did not in some sense participate in eternity? Whence shall we gain this assurance?

In this hour of evening worship we must allow a word of the eternal God to strike home to us. Which word shall it be? How are we to choose among the riches of the words of Scripture, without running the risk of selecting a word which is in harmony with our mood, and with those secret wishes hidden in our mood? The New Testament contains no word which has a direct bearing on a situation such as is ours to-day; it contains no word which alludes to specific turning points of time, whether those which nature has fixed such as morning and evening, or the course of the year and its seasons; or the beginning and end of those periods into which time is divided for the organization of our human work. The New Testament pays no attention to these time periods which are so significant to us. It knows only one critical turning point of time—when the "time was fulfilled"; it knows only one "now" which in human life is lifted above the stream of time—the "now" when the word of God strikes a man and which becomes for him the decisive moment between time and eternity.

Hence it would be a mistake to search the Scripture in the hope of finding a word which is especially appropriate to this evening hour of worship. And we may seek only the word of Scripture, the one word which is the same for all times and which alone makes any time decisive, which alone makes any time a genuine beginning or ending, an ultimate turning point.

### II

Nevertheless, it is sensible to look for a particular word of Scripture; for the one word is enshrined in many forms. And if this evening we seek such a word—we who are specially conscious now of the passing of time-then let it be a word which assures us, whose time is now fading away, of eternity lying above and beyond time; let it be a word which will bring the peace of God into the dispeace and agitation of our human time. Let us listen to the farewell words of Jesus to His disciples:

"Peace I leave with you; my peace I give to you. Not as the world gives, give I to you. Let not your hearts be troubled, neither let them be afraid" (John 14: 27).

It is peace which we need, peace which gives us the certitude that we are not delivered over to the irresistible sweep of time but are already integrally rooted in eternity. But it is the peace of the Lord which the world cannot give; His peace which is essentially other than all contentment which the world can give. And does not the end of the term make us acutely aware that we need this peace?

There is a pagan meditation for evening which we might like to remember at this evening hour too:

"Let not sleep sink on tired eyelids without giving a thought to each of the day's deeds. Where have I failed? What have I done? What has remained undone? Thus search your heart honestly. And then you may repent of what you have done amiss, and rejoice in what you have done well."

Who could deny that such meditation would be proper for each one of us at the close of this term? Ought we not to reflect on what we have achieved and what we have failed to achieve? on the opportunities which the term has brought us and which we have used and turned to good accountas also on what it has denied us and that in which we ourselves have been found wanting. No doubt the result will be different in each individual case if we weigh things up and see whether the profit or loss has been the greater; very different the measure of joy or sorrow, of thankfulness or bitterness in our hearts. But there can be no doubt of this, namely, that each one of us should be thankful. Even he to whom the term has brought distress and disappointment, and who is haunted by the tormenting sense of the un-attained, of having trodden devious paths. He too who would like to echo the words of the poet:

"How precious is the coming day, O my judge! How one dreams of wandering in the mountains and of great things! But the day that has just died is terrible; unwillingly, regretfully we look back on it, as on a rubbish heap."

Should not even such a one feel obliged to be thankful? For if he honestly faces the bitterness of his inner self-judgment, must he not confess that in consequence he should be purer and more experienced? But may he affirm this to himself? Now, it is exactly to such a man that the word applies: "My peace I give you. Not as the world gives, give I to you. Let not your hearts be troubled, neither let them be afraid!" For has he not been pursuing a false peace? A peace such as the world gives and which he sought to attain himself and by his own resources? The peace of God can only be given him; and it will be given when such a person turns his attention away from time and from thoughts of self, and is ready for the peace of eternity: "My peace I leave with you. My peace I give to you!"

But this word of Scripture applies no less to those who are looking back on the term with glad and thankful hearts: on its tasks which inspired increasing ability, on their progress in knowledge and understanding, and on the enrichment of their lives through social intercourse and fellowship with others. They too must take to heart this word of Scripture: "My peace I give you; not as the world gives, give I to you" lest from grateful joy they should fall into complacency and self-satisfaction.

Just as none of us, even those who are most disappointed, should look back without gratitude, so none should fail to ask himself the searching question, "Where have I failed? what has remained undone?" And if he faces the test of this inner self-examination, if he discovers where he has been neglectful, and what should be improved in the future-and which of us must needs not confess so much?—is it really enough to make up one's mind to do better in the future? There is no doubt that at the end of next term we shall again have to look back in shame on the things we have failed to do and the points at which there is room for improvement, and so on throughout the entire course of our lives. We should have to have a great deal of time at our disposal if we were really to make up for what we have neglected, and the truth is that we have no such extra time to dispose of. Each future to which we look forward brings its own tasks, and what is gone, is gone for ever. Our lives are inescapably caught up in the ever-flowing time stream. Who knows how soon he will have used up this available time? We do not lay hold of peace by our repentance and good intentions; we remain in perpetual dispeace and restlessness.

This state of affairs suggests that our repentance has not the power to make us better. We always remain the same; we are not masters of ourselves. If it is the case that a retrospective glance such as we are taking to-day always shows that our consciousness includes the twofold feeling-grateful joy and tormenting shame-then this is no incidental thing but rather is suggestive of the very pattern and structure of our being. This characteristic is the fissure that runs through it. The really torturing and painful thing is not the particular actions in which we have erred or which we have failed to perform, but just this: the inescapable dualism in our nature.

"Dein Psalmist, mein Vater, hat wider seine Feinde gesungen,

Und ich, mein Vater, folge ihm, und singe einen Psalm hier

> wider meinen Feind!

"Ach, ich habe keine Feinde, denn wir Menschen lieben

einander nicht einmal so sehr, um uns Feinde zu sein. Aber ich habe einen Feind, einen gewaltigen Feind, der mich berennt und an alle meine Tore pocht. "Ich habe einen Feind, mein Vater, der meine edle Rede

in Geschwätz verkehrt und in Selbstbetrug. Ich habe einen Feind, der mein Gewissen liebedienerisch

> macht

und meine Liebe mid Trägheit erstickt . .. "Warum hast Du mich mit diesem Feind erschaffen, mein

> Vater,

warum mich zu dieser Zweiheit gemacht? Warum gabst Du mir nicht Einheit und Reinheit?

Reinige, einige mich, o Du Gewässer!

"Befreie mich, reinige mich, mein Vater, töte diesen Feind,

töte mich, ertränke diesen Mich!"1 In answer to this prayer there sounds the word of Jesus: "Peace I leave with you, my peace I give to you." Such a word frees us from our inner division, and slays the enemy we harbour within ourselves. For this is the word of love which gives us the assurance that God does not see us as those who we see ourselves in our inner distress to be, but as those who are purified and unified in the embrace of His

> **[1]** Franz Werfel:  
> "Your psalmist, O my Father, sang to denounce his enemies,  
> And I, my Father, follow suit and sing a psalm against my foes!  
> "But alas! I have no enemies, for we men do not love  
> Each other enough to make real enemies.  
> But I have an enemy, a powerful enemy,  
> Who assaults me and hammers at all my gates.  
> "I have an enemy, my Father, who turns my noble speech  
> Into idle chatter and self-deception.  
> I have an enemy who makes my conscience to serve my desires,  
> And kills my love with idleness.  
> "Why have you created me, my Father, with an enemy like this,  
> And made me thus discordant;  
> Why did you not give me oneness and pureness of soul?

> "Free me, cleanse me, O my Father, kill this enemy,  
> Slay and drown this me!"

love. And one thing alone can save us from the world and from ourselves: that we truly place ourselves before God as the One who alone has the right to judge us, who overthrows the verdict of our own self-examination by His judgment which humbles us far more deeply than our own, because it is the judgment of love which is spoken to us in Jesus. "My peace I leave with you. My peace I give to you. Not as the world gives, give I to you. Let not your hearts be troubled, neither let them be afraid."

### IV

Our disquiet and distress in the midst of the world spring however not only from our own personal conduct, but also from the fate in which we are involved, however characteristically our conduct and our fate may be intertwined. Our meditation this evening must consist not only in reflecting about ourselves, but also in reflecting about the world in which we are placed. For it is just as we consider the surrounding world that we need the promise: "My peace I give to you."

For is it possible to carry on our work without again and again being irritated and disturbed by the distress and dispeace which prevail in this world in which we are so deeply involved? Who could remain unmoved in face of the calamity which has overtaken millions of men, our brothers in the Far East? And who must not be filled with continuing pain and indignation on hearing of the injustice and violence, of the sacrifices which have been and still are, here and there, the awful consequences of political revolution? Which of us has not been oppressed by the sufferings of the Church, and which of us this evening can fail to think of the troubles that have disturbed the intimate circle of our studies and of those who have been, directly or indirectly, affected? Can we preserve our inner peace in the midst of all this tormenting unrest and disturbance? In fact, ought we to try so to do?

We could not and ought not to do so if the peace which should fill our days were that peace of the soul which a man has to wrestle to attain by personal struggle, and which can only be maintained by the deliberate exclusion of disturbing influences. But that too would be a peace such as the world gives, since it would be we ourselves who fight to conquer it. The peace which the word of Jesus promises is the peace of eternity, which abides with us at the very heart of our inner disquiet and the storms which beat upon us, just because it is not our peace but God's peace, which transcends our tumult and unrest. By this peace we gain the power to face our inner distresses, so that we do not attempt to impose silence on them, but rather let them speak out loudly. Thus, so far as is possible, we are enabled to comfort sorrow and to soothe pain, and we who know something of the peace of eternity are thus strengthened to deliver the message of this peace to the world, and to convey its very spirit to the world.

"Peace I leave with you. My peace I give to you. Not as the world gives, give I to you. Let not your hearts be troubled, neither let them be afraid." This is the voice of the love of God who calls us to Himselt, and calls us to come to Him as He invites us to come to this celebration of the Lord's Supper.

## St. Matthew 11:2–6

*December II, 1938*

Now when John heard in prison about the deeds of the Christ, he sent word by his disciples and said to him, "Are you he who is to come, or shall we look for another?" And Jesus answered them, "Go and tell John what you hear and see: the blind receive their sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed and the deaf hear, and the dead are raised up, and the poor have good news preached to them. And blessed is he who takes no offence at me."

ADVENT, the time of preparation for the Feast of the Nativity, the incarnation of the Word, confronts us with the question: How shall I receive the Lord?

For the Third Sunday in Advent the Church chooses the gospel concerning the question of John the Baptist, so that from it we may learn the right way to prepare ourselves. This gospel tells us how the Baptist, cast into prison by Herod Antipas, sent two of his disciples to Jesus with the question: "Are you he who is to come?" Many readers are puzzled to know how the Baptist came to ask Jesus such a question. Did he not already know Jesus? Must it be that doubts arose in his mind with regard to Jesus? Or was it that the disciples were to ask Jesus this question not to satisfy any curiosity on the part of the Baptist, but for their own good, that they might hear from the lips of Jesus Himself that He was the One who should come? We do not know and ought not to seek to know. The story is told us only in order that we might hear this decisive question and might make it our own: "Are you he who is to come?"

Hence in the season of Advent we should concern ourselves with the question about the One who is to come. What does this question mean? What is implied in the phrase "the One who is to come"?

In the time of John the Baptist and Jesus the question was a very living one and the hearts of the Jewish people were aglow with hope in the One who was to come. We refer to the nation's hope in the Messiah, the King who was to reign in the latter days of blessedness, who was to bring to an end all sorrow and wrong on the earth and to set up the kingdom of God, a kingdom of righteousness and salvation. Such hopes arose from the words of the ancient prophets, which were carefully preserved in the Scriptures and cherished with yearning hearts. And from hopes of this kind arose the question: When will the expected Messiah come? Who will it be? "Watchman, what of the night?" And it is from the ferment of such hopes that Jesus is challenged with the question: "Are you He?"

The question flows from the Messianic hope. Hence we ourselves can ask such a question only if such a hope is alive in us. It is a question which has no meaning for all those who are satisfied with the world as it is. Whether it be that the world is for them a place of pleasure and enjoyment, where they disregard the shadows which of course there are in life, and cling to the light that is given them, banishing cares from their minds and seizing the joys which each day brings. Or again it might be that the world for such people is a sphere in which they fulfil a vocation to work and struggle, accomplishing their tasks either as individuals or communities, tasks which reward initiative and self-sacrifice, and make life worth living.

"For the strong man the world is not silent and un-responsive. Why should such a one waste his life in dreaming of eternity? What he knows can be grasped and embodied!" It cannot be denied that men to-day are to a large extent satisfied with the world and that, in so far as they are not, they live in the belief that it lies in their own hands to give the world the form which they consider right, and that for this high aim they are prepared to sacrifice their time and strength and in fact also their property and blood. But

IOI anything corresponding to the Jewish hope of a deliverer is remote from their minds.

For this hope presupposes of course that man in his inner being is dissatisfied with all that the world can offer of beauty and nobility, with all its values and all its demands; it pre-supposes that man suffers because of the actual condition of the world, when he sees that life and death are here interlocked in an unending struggle, that evil always holds the greater sway over man's heart, and that man despairs of ever being able to establish on this earth a kingdom of righteousness and love.

Do we belong to those who suffer in the world as it is? Have we this hope glowing in our hearts? To-day we hear voices loudly raised and in no uncertain manner, to declare that such a hope is not only a childish dream but is actually pernicious and morbid, since it incapacitates people to perform the work that is required to be done; and that for these reasons it should be fought and conquered by all possible means. And in this struggle which is now engaged, it is being said: what is the origin of such a hope? It originates with Israel and the Jews! with Jewish prophets and dreamers! All that is essentially foreign to our own way of life.

We must not allow ourselves to be led astray by such voices even if the picture they draw were true. But it is not true. Suffering in face of this present world is familiar not only to the Old Testament, but to all peoples everywhere. And the hope of a time of deliverance, in which sorrow and pain will be banished from the earth by divine aid, is by no means confined to Israel but is widespread throughout the world. It is with justice that the Christmas hymn declares: "With longing hearts have the nations waited for Thee, until the time was ripe!"

All over the earth there has always been the living hope in a return of paradise, a restoration of the early golden age, when there was no pain and no wrong on earth. The north Germanic tribes as well as the Aryan Persians regarded the world as the sphere of a struggle between two forces, those of good and evil, of light and darkness. They knew that the whole history of man stood under the shadow of world destruction and world judgment; and hope, soaring beyond this state of affairs, concentrated itself on a renewal of earthly life, a new humanity, a new acon. In particular the early Church fathers were astonished to see that the Roman poet Virgil, of the period of the emperor Augustus, wrote a prophetic poem about the One who is to come. In his vision the poet thinks he sees that the last age is here and

> "Time's great Order's born again..•  
> If any prints of ancient crimes remain,  
> Thou shalt efface them in thy happy reign,  
> And from perpetual fear all Nations free."

And he thus addresses the divine child who is to usher in this new age:

"Go, heavenly race, great progeny of Jove,

> The time draws near thy Honour to improve.  
> See how the Pole shakes, with the pond'rous Globe

Of Earth and Sea, and Heaven's all-spangled Robe;

> How all things at the approaching age rejoice!" 1

Yes, it may be said with truth: "With longing hearts have the nations waited for Thee." In all ages men have suffered because of the woes of the world, and always there has arisen the hope for a time of salvation which will be ushered in by a divinely-sent Ruler. A German poet of the igth century, Karl Immermann, has given expression to this hope in a series of splendid poems. To-day they are almost forgotten; but they are worth recalling. The first one begins:

> "I gaze into our night and lo, beyond  
> Our time, a star to guide our children's course!  
> Star by whose light our duties shall respond  
> To that true God and Lord who is their source.  
> "Its rising is not yet. Like ancient woe  
> The story of our days, our age's stain,  
> Must be effaced, that this new seed may grow  
> And yonder king foreshadowed rise and reign."

> **[1]** From Eclogue IV; tr. John Ogilby.

This hope only faded with the period when man began to suppose that by knowledge and technical science he could wholly subjugate the world to his own dominion. We are now living in this age, and indeed we are at its climax since we not only understand how to organize and exploit the powers of nature by technical mastery, but also suppose that we can control the life of man by means of an ingeniously contrived and subtly elaborated technique designed to ensure complete dominance over the minds of men. Thus it is hoped to establish an empire of success.

How in such circumstances shall our vision penetrate beyond this visible world into the realm of the eternal and invisible? How should we lift up our eyes to the hills whence comes our help, since we expect all needed help to flow from our own insight and power of accomplishment? How should we still hope for the end of this world and its ways? How should we still be able to ask the question concerning the One who is to come? But this is the mind of Christians, that they suffer in face of the world and its woes. Not that they withdraw themselves from the world, or refuse gratefully to receive the world's gifts, or to co-operate in the work of the world with a full sense of responsibility. But it is well that they realize that all this world's gifts and demands cannot make a man happy within, nor can they in the last resort claim the surrender of his being. Christians know that in this world there can be no reign of peace and justice and pure joy.

How do they know this? They cannot hide from themselves the sway which death exercises over the earth, nor transfigure it with fine-sounding words. They see how cruelly it often crushes the blossoms of life, and how its auxiliaries and accomplices, those powers which ravage youth and health, bring distress and pain to men which cannot always be removed even by the most advanced hygiene and medical science. They know that no human achievement is abiding, and that everything that men create and build is marked by the curse of decay; that all talk of the eternity of human accomplishments and values is blasphemy against God; for God alone is eternal and what He effects, abides. "All flesh is grass, and all its beauty like the flower of the field. The grass withers, the flower fades, when the breath of the

> Lord blows upon it;

surely the people is grass. The grass withers, the flower fades; but the word of our God will stand for ever." (Isa. 40: 6-8.) Christians realize that whenever any system is established in this world, however noble and lofty its aims and however much it may be inspired by the will to good, it is only possible through sheer power, and that in the process of setting up any secular order, whether nationally or internationally, human beings are sacrificed and crushed. The prosperity of some is purchased only at the expense of the sufferings and indeed often the destruction of others. That is the way things inevitably go in this world. But Christians refuse to be satisfied with this inevitability; they feel as a painful burden the sorrows and the tears of those to whom violence is done. And if this is the unalterable way of things in this world, if the way of secular government is a way which always leads through blood and tears, then we conclude that this very fact is a sign that the dominion of men is never the dominion of God nor can it ever lead to the dominion of God.

Christians know above all, however, when they honestly reflect on their own personal lives, that in their own hearts the strife between good and evil is unending, that they never completely master evil, that repeatedly the power of the lie triumphs over the will to truthfulness, that over and over again cowardice paralyses our courage to speak the truth, that impure thoughts constantly arise and stain the purity of our heart and will; that repeatedly we are swept by outbursts of selfishness, bitterness and hatred which destroy in us the spirit of love. And Christians do not speak lightly of God because they cannot name Him without fear. They realize that the mere will to truth, purity and goodness, however sincere and alive, is not yet the same thing as the divine will in us, such as would permit us to talk boldly of the "God within us". They know that God is a holy God who is not mocked. They are aware that they cannot of their own will power free themselves from the toils of evil in which they are caught, and thus they look for a release and redemption from above. They look for the One who is to come.

### II

Do we belong to the number of those who suffer in this world? Those whose vision penetrates beyond the sphere of the visible to that of the eternal and invisible? Are we among those who hope and wait in expectancy for the One who is to come? This is the critical Advent question. If we do belong to this number then we must in our turn, like the Baptist, address to Jesus the question: "Are you the One who is to come?" Is Jesus Christ in truth the One in whom all our hopes and longings find their fulfilment? Is He the one who brings in the kingdom of peace?

Were He the One-then surely He must already have established His kingdom! If He is the destined Messiah, He has come already indeed, long ago. More than 1900 years ago! And has the world changed as a result of His coming? The Christian Church affirms just this: He has come and has ushered in the new age, and yet He is still the One who is to come. In what sense may this be understood?

What answer does Jesus give to the Baptist? Go and tell John what you hear and see! "The blind receive their sight and the lame walk, lepers are cleansed, and the deaf hear, the dead are raised up, and the poor have good news preached to them. And blessed is he who takes no offence at me."

We notice, first, Jesus gives no direct answer. He says neither yes nor no. He refers the questioner to the things which he can see and hear; and thus the latter must himself

ıo6 win the answer to his own question; he must himself decide. And the final phrase expresses the full seriousness of the decision: "Blessed is he who takes no offence at me." It is therefore clear that if Jesus is the One who is to come, He is not so in the plain unequivocal way which longings and hopes, desires and fancies picture. He is not so in the sense which men imagine that He must be. That He should be the One who is to come is an astonishing fact, and for many repulsive and offensive. As to whether He truly is the One who is to come, the burden of decision rests on us.

But secondly, there must have been a certain state of affairs with regard to Him, arousing a certain reaction towards Him, so that the grounds for a considered judgment were there. And what is this state of affairs? The picture is suggested in the words: "The blind see." Jesus points to His mighty deeds, which we are accustomed to call His miracles.

His contemporaries including His disciples narrated stories about these deeds, which to-day prompt the question: how are such happenings to be properly assessed and judged? What really happened in those far-off days? And whatever be the answer to this question, it is pointed out that we modern people are in no way furthered thereby. For us, these miracles do not constitute proof! No, and neither were they a proof at the time of their taking place; they did not convince everyone and in fact were rather for many a cause of offence.

Where Christ holds sway, miraculous things happen, but the miraculous is not proof in any sense acceptable to the world; it is only an intimation, which invites decision. Where Christ reigns, marvellous things happen; and if we proceed to ask whether this is still the case to-day, we must be careful not to ask for proofs which might relieve us of the burden of decision. There are of course no such proofs. It is often attempted with the best intentions to adduce a proof of the truth of Christianity by pointing to the enduring effects which the Christian religion has had on the course of world history, by showing what it has done for the development of human morals and manners, for the emancipation and enrichment of the human mind, and for human culture. But such phenomena constitute neither miracle nor proof. They furnish no proof; for one might say (and it is in fact said that Christianity had great significance for a certain epoch of history, but all that is over now and done with. Nor do they constitute the miraculous; for all spiritual and psychic forces work out their due effects in history, and everyone is influenced by them whether he is aware of it or not. But these things do not call for a decision nor are they offensive. Miracles, properly speaking, are only those events which essentially cannot be classified within natural and historical sequences, and which in the natural historical process stand out as arousing the sentiments of wonder, astonishment and repulsion. A miracle of such a kind isquite simply-every straightforward manifestation of Christian love, which strikes us, shames us, convulses our being, and yet quickens us.

A miracle is in fact every deed-though it is not strictly speaking a human deed—and every event which takes place where the Spirit and mind of Christ hold sway. And where does Christ hold sway? Wherever the Word of the Gospel is preached and heard in faith. And for this reason the most important element in the answer of Jesus, and in the light of which alone the other affirmations are intelligible, is this: the poor have good news preached to them. For it cannot be denied that there are deeds which are inspired by nothing other than the proclamation of the Gospel of Christ; such deeds have arisen not from natural impulses, or from moral considerations, or from the intention of serving the common good and bettering human conditions, but simply and solely from love, the love which the Gospel of Christ awakens in the human heart. These deeds do not propose to change the world, nor are they done with a view to the effect which they will have on the world's life; they in fact happen because the Word of the Gospel has commanded them—nay, has liberated the spirit of man to perform them.

We shall not enumerate such deeds, nor do we propose to refer to the organized works of Christian love; for only he can recognize them as miraculous who experiences their impact upon himself. They do not furnish proofs, for anyone is free to elude their efficacy, may indeed become scandalized by them; for it is often the case that they do not fit into the plan of the world's life, into its pedagogic or sociological and political schemes, into the efficient organization of things. They have no other ground than the fact that they have been inspired by the preaching of the Gospel.

For the real miracle is just this—that the good news of Christ is preached to the poor. The poor; that is to say, those who suffer in this visible world and who wait for the world to come. And what is the message preached to them? It may be summed up in one word: freedom from the present world. The Gospel has the power to free us from the oppression of the world, because it is the message of God's grace and the forgiveness of sins. It tells us that we become free from the world and from ourselves, when we honestly and un-reservedly commit ourselves to God who assures us of His grace, in the word and the person of Jesus Christ.

The Word of God's grace makes all things new for the believer. He who is to come has in truth come and has renewed the world, for those who allow their being to be renewed and transformed by Him. "Therefore if any one is in Christ, he is a new creation" (2 Cor. 5: 17). Now there exists the possibility of the total renewal of humanity, and to one whose being is thus renewed, the world too is renewed. "The light of eternal life shines here and gives the world a new appearance." In what way has the world become new? Paul describes this transformation of the world when he paints the picture of the true servants of God: they live their life and fulfil their ministry "through great endurance, in afflictions, hardships, calamities, beatings, imprisonments, tumults...; by purity, knowledge, forbearance, kindness, the Holy Spirit, genuine love, truthful speech and the power of God; with the weapons of righteousness for the right hand and for the left; in honour and dishonour, in ill repute and good repute. We are treated as impostors and yet are true, as unknown and yet well known; as dying and behold we live; as punished and yet not killed; as sorrowful yet always rejoicing; as poor yet making many rich; as having nothing and yet possessing everything" (2 Cor. 6: 4-10).

If then Jesus is He who is to come, and if, after His coming, the world is renewed for the man who allows his being to be transformed by the Saviour, then we have also the answer to our other question: how can we, after He has come, still speak of Him as the One who is to come?

Just because through His coming, our outlook on the world and on time is completely changed. The event of His coming is not an event within the process of world history, an event such as is straightway merged with the past; it is rather an event which signifies the end of the process of world history. Those who believe in the One who is to come are lifted out of the flux of time into the transcendence of eternity; they have truth, purity, and fullness of life; as pain and death have no further power to reduce them to despair, so the evil around them and within them can no longer make them afraid. It is engulfed in the full tide of divine grace.

Faith gives to the Christian this unshakable certitude. And it is the characteristic situation of Christians that so long as they remain on earth they must walk by faith, not sight. They who in Christ are made victorious over the world of time, nevertheless still remain immersed in that world. And just because the coming of Jesus is not merely an event taking its place in past history, but an event which spells the end of all history, for them the One who has come is always at the same time the One who is to come and will be so to the end of the world and of time. For ourselves who still are implicated in the process of world history, He is always the One who is to come, always the One who ever anew enables us to transcend the flux and vicissitudes of time, with its sorrow and sin and death.

Thus He confronts us always as the One who is to come, and the season of Advent admonishes us to wait for Him as

IIO the coming One. But inasmuch as Advent proclaims not only His ever expected future coming, to which we look forward in every distress and sore temptation, but proclaims also His having come in the Holy Nativity, it draws our attention at the same time to that unique miracle which summons us to decision. For the Holy Nativity of our Saviour is a miracle in the twofold sense that at this point there is proclaimed loud and clear to the poor the good news of the eternal light which transforms the appearance of the world, and also in that through this Word the flame of divine love is once more kindled among us.

For the Incarnation is a sheer miracle. The feast of Christmas which celebrates it has as a result lost just that sense which in pagan times characterized the festival of the winter solstice. The Christian feast is precisely not a festival growing out of the regular rhythm of nature and human lite which is so closely intertwined with nature. Rather as a Christian feast it is torn out of nature's continuity and becomes the celebration of the miracle that to the poor of this world the good news is preached. No doubt this Christian feast is so deeply rooted in our whole way of life that it is also accompanied by much natural love and kindness which is not awakened by the Word. So much the more, for us Christians, this Word of the Gospel should stand right at the heart of the feast, penetrating, deepening and limiting all natural love, so that our love, awakened by the Word, should take on the quality of a witness to that Word.

Whether we are truly preparing ourselves to receive with expectant hearts the One who is to come, we can decide as we ponder seriously the lines of the old Advent hymn: "And he who would embrace and kiss the Holy Child with

joy In pain and torment with Him first his spirit must employ.

Thereafter die as He has died, with Him to rise and reign; For he who treads the Saviour's path, His deathless life

> shall gain."

## St. John 8:12; 9:39; 12:35–36

*December 14, 1939*

"I am the light of the world; he who follows me will not walk in darkness, but will have the light of life."

"For judgment I came into this world, that those who do not see may see, and that those who see may become blind."

"The light is with you for a little longer. Walk while you have the light, lest the darkness overtake you; he who walks in the darkness does not know where he goes. While you have the light. believe in the light, that you may become sons of light." THis year we are observing the season of Advent in time of war, and are preparing for a wartime Christmas. Whether there is war or not, nature follows its fixed regular pattern of seasons. After the bitter cold of winter the springtime sun will shine; the earth will grow warm, green leaves and flowers will burst into bloom, and the human heart, even though it is wartime, will be cheered and animated by this renewal of nature. Is that the case also with regard to Christmas? Will it come and bring its gift to us with the same certainty as ever?

Yes and no! To be sure, the Christmas message will be proclaimed as usual when the day comes; and the immemorial customs bound up with its celebration will as ever somehow lend a festal glow to our houses. But does that signify the real coming of Christmas, which comes of course only when its message penetrates the individual heart? Certainly the message of the angel is meant for all: "Behold, I bring you good news of a great joy." But everything depends on whether the individual yields himself to this joy. Whether Christmas really comes to us means after all whether joy really floods our hearts in spite of all our care, anxiety and distress. The true coming of Christmas into our lives is essentially a question of the coming of the light of life to drive away our darkness.

I I2

Joy! light! Who does not wish it for himself! Of course at a time like the present we feel very strongly that it must be no ordinary joy—not the kind of rejoicing in which we might forget what is happening around us and with us, no joy which might lull us into forgetfulness of the heavy hardships we are undergoing, hardships which forbid all frivolous delights, no joy which would drown our remembrance of those who are fighting for us, and in face of whom we at home ask ourselves the question whether we have any right to rejoice at all, or to wish joy for ourselves. No! it must be no ordinary joy but it must at least be a joy which we would share in common with our fighting men, and which would link us with them. But in any case it cannot be joy of an ordinary kind. For how could any ordinary joy last in face of the distresses of the time? Such joy would be no more than the frivolous pleasures of two or three fleeting days, after which all would seem dark again around us and within us.

No, it can be no ordinary joy! It would at least have to be a joy which had the power to predominate over anxiety and grief, and to become within us a living source of power to transform the darkness around and within into light. No ordinary joy, which again means no joy such as earthly things confer. It would have to be eternal supernatural joy. And this is what it is to be! The message of Christmas speaks of precisely such a joy; and for this heavenly joy are we to prepare ourselves in the season of Advent. We are to move towards an encounter with Him who wishes us to come to Him, and who says: "I am the light of the world. He who follows me will not walk in darkness but will have the light of life."

"The light of life"-what is the meaning of that? What does Jesus promise the one who follows Him? What are we to picture to ourselves as the meaning of the phrase, "light of life"? To be frank, we can form no clear idea of what it means. But that very fact suggests simply that the gift of Jesus is not of this world. For all the values which we can imagine to ourselves as specific quantities in clear and firm outline, even if they are ideal values (so-called) such as beauty or character or nobility of soul, belong nevertheless to the sphere of the human. They are of this world. Their radiance fades in the very acute distress which now grips our hearts; they fail us in times of trouble, bitter sorrow and sore temptation. And we surmise that the "light of life" is something which lies beyond them, just as indescribable and vague as that which hovers before the minds of all of us and which the naïve man calls "luck"; no one can precisely say what it is. Or again just as indescribable and vague as what we mean when we speak of the fulfilment and fruition of our being, of what we essentially are or what we wish to be and ought to be and yet never are and of which we have only the vaguest intuition! But is it not just that which Jesus means when He promises us the light of life? He pre-supposes that everyone longs for it. And the newness of His message is only this: "I am it!" "I am the light of life!"

And now what this "light of life" is may be formulated somewhat more plainly, in so far as we may express more clearly what it means for our lives, however mysterious it may be. Why is it that this thing which we long for, without knowing clearly its nature, is called the "light of life"? Light is the power of illumination bringing clarity of vision so that in consequence we walk "in the light" and do not grope in darkness, uncertain of the way. Hence the "light of life" would mean that life for us gains transparent clarity, that the oppressive problems, the torturing mysteries of our life come to an end; that we are no longer afraid of what is to come, of what threatens, that we are no longer strangers to ourselves, that, so to speak, everything has become self-explanatory. "In that day you will ask me no questions," says Jesus to His disciples when He promises them the joy which no man can take from them (John 16: 23). No longer to need to ask questions because our whole existence has become full of light-that is what it means to have the "light of life".

No longer to feel the need to ask questions in the midst of all darkness, pain and grief; to be surrendered and un-afraid, quite sure of ourselves and of our life. This does not mean of course to be simply freed from pain and anxiety, nor even to be no longer pressed by questions and problems, as they constantly confront us in the duties of our life and in our fate. But it does mean that in our inner being we cease to be ambiguous, with the result that all anxiety and pain, all questions and problems have lost their sting for us; that they no longer have any power to assail us; that in calm and cheerful self-certitude we rise above their buffeting.

### II

This "light of life" Jesus promises to them who follow Him. The price to be paid for the gift is that of discipleship. What does this mean? At bottom it means nothing other than truly to desire what is promised. The peculiar feature here is that the word of promise is the word of judgment also: "For judgment I came into this world, that those who do not see may see and that those who see may become blind." It is the very word of promise which is at the same time a word declaratory of judgment; the gift itself, the "light of life" effects judgment. For it effects a discrimination between those who accept the gift and those who close their hearts against it. The judgment that is here in question is not a special arrangement for the end of time, but a process consummated in the hearing of the gospel proclamation itself. Elsewhere we read, "And this is the judgment, that the light has come into the world and men loved darkness rather than light" (John 3: 19).

This is very strange! Do we not all love light? Do we not all long for the light of life, for that luminosity of existence which enables us to be victorious over the world? No doubt we all have a dim surmise and a passionate longing-however misunderstood-that we must somehow be delivered from this world, from what is merely given and actual; a feeling that the world can never give us an inexhaustible satisfaction and fulfilment, that everything in the world, everything merely given and actual, is corrupted by the curse of the inadequate, unfulfilled and transitory. It is just this which we feel and dimly suspect when we speak of our real self and its fulfilment. But have we the will to carry through this feeling? Are we really ready, when the beyond, when God confronts us, to surrender the world? Hence are we truly ready, when God confronts us in the figure of Jesus, to become His disciples and to allow the world to be simply the world? This is the choice which is in question here. This is the point on which we are called to make a clear-sighted decision, namely that what at times we probably dimly surmise, what at times we no doubt long for—that it should really become the controlling force in our lives. Here is the decisive issue which Jesus puts before men. The light which He sheds is the light of the eternal world; the joy which He bestows is supernatural joy. Truly to desire this light and this joy, hence to be His disciple, means to live as though one no longer really belonged to this world. Do we desire this with all our hearts?

That restless unappeasable surmise that our true self cannot find its fulfilment in this world, in this-worldly values and aims, gifts and duties, usually comes over us only when we disastrously fail in our desires and strivings, when that to which we had lost our hearts, that which made our little world dear and familiar and homely to us, is shattered. Or again it visits us when a sinister future looms threateningly before us, for which we feel we are no match, and in whose sombre shadows the colours of the present fade. At such moments as these when the world in which we live suddenly strikes us as alien, when in the midst of its homelessness we long for our true home, we are visited by this intimation of the beyond. Then it may well be that we feel as if the affairs of this world in which we are so deeply implicated were a confusing dream from which we must awake. The actualities in which we live seem to us unreal and ghostly. "There are dark moments of dismay and moods of sad heart-sickness, when all hovers dim and ghostly, in a sea of mist."1 But

> **[1]** Novalis.  
> I 16

is it not true that what we experience at such times is usually soon forgotten? Do we not normally become once more quickly caught up in life with its claims, its this-worldly values and purposes, the pressing responsibilities of the day and moment? So that, in fine, this world is for us equated with reality? But is it not possible that we viewed reality more deeply and fully in those moments of failure in which all the turmoil and striving of this world struck us as alien and, in essence, of questionable value?

"The most frightening dream is accompanied by a secret feeling that it all means nothing, howsoever we sweat with fear. A loving smile shines gently upon our tears. But I would like to think that it must always be so."1

Yes, in fact it should always be so; we should always be prepared to see the realities of this world and our life within them as appearance only, in which genuine reality does not inhere—or which can become real only through ourselves, when we ourselves are living in the power of a higher, more genuine reality as a result of which we can transform our life in the world. For of course the Christian faith and discipleship to Jesus is not a mere matter of resignation, it is not merely a feeling of homelessness in the world, it is not simply something negative. Rather it is just the consciousness of living in the fullness of a power which streams from super-natural, ultimate reality. But to receive this power demands the readiness to adopt an attitude of inner detachment from the world, so that we move in it as though we did not belong to it, are active within it, rejoice or mourn in it as though we did not do so. In the last resort this means that we should be detached from ourselves, from that restless life which we know to be active in our wishes and plans, our work and our enjoyment. What is required from us is the confession that we are not those who see, but are blind.

"For judgment I came into this world, that those who do not see may see and that those who see may become blind." This means that following Jesus requires from us a resignation of the claim to be able to take our lives into our own

> **[1]** Hebbel.

hands, of the delusive supposition that we ourselves can find the way which leads to the fulfilment of our life and to our true self; resignation of the fancy that we ourselves are powerful, are the masters in control of things. To have the "light of life" means to have no further question to ask. Do we wish that? Are we willing to give up our questioning attitude, our persistent "why and wherefore"? For so long as we ask, we are anxious to be the masters of ourselves.

Yes, we often wish for each other light in the darkness of our days and in the mystery of our destiny. But we shall only receive it when we pray for light to illuminate the darkness of our inner being. Discipleship implies the confession that here in our inmost being the darkness is thickest, it implies the readiness to receive our true selves as a gift from the hands of God, and to allow ourselves to be re-created as children oflight in the light which streams from the Holy Child.

But if we are so prepared, God wills to bestow His gift on us. To such preparedness the Christmas hymn of Novalis applies: "See the far glory in the kindling east!

The time comes round that makes the old world new. Rise-let us drink triumphant at the feast

Of light itself, diffused in every hue! The powers on high have read man's deep desire And Love transformed comes down in heavenly fire." He bestows upon us the "light of life", that unquestioning transparent luminosity of our being. For in Him the love of God shines fully; because, if we are so prepared, He becomes understandable to us as the very act and initiative of the divine love, as the gift to us of the divine love. It is the love of God which has always sustained us and ever will sustain us. To be sustained and held by the love of God means to have an attitude of unquestioning childlike acceptance, to be comforted and commit ourselves to the control of a hand which guides everything for the best even when we ourselves do not know what the best for us is.

By this means our life gains clarity and peace. This does not mean that in the ground plan of our life, in its purposes and aims, we embody an intelligent solution to the riddle of existence. Every question which is aimed at mastering the secret pattern of existence must in fact be silenced; and we remain ignorant of the goal to which God is leading us. No, our life gains clarity only because everything He sends us we may and must receive as the gift of His love:

"Let His loving glance deeply penetrate your soul, and His eternal light and joy will flood your being. Heart and mind and spirit shall then awaken to new life."

Luminous transparency must also penetrate our life in so far as it is a life of responsibility and action. We do not suggest that the Christian should be able to organize his life in accordance with a completely clear plan. The clarity which the divine light gives is not the clarity of a programme, but the clarity which flows from the awareness of being sustained by the divine love which frees us for the manifestation of love in our own lives. Our eyes are then opened to the truth that we ourselves can and must be bearers of divine light and love in the world; that the decisions with which each day and moment face us should be made in the spirit of love; that the plans and the projects which our responsibilities in daily life constantly demand from us, should be conceived in the spirit of love. We are able to see how much others need us and where and how we can help them. Thus our life becomes filled with a new radiance and transparency; thus our true self finds its fulfilment, if we no longer will to live for ourselves and by our own resources, but in the light of the love of God and in the service of others.

"Divinity, of which man has so often been afraid, has now been imparted to us. In the south and the north, it has awakened to life seeds of eternity. Let us then, in the richly dowered garden of God, faithfully await the blooming of each bud and blossom."

Yes, this world becomes God's garden, its apparent reality becomes genuine reality as a result of the fact that we are invited to encounter therein the love of God and are privileged to impart that love to others. And if our joy this Christmastide is to be and must be that we receive anew the gift of divine love and are freed by it to love with new power and inspiration, is not that something which will unite us with those who are fighting for us? They too are offered the gift of this joy and upon them too has this light shined, and all of us, both those at home and those out there at the front, must help each other to prepare ourselves to receive this joy and this light. At Advent we should make it clear to ourselves that we are faced here by an issue which calls for vital decision. Do we or do we not desire these divine gifts? Do we truly want the heavenly light to shine in our earthly darkness? Are we prepared to admit that our world, and that we ourselves, are wrapped in thick darkness? Let us rightly understand this summons to decision, let us obey the call to discipleship! Which of us can tell how much longer he will have to hear the Word?

"The light is with you for a little longer. Walk while you have the light, lest the darkness overtake you; he who walks in the darkness does not know where he goes. While you have the light, believe in the light, that you may become sons of light."

## St. Luke 18:9–14

*August 4, 1940*

He also told this parable to some who trusted in themselves that they were righteous and despised others: "Two men went up into the temple to pray, one a Pharisee and the other a tax collector. The Pharisee stood and prayed thus with himself, 'God, I thank thee that 1 am not like other men, extortioners, unjust, adulterers, or even like this tax collector. I tast twice a week, - give tithes of all that I get.' But the tax collector, standing far off, would not even lift up his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast, saying, 'God, be merciful to me a sinner!' I tell you, this man went down to his house justified rather than the other; for every one who exalts himself will be humbled, but he who humbles himself will be exalted."

### I

THe story of the Pharisee and the Publican may seem to us a very simple one which everybody can understand immediately, and the truth of which is transparently clear to all. And yet we ought to guard against the danger of taking it in too simple a sense and regarding its truth too much as a matter of course. Certainly in detail there is little to explain. The story is not strictly speaking a parable; for nothing is here compared. Parable is here used, as often in the New Testament, in a broad sense, that of an instructive story simply; and the story presents the contrasting figures of the Pharisee and the Publican as types so that we should ask ourselves: which of these types do we resemble? The Pharisee is the model of correct and zealous piety which in its observance of the law does too much rather than too little; the Publican is a despised figure who stands in close relation with foreigners, with Romans, and lives a dubious existence commonly disparaged as a life of fraud and trickery. The judgment to be passed on both seems completely a matter of course: we feel immediate sympathy with the Publican in his modesty and humility, whilst the Pharisee in his self-conscious pride is so repulsive to us that we are almost tempted to say: "I thank Thee, God, that I am not as this Pharisee!"

But if we were to speak thus we should have misunderstood the story. We should not in any case have rightly understood it as a message addressed to ourselves. It is dangerous if it appears to us as something completely self-explanatory. We must not forget that when the story was narrated it seemed to the average listener at least to be obnoxious and offensive. They felt it as a defiant and offensive thing that the exemplary Pharisee, who represented a generally respected class of men, and who was irreproachable, should have been put to shame thus by being contrasted with the Publican, who belonged to a class that was despised and avoided, a class with which no self-respecting man could have anything to do. We need only consider what our reaction would be if we ourselves, who perhaps do not feel ourselves to be particularly distinguished examples of our profession and status, but are nevertheless proud that we do belong to a respected class and follow an honourable calling in life—if we were similarly put to shame by being contrasted with the example of a man who came from the most degraded and shunned sections of the community, from which only what was squalid and vulgar could be expected. In any case if the story does not strike us too as offensive and obnoxious, as it struck its first hearers; if we do not feel that we are perhaps intended by the Pharisee; then it is to be feared that we have not rightly understood it, and listened to it.

We are certainly quite right in being careful to see that in our circle and profession, propriety and decorum and a good moral tone should be a matter of course; and it is assuredly legitimate for a man to be proud of his station in life, whatever it be, proud of his rank and profession and of the respect which accrues to it through conscientiousness and loyalty, just as a family or nation are justifiably proud if

I22 decency, reliability, industry and cleanness are virtues with them which are taken for granted. But we must not forget that there are situations in which a despised and outcast person can shame us, so that he suddenly emerges as great and we appear small in comparison. Jesus is fond of reminding His hearers of situations like this. Just such a situation is provided by the story of the Good Samaritan: the despised and outcast Samaritan puts to shame the priest and Levite. Most strange and scandalous! And here too: the despised Publican towers high above the highly respected Pharisee conscious of his rectitude. Most strange and scandalous!

Of course Jesus did not praise the Samaritan because he was a Samaritan, but because he was merciful. Nor does He here put the Publican above the Pharisee because he was a Publican. He does not propose either to condemn the pious zeal of the Pharisee, or to exonerate the vices incidental to the calling of the Publican. But the vision of Jesus pierces deeper than that superficial view of things with which we are usually content. He sees that the true essential being of man lies at a deeper level than that attitude which has become normal for him as the member of a particular profession or social class or even nation, whether good or bad. Yes, the real being of a man lies on a deeper level. There are situations in which it comes to light and those are the really decisive situations. As in the story of the Good Samaritan the situation is that a man sees someone lying wounded in a sinister area and on a dangerous road, and it is then revealed whether he realizes what is the meaning of love of one's neighbour, so here the situation is that of prayer which discloses whether one realizes what is man's true relation to God or not; whether man is inwardly arrogant or humble, whether he— as we may further say-knows himself or is self-deceived.

### II

Whom do we resemble, the Pharisee or the Publican? We must explore more deeply the picture that is conveyed of these two figures, and ask what constitutes the essential being of these two men and in what features we see our own being reflected.

When the Pharisee boasts of what he is and does, he is not lying. He is in truth a pious man distinguished for his piety and zeal, and in fact practises the works of piety which he enumerates. Nor is it his real failing that he forgets that there are also in his life, as in the lives of all men, things of which he could not boast before man and God. His real fault is rather that he should count up and boast of his good works in the presence of God at all; in supposing that by

', i.e. that this means he can stand before God as "justified" God could not fail to deny him recognition and reward. With his piety he does in truth serve God; but he serves Him only in order to gain by this service recognition and reward. By this attitude he discloses that what fills his being to its depths is not love of God and goodness, but the need for recompense. The more he exacts from himself in the way of self-renunciation and sacrifice, the more passionate must be his need for reward; the more proudly he points to what he does, the keener is his sense of his own deserving.

But this means that the figure of the Pharisee does not belong simply to a past age, and is not exclusive to the Judaism of the period concerned or even to the Jewish race in general, but is characteristic of humanity as a whole. Modern psychology itself has taught us what a part is played in every human life by the impulse to self-assertion. How many of the words and deeds of men are attributable to the desire to gain authority and importance, words and deeds in respect of both good and ill, the moral and the immoral, great achievements and great crimes. Psychology has shown us how the natural need for recognition, if suppressed, can assume morbid developments, and how a human life may be ruined and brought to disaster if this desire for recognition is repressed so that it results in a so-called inferiority complex. For this reason great importance is attached in education to helping the child to realize that it can achieve something so that its consciousness of its own value may be strengthened, and it does not need to feel itself to be inferior.

124

In fact, the need for recognition of one's value is something implanted in the very nature of man. We can only live and breathe freely in a circle where we are appreciated. We only feel ourselves to be uninhibited and secure when we have the feeling that we are esteemed at our full value. And just as we need that others should respect and appreciate us in our peculiar and characteristic worth, so we in our turn must respect the special merits of others and give them full recognition. But the decisive thing in this matter is that the recognition which we all need can never be made the real aim of our action and conduct. It can accrue to us only as the by-product of our behaviour, of our straightforward positive contribution, of our actual attitude towards our fellow men. Appreciation cannot be a goal to be striven after and attained with exertion and effort; it can only be given us as a gift.

The mistake made by so many is that they aim at appreciation as an end in itself, and they are anxious to attain it as a desirable good to the attainment of which they must concentrate their efforts. This urge for recognition can afflict a human life like a disease. It leads us astray into an impurity of motives, with the consequence that we do our work not disinterestedly for its own sake, but for our sake, and in order to gain credit for ourselves. A man who acts to increase his own prestige may well do great things; and it is just what we usually term record achievements which are often traceable merely to the urge for recognition. The greatness and true value of a work does not after all consist in the fact that it goes one better than other works of its kind, that it shows the intensity of the exertion and the extremity of the cost at which it has been achieved, but is decided simply by whether it is sensible and useful in its place, and serves the general good of the community.

If the mind of a man is obsessed by this urge for recognition, so that all his accomplishments, as it were, declare: "Look, that is what I can do!" then his whole life becomes corrupted by something hollow and unreal. He does not work with his eye on the thing itself but with a sidelong glance on others in whose presence he desires to distinguish himself. Sooner or later, in the case of most men, this failing will be avenged. The quality of the work done will suffer if a man thinks less of the matter itself than of the impression which he wishes to create on other people. We need hardly be reminded that there are men who from the urge to gain recognition undertake senseless and mad things, with the sole object of increasing their own sense of merit; just as there are children who, being incapable of doing anything useful, will do stupid and impudent things merely to draw attention to themselves.

Again, this lack of sincerity and objectivity goes hand in hand with a spirit of lovelessness. Men who are hungry for recognition may indeed compel respect and admiration for their accomplishments, but not love. Because they are concerned to win admiration from others, they in fact isolate themselves from others and make genuine friendship and fellowship impossible. When they take other people into account they do so in order to compare themselves with their fellows and to measure themselves against them with the object of strengthening the feeling of their own superior worth. Such a man becomes envious when he sees that others can do better than himself; he becomes arrogant and despises those to whom he thinks himself, in virtue of his achievements, superior. It is clear that true human society is ruined by this spirit. For community life flourishes only when the one freely appreciates the merits of the other and does not attempt to assert his own superiority, when each rejoices in the good which he sees in other people, gladly learns from them what can be learnt, gladly recognizes superior value wherever it is found. The man for whom other people serve only as a foil to set off his own sense of value becomes unjust, disputatious, harsh and unloving. He then resembles the portrait of the Pharisee who says: "I thank Thee, God, that I am not like other men!" We certainly feel that we should not like to have such people as friends. Do we wish to become such people ourselves?

Such a man poisons not only social and communal life but also his personal inner life. He strives to win recognition and in the measure of recognition which he attains he feels that he is strong, whereas in fact he is weak. Whether a man is really strong and free within is shown by the extent to which he feels that he is in a position to confess his own weaknesses and failings. When we are anxiously concerned to assert our merits, we take trouble to hide our weaknesses and to gloss over our faults. We easily succumb to bragging and lying, and especially to a dangerous trick of self-deception. There are many men who deceive themselves by refusing to face the fact of their weaknesses and faults, and flatter themselves by the splendid picture of their personalities which they conjure up. There floats before their minds an image of what they must be or would like to be, and they end by fancying that they are so in reality.

The Pharisee too is a man who deceives himself. There are many various ideals which infatuate the minds of men, for example the ideal of the quaintly interesting, or the heroic in various walks of life. The ideal which haunts the mind of the Pharisee is that of the severely moral and pious man.
