He belongs to those who do not shrink from energetic forms of self-sacrifice, and who do not spare themselves pain and trouble in order to obey the commandment, what the right and the good demands. And yet such people deceive themselves. All their moral exertion is vain, because they do not perform what is good from the motive of delight in the good, of serving others and increasing their happiness, but in order to be able to praise themselves. It is such men against whose blindness Jesus struggled; they are the "righteous who need no physician"-that is, who think that they need no physician and who rebuked Jesus as a "glutton and drunkard" because He did not associate Himself with their rigid moralism.

When we have thus pictured to ourselves the type of the Pharisee, and have allowed to pass before our imagination the portrait of the man who is greedy for the recognition of his deserts, the portrait of the man who is self-interested and loveless, who deceives himself, have we thought and spoken of others only? of people who are different from ourselves, so that finally we too might feel impelled to say: "God,

I thank Thee that I am not like other men"? Let each of us examine himself to see if there is not in him also something of the Pharisaic pattern of life, in some form or other, in some degree or other! Are we truly in our words and deeds filled with the spirit of disinterested honesty and objectivity, of faithful devotion to our work and service? Or are our minds tainted too in some measure with this will to recognition? With the intention of standing forth as distinguished by what we have done? Do we not often eye others with the intention of comparing ourselves with them and fancying ourselves to be better and greater? Or do we eye them sometimes with tenseness and anxiety lest they should get in front of us? Is not our attitude towards others often envious and loveless? And are we not as a result oftentimes contentious and dogmatic? Do we not often deceive ourselves and fancy that we are better and greater than we are in fact? Do we never falsify the essence of the good that we like to do when we do it not from genuinely good motives but in order to fortify our self-conceit?

There is an infallible test of our dispositions in this matter. For the man who is truly free from the urge to self-assertion, from arrogance and ruthlessness, from envy and self-deception, feels himself free and secure in mixing with others; he does not get vexed when he has to stand back behind those who are better than himself, and it is with pleasure that he recognizes their superiority. It is of no consequence to him either when he stands behind others who only fancy themselves to be better than he and whose false pretentiousness he penetrates. They cannot affect him harmfully; he does not allow himself to be embittered, but is always cheerful and free from self-interested thoughts. Do we always feel ourselves to be so detached and inwardly secure, and if not how shall we gain such inner security?

### III

Perhaps some of you have been thinking that our discourse has only taken into account the human need to enjoy

128 prestige in the eyes of other men, whereas in the parable it is a question of having credit with God. This is only apparently true, for in reality every man, whether he cares to admit it or not, is in the last resort concerned about his standing before God. For why does he wish to be recognized and distinguished by other men? At bottom not because he attaches so much importance to other men and their opinions; he may despise them even while hankering after their recognition. At bottom he needs their appreciation because he wishes to be certain of himself. In the last analysis every man feels that his existence is of questionable and dubious value; there lurks in the mind of every man the fear of nothingness and the void, and the fear that it may also be true of him to say: "Weighed in the balances and found wanting." And the life of men consists largely in the effort to delude oneself about these inner realities, and to suppress these fears. Man tries to do this by various means, and it is this very thing which he is trying to do in his search for recognition and prestige. Being aware of the emptiness of his inner life, he clings to the picture that others form of him. In this picture he as it were sees himself mirrored, he holds fast to it, and views in it the reflection of his true self. And just for this reason the picture must be as great and imposing as possible. In essence it is not in the eyes of others that he wishes to stand out as great, but in his own eyes; in essence he wishes to enjoy credit not before others but before himself.

Before himself! And that in the last resort means before God. It is God who faces him with this pressing question, whether he has something to show or whether he has lived in vain and worthlessly. He feels that he is responsible for his being and that he is called to make something of it. All human longing for recognition is ultimately aimed at providing an answer to that decisive question: am I, generally speaking, a creature of worth or am I worthless? Now of course it can make a great deal of difference whether and to what an extent a man reckons with God in his life or not. If a man no longer recognizes God as the controlling force in his life, then he has only the judgment of his fellow-men in view. If a man still reckons with God he strives to gain God's approval; but he does this in a false way since he views God as a man on whom he can make an impression by the count of his good deeds, just as the Pharisee does who boasts of his merits before God. Hence in truth men seek to stand in favour with God and at the same time they flee from God because they suppose that they can obtain the credit they desire by their own efforts and performances.

But how then are they to find it? How are they to be liberated from the tense struggle to win favour and prestige? How shall they gain freedom and security of life, and the assured conviction that their lives are not worthless but genuinely good? In no other way but by fleeing from themselves and seeking refuge in God! And it is just this which the portrait of the publican is intended to teach us. He "stood afar off and would not even lift up his eyes to heaven, but beat his breast saying, 'God, be merciful to me a sinner'". This man does not speak at great length. He does not compare himself with others and does not say for example: "Before Thee O God I lament the fact that I am not like other people who are so much better than I." He does not enumerate his faults: "Here and there I have forgotten myself! In one or two matters I have been un-faithful!" He says simply: "God be merciful to me a sinner!"

True self-knowledge in the face of God does not arise from comparing oneself with those who are better than oneself; nor from reflecting on what we have done badly and falsely. We may of course have plenty of occasions for self-examination and for remembering what we have done. And we must not silence the voice of conscience with its spontaneous warnings. But the important thing is not that in our prayers we should count up our sins before God. Just by doing so a secret feeling of pride may arise in our hearts as we reflect that at least our penitence is a work pleasing to God. And if we compare ourselves with others who are better than we, in that too the impulse to self-assertion may be concealed: we would like to be as others are and to feel distinguished and important.

The tax collector has nothing of all this. He looks not on others but on himself alone: "God be merciful to me a sinner!" He knows and confesses that in the presence of God he can raise no claim and must cast himself utterly on the divine mercy. He would like to enjoy some favour with God: "God be merciful to me a sinner!" But he has completely surrendered that claimfulness which supposes that by our own achievements we can acquire merit with God. It is just this which is the fundamental sin: to presume by one's own strength to assert a claim before God. In the presence of God no man can be worth anything on the ground of his own merit; none can boast before God. Such boasting infringes the honour of God as Creator. "What have you that you did not receive? If then you received it, why do you boast as if it were not a gift?" (I Cor. 4: 7) In his claimfulness man forgets that he is God's creature. And from this point of view it is a matter of indifference whether he speaks of God as does the Pharisee or whether he ceases to speak of God. Both attitudes are sinful; both show man's self-assertive claims in the face of God; this is sin and self-deception.

The reason why so many people resist the idea of allowing God to become the controlling reality in their lives, the reason why they are averse to acceptance of the Christian message, is that they feel it would mean surrendering man's claim to be his own master. They prefer to live in self-deception rather than by grace, to live by meritorious achievements rather than by the free gift of God.

Even on the plane of human intercourse some people feel it to be very difficult to accept gifts. Of course to receive the gift of something which one particularly desires and then to possess it freely as one's own-that is agreeable to all men. But to receive a gift in the fullest sense means not simply to receive the gift as an object merely, but to be indebted chiefly to the kindness of the giver, with the result that one enters into a relation of dependence on the giver. Naturally if this means an outward dependence, an outward obligation, we are justified in feeling the situation to be repugnant. But a gift which is given with the intention of committing the recipient to a sense of material obligation is not a true gift. It is right to refuse to feel dependent on the calculations of the other party; but it is not right to resist the sense of dependence on his kindness.

True giving binds two people together in perfect freedom without implying any material obligation of the one party towards the other. The acceptance of gifts of this kind requires open-heartedness, glad and spontaneous sub-mission to the kindness of the giver. And it is just this attitude which so many people cannot bring themselves to adopt. They suppose that it means yielding ground too much and they shut themselves up in an attitude of hard inaccessibility, from the sheer need to assert their power and authority. They do not know the happiness that lies precisely in receiving gifts and thereby winning favour. This is especially clear from the fact that so many people resist receiving from others one of the greatest of gifts which a man can bestow on his neighbour: namely, forgiveness. For in order that this may come about a man must confess his wrong to another, and this injures his pride and sense of deserving. And yet our whole life together as a community wins freedom and happiness just from the fact that one is prepared to confess his sin to another and to receive forgiveness.

In just the same way men spoil their relation to God because they are not prepared to confess what they truly are in the sight of God: worthless creatures who have nothing which they did not receive from Him, creatures whose lives are without any content or meaning apart from His grace, and who must flee again and again to His grace in order to have any value, creatures who can receive trom Him alone that meaningfulness which frees them from the tense struggle to assert their value.

"God be merciful to me a sinner!" cries the tax collector. And Jesus adds: "I tell you this man went down to his house justified rather than the other." It is just the confession of sin which frees man from the fetters of self-from himself as he is in his urge to assert his authority and in his pathetic selt-deception; and it brings him back to his true self—as he receives himself from God's hand: as the justified man, who in his spiritual freedom does not need to lower his eyes before any man since he has lowered them before God. For such a man the world too, and human society, is transfigured. Instead of an unscrupulous desire to press one's claims at all costs, we have the spirit of sincere, honest and self-forgetful service. Instead of envious ill-will, and ruthlessness, we have a cheerful spirit of mutual regard and esteem. Instead of reserve and tense self-concern, we have friendliness, frankness and joy in fellowship with others. If our parable persuades anyone to adopt this new way of life, on such a one Jesus has exercised His liberating redemptive power.

## Revelation 3:14–20

*December 8, 1940*

"And to the angel of the church in Laodicea write: "The words of the Amen, the faithful and true witness, the beginning of God's creation.

"'I know your works; you are neither cold nor hot. Would that you were cold or hot! So, because you are lukewarm, and neither cold nor hot, I will spew you out of my mouth. For you say, I am rich, I have prospered, and I need nothing; not knowing that you are wretched, pitiable, poor, blind, and naked. Therefore I counsel you to buy from me gold refined by fire, that you may be rich, and white garments to clothe you and to keep the shame of your nakedness from being seen, and salve to anoint your eyes, that you may see. Those whom I love, I reprove and chasten; so be zealous and repent. Behold, I stand at the door and knock; if any one hears my voice and opens the door, I will come in to him and eat with him, and he with me.'"

THe season of Advent proclaims the coming of Christ to judge the world, and on the second Sunday in Advent, according to ancient Church use, we are to think of the theme of divine judgment. The solemn summons of the

, also invites text: "Behold, I stand at the door and knock" us to such thoughts. It admonishes us to be prepared for the coming of the Lord, and the preceding verses suggest how we should acquire this spirit of vigilant readiness. They are introduced by the remarkable word: "The words of the Amen, the faithful and true witness, the beginning of God's creation." This means that the words with which we are challenged, John the seer has heard in the spirit as the words of Christ, the words of the "Amen"

'. Amen signifies validity: what is unshakably true. Hence we are to hear these words as the solid abidingly true message of Him in whom God Himself became for us the incarnate Word, a living and true summons.

What do they tell us? They form part of the seven letters addressed to the seven Christian Churches in Asia Minor in a time of persecution, and they are to be heard by the whole Church. In ever fresh variations they repeat the summons: "I come, I stand at the door!" It is a rousing, soul-stirring summons, and its meaning may be summarized as: "This world is not the sole or ultimate reality."

This our world has its hidden background which we are accustomed to forget. And from that hidden beyond, there can and there will one day break forth into this world a power for judgment or salvation, according to the quality of our preparedness and vigilance. We are not, as we fondly suppose, in sole control of this world but the world and all our activities within it will be brought to account by its Creator and Lord. Remember what confronts all men with imminent judgment! This our world, so familiar and homely as we think, is not after all our true home: we shall be torn from it and it will sink into nothingness for us on the day when we must die. Be mindful of your last day! Our life which we suppose to be our own does not lie within our disposal, has not the unequivocal goal which we assume for it, nor the completeness of meaning which we would like to lend it. A higher hand governs its affairs, unseen, and may at any moment intrude with a painful thrust. Be mindful of the truth that you do not belong to yourselves!

### I

"Behold, I stand at the door and knock." Who is this "I" who stands at the door and knocks? It is eternity! In what state will it find us?

The first warning message is very singular: "I know your works; you are neither cold nor hot. Would that you were cold or hot! So, because you are lukewarm, and neither cold nor hot, I will spew you out of my mouth."

Who is being addressed here? Who is being aimed at? The undecided man! Is indecision then the worst thing of all? Worse for example than decision for evil rather than for good? In order to understand this we must remember that what is in view here is not any worldly or political issue but the ultimate issue, that of eternity, that of God. And here in the decisive question of God it is always better to decide against God than to be poised indecisively in a middle term. For the struggle against God implies always a certain degree of connexion with God; every struggle against God is a wrestling with God, as the example of one of the most passionate fighters against God, Friedrich Nietzsche, can show.

It must of course be a genuine and honest struggle against God. A struggle which understands the question of God only in theoretical terms or only as a political issue is not that. Such an attitude is in fact an ignoring, a silencing of the profound and searching question of the reality of God. And such a fighter is the half-hearted indecisive person for whom the question of the reality of God in all its seriousness and depth has not yet become vivid. But the passionate fighter against God stands near to God and his ear is open to the voice of eternity. Mere talk about God and religious belief does not of course bring us near to the living majesty of God. A man may talk a great deal about God and yet belong to the category of these half-hearted persons; for the search for God is only genuine when it stirs and masters man's mind as a passionate concern. Are we the kind of men for whom the question of God is a passionate concern? Or have we squandered our inner strength on other questions?

"Behold, I stand at the door and knock." How will it be when the Eternal opens the door? Whom will He find? Alas, the most terrible thing of all is not that He should find a foe in our breast, but if He finds no one there, if our souls are hollow and void! For the most frightful danger we incur is that we lose our souls in preoccupation with our duties and cares, our joys and sorrows, and that in our innermost selves we cease to be anything that has meaning; if in this situation eternity knocks at our door, it will find no one there! This danger of losing our souls through preoccupation and distraction threatens all of us. It is the danger that in spite of all our faithful devotion to our duties, our personal selves become dissipated through the pressure of our cares and anxieties, that our being is consumed away and sucked dry by the obsession of our work. Or again we might lose our souls through those distractions in which we relax from our work. We in fact run this risk even when we appreciate and foster spiritual values such as enrich our lives, for example the world of truth and beauty. May not all this be something which, in spite of the interest and enjoyment it brings, in the last resort signifies nothing decisive for our inner life?

But we are to be something of value in ourselves; from all that engages our attention and interest, from duty and work, from care and sorrow, from our relaxations and pleasures, we must return to ourselves and collect and concentrate our inner spiritual life. Are we something of value intrinsically? May not this question have occurred to us at times when we lay sick in bed? There are many men who, when sickness or old age makes them incapable of work, feel that they are faced as it were with emptiness, and begin to wonder for what purpose they exist at all. Have such people really lived or have they lost their true life in preoccupation with their work and business? Do we imagine that the real worth of a man lies in his actual performance, in what he has produced considered apart from what he is in himself, and not in what he is as a human being, not in the love which streams from him to brighten our lives? As though a life which is quite incapable of producing anything and which from the point of view of objective production is a burden to the community, might not in truth be a secret source of blessing to the community!

Or perhaps the inner void of our lives has become painfully apparent to us at some moment when we have been called upon to help and comfort another whose life was shadowed by inner personal troubles and temptations? How can we help others when we are nothing in ourselves? Only the man who is intrinsically something of value is capable of fellowship with others, of friendship and love.

People whose inner life is empty may of course very easily fit into an organization, but they are incapable of genuine love and fellowship. The sign of our being something of value within is our capacity for receiving and giving love. A poet, Wilhelm Busch, with his vein of playful fantasy, describes with somewhat grim humour a dream which transported him into mathematical worlds. There he meets, instead of human beings, mathematical figures who, inwardly hollow, yet live a ghostly life like that of men. Thus with mocking irony he holds up a mirror to our human world, challenging men to reflect and reconsider. Is not our life as a whole threatened by the danger of being more and more absorbed in the organization, instead of being part of a genuine community of true persons who mean something intrinsically and so also can signify something for other people? Modern life increasingly crowds out times of pause for self-communion, for silent meditative heart-searching in face of eternity. Many people are overcome by boredom when the rush of activity ceases, whether it be that of work or that of pleasure; and few men can any longer bear to be alone. Solitude seems to them tedious. And is it worse in the eyes of God to steal or to transgress a moral commandment, than to grow bored? Yes, there are people who dread being alone and take refuge in a round of activities which give them no time for thought. They escape from themselves in sheer noise which stupefies their ability to think.

### II

But how do we come to ourselves? How do we become our true selves? When we listen to the voice of eternity: "Behold, I stand at the door and knock." Eternity stands at the door of our souls with its summons. It asks us whether we are a true person or whether we have lost our souls in our pre-occupation with the things of time. Is this a fantasy which we should reject as making us weakly incapable of attending to the demands of the moment, of getting on with the task in hand, of plucking the fruit which time offers? A fantasy blinding us to the actualities of life and the world in which we live? Or is not the truth rather the reverse; that the thought of eternity alone makes us clear-sighted? "For you say, I am rich and I need nothing; not knowing that you are wretched, pitiable, poor, blind, and naked. Therefore I counsel you to buy from me gold refined by fire, that you may be rich, and white garments to clothe you and to keep the shame of your nakedness from being seen, and salve to anoint your eyes, that you may see."

Are we not in reality blind if we regard this world as a securely grounded home, forgetting its homelessness; if we do not pay heed to those times which draw our attention to eternity, the hidden "beyond" which transcends this world?

How often are we involved in the blindness of pride! Eternity knocks at our door, yet we do not hear it, because we suppose that we have subsumed eternity in our life in this world and that it lies at our disposition—whether it be that we talk of eternal values which lend the radiance of eternity to our lives, whether it be that like the builders of the tower of Babel we wish to erect a tower reaching to heaven, that we dream of creating works of abiding value. For us men eternity is not a possession but a bar of judgment; it spells the end of our planning, organizing and creating. Do our works give eternal meaning to our life? even though the monuments we erect were to last to the remotest conceivable times and not rather fall long before into ruins? Is then eternity endlessly protracted time? In any case such eternity would not be ours, for we are not endless.

How often are we involved in the blindness of superficiality and frivolity! Is it not the case that in general we count on the continuance of the normal routine of life and on its basis make our plans for the future? And yet disappointment and pain are spared no one. Yet we go on living as though disappointment and pain were the exception, while the normal course of things is the ground on which we build in our plans for success. But might it not be the case that what we call the normal, that the world with which we reckon in our projects, is really only the superficial? May it not be true that what we consider the abnormal and disturbing, what, we suppose, really ought not to be, is precisely the kind of crisis in which we catch a glimpse of true and essential being, of the innermost reality of life?

In the life of every man eternity finally intrudes in the form of death. Death stands at the door and knocks some time or other for all of us. The popular legend speaks with deep truth about the messengers of death whom the latter sends in advance to warn people of its imminent approach. These messengers are pain and sorrow, old age and other tokens of our transience. Shall we frivolously disregard them? Or shall we pay heed to them and prepare ourselves for the coming of eternity? Is it not the messengers of death, of eternity, which bring us back to ourselves? Especially is sorrow meant to bring us to ourselves and to enable us to receive that gold which is refined with fire. "Those whom I love, I reprove and chasten; so be zealous and repent."

Yet it would be wrong to think that only pain and the signs of our transitoriness were the messengers of eternity. There are indeed other messengers and with the blindness of our ingratitude we fail to notice them. They are all the pleasing and cheering things which are sent to us to give us joy. It may be every beam of morning sunlight, every flower which delights us with its beauty and fragrance, every look of love, every kind gift which comes our way. Of course all these things have to be rightly understood and rightly received if they are to be for us messengers of eternity. If ungratefully we take all this happiness and joy for granted as something which we deserve, then we do not understand the message of these things, which is that what gives purest joy and deepest happiness to our lives is always a wondrous gift, a sheer marvel.

If we fail to open our hearts to these small occasions of happiness, or if we allow the moments or hours of blessing to fade away without response, without really taking them to ourselves by an impulse of grateful acceptance, then they lose their meaning for us and our souls remain empty. If on the other hand we greet them thankfully as messengers of eternity, and give them a place in our hearts, then we gain new strength to bring happiness and joy to others. And in so far as we see that we can be a source of blessing to others, we may justifiably feel that we are something of value in ourselves. It then becomes clear to us that it is not what we have planned and intentionally done which confers the richest content and meaning on our lives, but rather what we have gratefully received and faithfully preserved as a gift, and that the greatest of these gifts bestowed on us is love. We come to see that in the fellowship of love and trust, whose secret network mysteriously interpenetrates the world of the tangible and of purposeful organization, our true essential life is rooted.

"Behold, I stand at the door and knock." This finally applies to the word of Holy Scripture, which summons us to vigilance and readiness. This word is not faded or remote; old as it is, it speaks to us ever anew. It strikes home to the heart of our daily life as the word which sounds from another world. It vibrates essentially in the very sound of our church bells, so different from all the other sounds which beat upon our ears—an alien mysterious sound at the heart of our so familiar world. And it would be a sad day for us if ever the music of church bells were to become silent in our villages and towns. For it is this music which calls us away from the world of visible things, the world of our scheming and talking, inviting us to gather ourselves together to hear the word of God which resounds from the invisible world of eternity: "Behold, I stand at the door and knock; if any one hears my voice and opens the door, I will come in to him and eat with him, and he with me." Who is this "I" which calls us thus? It is eternity—the eternity which addresses us also in the messengers it sends. Yet here it speaks with a clearer voice, lending to those other messages their full meaning. For here God speaks to us through His word, in which He has fully disclosed His being to us, and through

His Son whom He has sent into the world that in Him we may apprehend the full manifestation of divine love.

"Behold, I stand at the door and knock." Once more in the season of Advent the summoning message rings out, admonishing us to look with concentrated gaze on Him who is the very image of divine love, and who is also in consequence-and the second Sunday in Advent teaches us to think of this—the Judge of the whole world. For so it is: the depths of divine love are opened out only to him who allows himself to be emancipated from his attachment to the things of time and space and who allows Christ to effect this redemptive work of emancipation in his life. May we prepare and dispose our hearts for this work of divine redemption! "Bewildered I stood in the world of my night.

Yet Thou comest! Because I exist, Thou art born. From error and gloom set my face to the light

Of Thyself, Word incarnate, Thou Lord of the morn!"1

> **[1]** R. A. Schröder.

## St. Luke 14:16–24

*June 22, 1941*

"A man once gave a great banquet, and invited many; and at the time for the banquet he sent his servant to say to those who had been invited, 'Come; for all is now ready' But they all alike began to make excuses. The first said to him, 'I have bought a field, and I must go out and see it; I pray you, have me excused.' And another said, 'I have bought five yoke of oxen, and I go to examine them; I pray you, have me excused.' And another said, 'I have married a wife, and therefore I cannot come.' So the servant came and reported this to his master. Then the householder in anger said to his servant, 'Go out quickly to the streets and lanes of the city, and bring in the poor and maimed and blind and lame.' And the servant said, 'Sir, what you commanded has been done, and still there is room.? And the master said to the servant, 'Go out to the highways and hedges, and compel people to come in, that my house may be filled. For I tell you, none of those men who were invited shall taste my banquet.? " My friends, we are all deeply shaken and disturbed by the news which we have heard this morning. Events have taken a critical new turn and we are now at war with Russia also. It was with divided feelings no doubt that we sang our introductory hymn:

> "Hallelujah, Sabbath morn ..•  
> To-day my anxious cares depart."

For however much we take pains to forget or to surrender to God our earthly sorrows when we are assembled here in the house of God, can we forget the heavy burden of anxiety which lies on all our hearts to-day?

Had I known beforehand in what circumstances I would have to preach to-day, I would have needed a long time for reflection on the choice of a text, a text that would contain an appropriate message for this hour of crisis. As it is, I am relieved of the burden of such a choice; I had chosen the text suggested by the traditional gospel for this Sunday, and perhaps it will be found to contain precisely the right message for our present situation. Let us try to reflect in calmness and quiet on the message it has for us.

In order rightly to understand the story which Jesus narrates in our text, we must remember a custom which existed in the country and at the time of Jesus. When a man of means proposed to give a grand banquet, he first issued a preliminary invitation, without specifying the day and the hour of its taking place. Thus the guests were warned that they should hold themselves in readiness for an occasion in the near future. Then, when the day came, a second invitation was issued: "The day is here; come, for everything is prepared!"

In accordance with this custom, the rich man of whom our story tells had invited many guests and of course they had accepted his invitation; for who would want to miss such a banquet? But they had accepted somewhat casually; for, as we see, when the time came they were not ready. They did not organize their time and business with a view to the fact that shortly the host's messenger would come and invite them for the particular day, but they went on with their affairs and daily pursuits as though the final invitation to the feast were not shortly to be expected; in fact they probably forgot the invitation altogether. When therefore the messenger comes, the attention of all of them is claimed by things which for the moment seem more important than the banquet. One has bought a field and must go out to consider how to exploit it best; another has bought oxen and must go and look after them; the third has married a wife and therefore is prevented. The remainder who are not expressly mentioned have one thing or another which precludes their coming. In short, none of those who were bidden have now time to come; none comes to the banquet.

No wonder that the host becomes angry, saying to himself: "All right; let them stay away! there will be other people who will be glad to come." So he sends his messenger out again and has brought in from the streets and the lanes of the city the poor and the maimed who otherwise would have to beg their scanty bread; and in fact, since there is still room, he goes so far as to compel the tramps to come in from the highways and hedges.

### I

The story is a parable. What instruction does it convey? One thing is quite clear: the rich man's invitation symbolizes the call of God which goes forth to invite men to enter His kingdom. The people invited-both those who were first invited as also those who were called in later—are the men to whom the call of God comes. And the hearers of the parable are asked whether it will be with them as with those who were first invited, who, because they did not hold themselves in readiness, missed the joy of the banquet. The tone of the parable is obviously not consoling and cheering. It is not that the teller is primarily thinking of those who were not originally invited and later were in fact summoned to share in the joy of the feast. The tone is rather solemn and threatening, as the conclusion plainly shows: "I tell you, none of those men who were invited shall taste my banquet."

It is easy to understand the parable in relation to Jesus and His time. The original guests are the pious members of the Jewish people, to whom Jesus' call to penitence was so often directed, and against whom His anger was so often kindled. These were men who for long had had special knowledge of God and His call; who for long had said "yes, yes!" and who now when Jesus comes with the proclamation that the time is fulfilled close their ears and harden their hearts against Him, and from whom He turns away to go to "tax collectors and sinners" who were willing to listen to His voice.

But if Jesus is speaking to us to-day through this parable, then He is summoning us also to consider whether we do not belong to the category of the original guests, who have long accepted God's call and God's invitation but who, when the matter becomes serious, calling for decision in the present moment, prefer to be absorbed in private affairs and to despise God's summons.

We can hardly fail to think of our nation as a whole. To think of how long ago it first heard God's call and invitation when the gospel was first preached to our forefathers, the old Germanic tribes; and how again it was struck by the call of God when in the sixteenth century the preaching of Luther caused the original message of the gospel to ring out anew. We cannot help thinking of the days when Germany was a country dominated by the Church. Is not this shown by the churches which belong to the whole picture of our villages, by the minsters and cathedrals which tower up in our towns and cities? Is it not shown by the Christian teaching which moulded the minds of our youth in school and at church? Is it not shown by church customs and festivals which were interwoven in the very texture of our nation's life, by holy baptism and holy marriage and church burial?

Our churches still belong essentially to the picture pre-sented by our towns and villages; and still on Sundays peals of bells ring out over houses and meadows. I do not propose to enlarge on the fact that already there are villages the focal point of whose life is no longer the church. But I must ask: how many churches are filled Sunday by Sunday with devout hearers of the Word? How many of our compatriots still allow themselves to be summoned by the bells to enter the house of God? For how many are the great cathedrals of our cities no longer the house of God where the word of God speaks to them and finds them, but simply artistic monuments, an enduring product of the German mind and civilization? What has happened to the Christian education of youth? How many people still feel themselves seriously bound by church discipline and obligation? We all know that Germany is no longer a Christian country; that church life is now only a surviving remnant, and that many desire and hope that even this remnant will shortly disappear.

What is the reason for all this? Is it not that for so many of us the affairs of daily life are normally more important than the call of God? That our striving to make money, to gain power, to enjoy the pleasures of the world, has been a more powerful factor in our lives than the question: am I in a state of readiness for encounter with God and His summons? That our delight in technical progress, in sporting achievements and so on, has been greater than our joy in hearing the word of God? In short, is it not the case that we are no longer ready to hear God's call and demand upon our lives?

### II

Someone, however, may be asking the question: "Yes, but for what exactly should we be prepared? When did the second invitation come to us: 'Come, for all is now ready'— has it not been lacking?" This question leads us to remember that whenever a nation as a whole turns away from the Church, it is always individuals who are guilty of the defection. It is the individual to whom the second invitation is issued, the call: "Come, for all is now ready." And it comes in fact just when he is not expecting it, when it is least convenient, with the consequence that he prefers to close his ears to it.

Just as at the time of Jesus it was His appearance itself which embodied God's call-inconvenient and for the mass of the people lost in the wind—so since those days, His call has sounded everywhere and time and time again, as Jesus Himself confronts us everywhere and time and time again. Where does He meet us? Where have we failed to perceive His presence or to hear His call? Jesus Himself has anticipated this question. To those who have failed to apprehend His presence in life, the following words apply:

"I was hungry and you gave me no food, I was thirsty and you gave me no drink, I was a stranger and you did not welcome me, naked and you did not clothe me, sick and in prison and you did not visit me. Truly I say to you, as you did it not to the least of these, you did it not to me" (Matt.

> **[25]** 42, 43, 45)•  
> Hence, time and time again, wherever the distress and  
> need of our brother require our compassion and helpfulness,  
> Jesus Himself meets us and His call to us is sounded. Here  
> again we must think of certain developments in the history

of our nation. Certainly the Church has done what it could to help in the social distresses of our time, especially in the work of the "home mission" field. But it is quite clear that if all who professed and called themselves Christians had been perceptive and sensitive to these social troubles, to the problems arising from industry, to the housing shortages, etc.; if readiness for self-sacrifice, eagerness in renunciation had been alive among us Christians, then contempt and hatred for the Church would not have grown to terrifying proportions as has in fact been the case. Nor would it have been possible to come to the conclusion that Christianity has counted for little or nothing in regard to the amelioration of conditions of life.

But we must think not only of material needs, but also of the inner spiritual needs of our people. And in this respect the Church itself comes under criticism. Had it had more understanding for the spiritual and intellectual life of our time, and especially for the interests and problems which have stirred the minds of our growing youth; had it better understood how to proclaim the Word of God in such a way as to make it readily intelligible and to bring it vividly to bear on the burning questions and struggles of the time in which we live, and not, as so often has been the case, to preach it unintelligibly to modern people and in such a way as to make it sound remote and alien from modern interests; then the indifference to the Church would not have assumed the alarming proportions which it has done.

But we will not linger in consideration of the past and its failures. What we have said deploringly and reproachfully about past mistakes must be for us a rousing summons in the present, so that to-day our eyes may be opened and our hands put to the plough, so that in the decisive present we hear God's call, each one in his own place. And we must make a beginning by asking ourselves the question where in our daily life we are struck by God's demand. If we think only of great needs we easily overlook the individual and small distresses which are the very thing that cries out for our help. And all great needs in the last analysis grow out of smaller needs, or rather are the consequence of the fact that in our blindness we have been insensitive to these smaller distresses.

I cannot myself say in detail-nor is it my duty to do so— where and at what point God's summons hits the individual, you and me, in the course of our daily life, in the routine of our work and our many social contacts. For it is the mystery of Jesus' encounter with us that He meets us disguised in ever new forms; it is the mystery of God's call that it comes to us always clad in a different shape, that it sounds ever new, and in circumstances where we least expected it. Hence I can give only a general warning that each of us should be prepared and vigilant and alert both to hear the summons and to obey it.

The popular legend of the poor and the rich man, which no doubt is familiar to all of us, has as its basic presupposition that God's meeting with man often takes unexpected forms and that if we fail in vigilance we overlook it to our hurt. The legend relates how God once walked the earth as a simple wayfarer, and sought a shelter for the night. He knocked at the door of a rich man and asked for sleeping accommodation. The rich man looked at the unpromising wayfarer who stood at his door, and whose appearance did not exactly suggest that He would be able to pay well, and on various pretexts he refused Him shelter. It was just not convenient. God then knocked at the door of the poor man's house, where He found a friendly reception. As the tale goes on to say, the rich man had punished himself, whereas the poor man was granted a rich blessing.

Yes, friendliness and kindheartedness, patience and the will to sacrifice oneself are essential to the vigilance that is required of us; perceptiveness about what the present occasion demands. God comes to us, Jesus comes to us in disguise; and if we have failed to recognize the encounter, then the moment has irrevocably fled and we have missed the blessing with which it was rich. Hence we should insert into our restless and often so harassed life hours of silent reflection in order to search our hearts and think: what have

I neglected? Who is looking to me for help? Who longs to hear a kind word from me? We should not let our attention be dissipated in the noise and bustle of the day, in the cares of our work, in our joys and sorrows. We should not forget to notice what God now and then wishes to say to us!

To be sure, God's call is as a rule very inconvenient to us. When we are called to help anyone it rarely fits in with our personal situation. We have no time, our work is so pressing! We are constantly expected to give out and we have not enough ourselves. It is true that there is so much distress that we cannot help everybody. But have we always done as much as we could? Have we at least always had a friendly glance, a kind word when we were approached? Or if anyone wants anything from us, do we always feel it to be a horrid disturbance?

But it is not merely the need of our fellow men in which God's call to us is heard. It may also be our own need, our own fate, which strikes us down and makes us shiver with dismay. When our lives go according to our plans and wishes, when life brings attractive work for our powers, and when our work is crowned with success and joy, then our world seems to be irradiated as from within by its own light. In such circumstances it is easy for us to forget that the light which illuminates our path really falls on our world from without, that it is the light of the goodness of God, that it streams upon us as sunlight on the earth. And just as the sun can be hidden behind clouds as the sun sets, so also our world gladdened by congenial tasks and the pride of success may once again be plunged in darkness.

When this happens our world can grow sinister and un-canny, and the activities of all men including our own begin to seem strange and purposeless. If such a fate strikes us, in one way or another, then it is God who is confronting us anew. But shall we be able to recognize Him in this mysterious darkness? Shall we hear His call in the terrible silence of solitude? Or shall we be plunged in despair, or in dull

15° indifference, and so miss the blessing of our experience? "Through the darkness and mystery of their pain God draws men unto Himself"

, writes Agnes Günther. We ought to remember how uncertain are the brilliance and good fortune of our lives, and in days of sunny prosperity we should be prepared for God's call which summons us in the "dark night of the soul" unto Himself.

And have we not to some extent already experienced how this call of God, extremely inconvenient and disturbing as it was, nevertheless became a blessing for us? Have we not known how, just by being torn out of a life of custom and routine, we have been preserved from inner torpidity and indolence? That our life thus received a new impulse and a new depth (whether through our own pain or through our perception of another's sorrow) so that then for the first time we realized what it meant to live? Have we not felt in such circumstances that we enjoy more deeply and gratefully the happiness that was given us to enjoy, and that we found a new and closer link with our families? That in days of dark distress we became more patient, and from hard times were permitted to reap a harvest of blessing? How easy it is for us to become buried in the sameness of the days; how easily we are deadened by the routine of life, so that our personal life and its spiritual condition grows to be a matter of indifference to us! Or again, how easily we get agitated by things which are not worth such excitement! God's call, extremely disturbing and inconvenient as it is, shakes us up and causes us to throw off our lethargy, restoring to us our true life and enabling us once more to view things in their true perspective.

To be vigilantly prepared for the moment which is always the decisive moment! The Christian faith of course is no calm and static possession. It is not simply the conviction of the truth of certain doctrines which can once for all be assimilated. The Christian faith consists in a certain direction of the will. It is only alive in us if its reality is ever anew proved and tested in the process of living. It is not sufficient for us to have once decided to have faith in God, but our decision for God has to be consummated ever afresh when we are confronted by Him in the actualities of living and His call strikes us. Again and again it is the decisive now on which everything turns. By His living encounters with us God puts us to the test. The heart of the matter is to hold ourselves in readiness for His call, which requires us to be inwardly detached from all that claims our attention, from work and care, from sorrow and joy, and to see that nothing claims and absorbs us wholly, with the consequence that we become blind to His confrontation and deaf to His call.

CIV

This brings us to the main point. To be ready for God's call means just this—to be prepared to live in the conviction that our life of earthly work with its cares and projects, its sorrows and joys, is not the ultimate reality. What then is the essence of God's invitation? It is surely the call to a higher life, to a future life, to a life that lies beyond this world. It is the call to free ourselves from this world and to become centred in God's world

No doubt when things are going badly with us here, when we feel acutely the oppressiveness of our wretched and distressing fate, we long to soar beyond it all and we eagerly listen to this call which invites us to another world where there is no more pain and sorrow. But God's call demands more than this. It demands that in good as in bad times we should be deeply aware that this our world can give us no final satisfaction, can bestow on us no true fullness of life. God's call faces us with the decision between our world and His world, between the present and the future, between the here and now and the beyond. Do we really want the reality of God or do we in fact want our world and God only occasionally for the purpose of transfiguring and enhancing our world? Is it the fact that as far as we are concerned, what we plan and do here, the structures of life which we carve out and build with all the trouble and care involved, what causes us sorrow or joy, is the supreme and final thing? The thing for which we live and die? Or do we know a higher life? Are we prepared to take seriously the thought of eternity, and to engage in our activities in the light of eternity, to place them under the judgment of eternity?

Yes, do we know any higher life? Do we know anything of the future, of the transcendent? If we are honest we must admit that we have no direct experience of it, and we know nothing about it. What we do know, and what we have immediate experience of, is ever and only our life up to the present; our lite here below, the lite of our earthly destiny, the life of our plans and projects. But to reckon with eternity means just this: to view our familiar life as a provisional, unfulfilled and unfulfillable life, and to live it in preparation for a future fulfilled and true life which God wills to bestow on us. If we were to try to picture the future to ourselves, we should only fill it out with the images suggested by our wishes and dreams. Now it is just these fantasies which we are to surrender. To be ready for God's future means to walk trustfully into the darkness, ready for the purposes which God in His wisdom designs us to fulfil.

This future which flows from God does not however meet us only at the end of our days, but he who listens to the divine call may now already and ever afresh experience the way in which God makes him purer and stronger, more serene and cheerful, how God fulfils the prayer: "Make me pure, tender-hearted, detached from the things of sense, calm and gentle, poised in the peace which passes under-standing!" The man who has cultivated this preparedness for God's call in the course of his earthly life will also find himself prepared for the final call of God summoning him out of this our familiar world into the darkness of the un-known. The fact that this final summons will at some time or other be sounded for us, forms the greatest disturbance of all. How few men there are for whom this last call of God is not the most unwelcome of their whole experience! For how few does it come at the right time! And who would not, if only he could, beg for postponement, so that he might put one thing or another in order? But of course when this last call comes no excuse can be admitted, and it cannot be overlooked. Then the question for us is whether it is the call of God who summons us thus into His presence, or whether it is the sinister call of fate plunging us into nothingness. Then the decisive issue for us will be whether we are ready and whether in the course of our earthly life we have learnt to be ready for the challenges of God.

We have not spoken specifically about the fate which at the present time has overtaken us, and the terrifying and tragic nature, of which we are painfully conscious, of this very day. Is it necessary to speak of it specially now? Whoever has understood all that is implied in the demands of Christian vigilance and preparedness, about which our parable warns us, will also realize what these demands imply just now above all. No doubt even without meditation on this parable we shall all know that we must hold ourselves ready to meet whatever the future may bring by our readiness for action and self-sacrifice, and by calm bravery. Beyond all this, however, our parable warns us to be pre-pared for what God wishes to say to us in the events which loom ahead. It teaches us to seek the firm ground on which alone we can find genuine peace and the inspiration for courage, on which we can win an inner detachment from everything that our earthly future may bring of good and ill, victories and sacrifices-that inner detachment which we win when we are ready for God's future, and cling to Him in calm trust.

## St. Luke 5:1–10

*July 13, I94I*

While the people pressed upon him to hear the word of God, he was standing by the lake of Gennesaret. And he saw two boats by the lake; but the fishermen had gone out of them and were washing their nets. Getting into one of the boats, which was Simon's, he asked him to put out a little from the land. And he sat down and taught the people from the boat. And when he had ceased speaking, he said to Simon, "Put out into the deep and let down your nets for a catch." And Simon answered, "Master, we toiled all night and took nothing! But at your word I will let down the nets." And when they had done this, they enclosed a great shoal of fish; and as their nets were breaking, they beckoned to their partners in the other boat to come and help them. And they came and filled both the boats, so that they began to sink. But when Simon Peter saw it, he fell down at Jesus' knees, saying, "Depart from me, for I am a sinful man, O Lord." For he was astonished, and all that were with him, at the catch of fish which they had taken; and so also were James and John, sons of Zebedee, who were partners with Simon. And Jesus said to Simon, "Do not be afraid; henceforth you will be catching men."

### I

THis story of Peter's catch of fish narrates a miracle and faces us with the question: what are the meaning and implications of the Christian belief in miracle? Is it an integral part of the Christian faith to believe in miracles? Are we as Christians committed to believing that the miracle stories of the New Testament and hence also this story narrate true occurrences?

We live of course in a time which not only does not normally experience such miracles, but does not even consider them possible. We are not only theoretically convinced that all happenings in the world take place according to certain norms, according to unalterable laws, but in practice too, in our work and affairs, we behave on the assumption that everything that happens has its natural cause and natural effect. In fact we could not carry on with our work, nor undertake responsibility for our doings, if it were otherwise, if we had to be prepared for the possibility that the continuity of cause and effect might suddenly and miraculously be broken. Naturally we are aware also that we have not always a comprehensive knowledge of the full scope of this continuity and cannot always securely calculate the effects of our deeds. We are aware that there are secret forces in the world of nature which we do not yet know and understand. That, however, does not mean that we believe in miracles; it means only that we reckon with the (as yet) unknown and uninvestigated. And science 1s constantly working at the investigation of the spheres of nature as yet unfathomed, at making known what is up to now unknown in order that our thinking and planning should be ever more securely based, that the world should pass ever more fully within our control. This in fact is something which can never be completely attained. New mysteries will always arise. And in the sphere of our human life we shall always have to be pre-pared for the unexpected and unusual; we might indeed call such things miraculous. But it is not yet real belief in miracle.

Hence it is not surprising that sceptical criticism has been expressed with regard to the New Testament miracle stories, ever since the modern age of scientific knowledge and world-controlling technology came into being. And it is not surprising that not merely many people who have turned away from the Christian faith, but also many whose earnest desire it was to take Christ seriously, have declared the New Testament miracles to be incredible. Certainly, they say, many of the miracles related in the New Testament may rest on a basis of fact; but they were certainly not real miracles in which natural sequences were interrupted, but events which can be explained naturally. The healings of the sick which Jesus effected may be understood to be the result of the influence and the powerful operation exercised by His personality on those who were thus healed. There are other miracle stories of course, the incidence of which cannot be explained with reference to natural causes (among which the story narrated by our text belongs) and which they therefore consider to be pure legend or pious invention.

Now there are other Christians who think that this outlook stands in irreconcilable opposition to the Christian faith and who deny that those who hold it can be called Christians at all. And there are others again who find the contradiction oppressive which exists between their scientific convictions and the principles which guide them in their daily lives, on the one hand, and on the other the miracle stories of the New Testament; who would by all means like to adhere to the Christian faith, but who suppose a Christian is obliged to consider these stories to be true. Since they cannot do that, they fall into doubt as to whether they may still legitimately claim to be Christians in any sense.

What are we to say to all this? In the first place, that belief in the miracle stories of the New Testament is not in fact the essence of Christian faith. Christian faith means rather faith in the grace of God which has been manifested in Christ. As Luther has already said, the real work of Christ consists in His victory over the law and over death. And to believe in Christ means to believe in Him as the One who frees us from the tyranny of the law and from the reign of death; but it does not mean considering the miracle stories of the New Testament to be true.

And what are we to say to those who retort: No! Belief in the miracles of the New Testament is also an integral part of the Christian faith. It is just in such assent to the truth of these stories that Christian faith is evidenced. For Christian faith means surrendering not only one's self-will but also the whole domain of one's thinking; it means not setting up one's own thoughts against the authority of God's word. If in the life of faith we are called to sacrifice to God all that we are and have, we must also sacrifice to Him our life on the intellectual plane. And if these miraculous happenings are impossible according to the mode of our present-day thinking, then the latter is simply false and must be given up.

What are we to answer? In the first place this: you must remember that in regard to those who are unable to do what you require it is not a question of evil will but simply a question of truthfulness. Now even though a man were in error, strict truthfulness is something which can never be separated from God and from Christ. On the contrary it is a factor which belongs essentially to the Christian faith.

But we shall say further: what you say contains one truth. It is true that we must surrender even our thinking to God. But we must enquire more precisely what that means. It cannot mean in any event that we are to give up completely the attempt to think for ourselves and to accept something as true which we cannot honestly believe to be true; it cannot mean that we are to suppress truthfulness. What it must mean will shortly become clear to us. In any case we might say about belief in New Testament miracles what the apostle Paul says about the eating of meats sacrificed to idols: "We are no worse off if we do not eat and no better off if we do" (I Cor. 8: 8). Yet having made it plain that Christian faith does not consist in accepting as true the miracle stories of the New Testament, we must make it equally plain that Christian faith implies faith in miracles, faith in the miraculous action of God, and the readiness to experience God's miracles in our lives. And our story is meant to enable us to understand what this means.

Hence we are not discussing whether this story represents the account of a true happening or whether it is merely legendary, a pious fiction. In order that no one may misunderstand me I will say that I myself consider it to be a pious fiction. But what it is intended to teach us, it will teach in any case, whether it be the report of a historical event or a pious fancy. We must understand it as a poetic image in which is mirrored the miraculous power which Jesus can exercise over a human life. The story itself leads us to suppose that it is in this sense that we should understand it. For the central point of the story is not at all that Peter has made a marvellous catch of fish, but that Peter has been called to be an apostle, a preacher of the Word. When Jesus says to Peter: "Henceforth you will be catching men", it becomes clear that the miracle of the catch of fish is a picture suggesting something far bigger. The real miracle is the apostolic effectiveness of Peter, the living reality and operation of the divine Word proclaimed by human lips. It is just this miracle which is to be illustrated by the symbolism of the miraculous catch of fish.

### II

What then is the meaning of miracle from the standpoint of the Christian religion? To what extent is Christian belief a belief in miracles? At all events, miracle implies an action wrought by God. To believe in miracle means, as we have already suggested, to be prepared to believe that the miraculous working of God meets us in our lives. It has often been said that all events are essentially miraculous. For if we believe in God as the Omnipotent, the Creator of heaven and earth, we must also be persuaded that all things are traceable to His action and energy and that all that happens to us is miraculous.

It is no doubt true that belief in God as Creator implies that all events are ultimately the effect of His operation. However, the situation is that the creative activity of God remains hidden from us, that we do not always and everywhere recognize and experience Him as Creator. Normally we view life and the world as an ordered state of things and as a process of controlled happenings grounded in the law of cause and effect. What takes place here springs partly from the movement of nature which is regulated by laws that we know, and whose forces we are more and more able to bring within our control, and partly from the impulsion of the human will and the planning of the human mind-things which are also intelligible to us. Where then does the miraculous creative action of God come into play?

There are no doubt moments in our lives when we believe that we become aware of the guidance and government of God the Creator; moments in which His miraculous action strikes us with powerful impressiveness. Such moments arise when for example we see scenes of great and sublime natural beauty, when we are deeply moved by a noble work of art, or when our experience convinces us of divine graciousness, or when we are shattered by a momentous historical event in which we feel compelled to see a manifestation of divine judgment. And we are right at such times to feel deeply aware of the wonder-working action of the Creator of all things. But we must admit at the same time that there are other men who see and experience the same things but who view and interpret them quite differently; who are unable to detect in them evidence of God's rule, or to hear in them God's voice.

This situation calls our attention to the most important point of all, namely that the creative action of God is not something which lies unbared to the eyes of all so that all must see and recognize it. No, God's creative action can only be apprehended when I experience something of it in my own life. Belief in God the Creator of heaven and earth is not a theoretical truth the rightness of which may be realized on the ground of demonstrative reasons, and of which a man might persuade himself once for all so as to be able to say: "If God is the Creator of the world, then He is also my Creator; if He is the Author of all that is, then He is also the Author of my own destiny." Belief of this kind often collapses sharply when a man is struck down by a dark and tragic fate. If he is no longer convinced that God is his Creator, he also ceases to be convinced that God must be the Creator of the world.

The truth in fact is that I can only believe in God as the Creator of the world, when I see that truth reflected in my own life and experience. When Luther in the Shorter Catechism explains the first article of belief: "I believe in God the

, he says: Father almighty, Creator of heaven and earth" "What does this mean?" Answer: "I believe that God has created me together with all other creatures." The "I believe that God has created me" is the primary matter. Only when I have reached certitude on that point, can I also believe that God has created all creatures. And for the same reason when Luther explains the first article in the Larger Catechism he does not speak in general terms about God as the Creator of all that lives and breathes, he speaks only of the individual: "What is now implied in the affirmation: I believe in God, Father, Almighty, Creator ...?" Answer: "By this I mean and believe that I am God's creation...

If this is so, then it is clear that belief in God as Creator and belief in His wonder-working action and energy is not a simple and easy matter, but a belief that is extremely difficult and hardly possible to come by, and for that very reason it is the fundamental thing. In fact belief in God as Creator contains in essence the whole Christian faith. Luther says in commenting on the creation story: "For doubtless the most important article of the faith is that in which we declare: I believe in God, Father Omnipotent, Creator of heaven and earth. And whoever truly believes this, has already been helped and restored to the right way and has reached the state from which Adam fell. But there are few people who have progressed so far as to believe in the fullest sense that our God is the God who creates and makes all things."1

"There are few such"! Do we belong to these few? Are we in truth constantly aware that we are God's creation? Does the unfolding course of events in world history always strike us as being grounded in the wondrous activity of God? We may well no doubt have moments such as those we mentioned earlier in which we believe we see the miracles which God effects. But do these moments give light to our whole life, so that we always realize ourselves to be held in God's creator hand? Nay, do they retain their sway when our passions overwhelm us or when we are struck by a fate which threatens to break us utterly? The beautiful and uplifting things we have experienced may well sound a comforting echo and prove a precious possession in times of hardship and distress. But can we hold on to them in hours of cruel pain and bitterest renunciation and sacrifice? Do they suffice to make us always certain of God as the Creator of all things?

If it were really true that we lived in the assured conviction that we are the creation of God, we would cease to

1 Luther, Sermons on Genesis 15, 27, Preface (W.A., 24, PP. 18, 26ff.).

ıбı question and complain, all our experience would be clear and intelligible and we would be able to go on our way with glad and grateful hearts. But which of us is there for whom the world and destiny do not again and again grow dark, sinister and puzzling? Who is there who has ceased to ask: why? Who has ceased to lament over the burden of pain and sorrow, disillusionment and shattered hopes? Which of us has no fear of fate and death? And further: which of us can be satisfied with himself so that his whole being is irradiated by sunny contentment? Who is not tortured by the problems of his own life, by his weakness and inadequacy, by his guilt? Who must not confess that listlessness and irritation, dark passions, untruthfulness, cowardice, or impurity, again and again overcome him? Which of us therefore could always confess with a grateful heart: "I believe that God has created me"?

But if we honestly confess all this, we also know what miracle essentially must mean: namely, such a happening as makes us profoundly aware that in spite of everything, in spite of all the mysteries of the world and of our destiny, in spite of the torment of our self-condemnation, God has created us by His grace, and holds us in His Creator's hand. And just then, and only then, when we focus our attention on this one great marvel, can we be aware of the miraculous in our experience. For when we are moved by this central question, the Word of Jesus comes home to us.

### III

Our story tells us that Jesus invites the disciples to catch fish after they have toiled all the night and taken nothing. Left to themselves, they would have abandoned their efforts. And just then comes the extraordinary command: "Put out into the deep and let down your nets for a catch!"-into the deep, i.e. where a catch of fish is most improbable. The story teaches us that the wonder-working word of Jesus comes to us precisely when our own resources are exhausted. Weshall not hear the word of Jesus so long as we have not recognized our situation as hopeless, so long as we refuse to confess:

I 62

> "With force of arms we nothing can;  
> Full soon were we down-ridden."

For why is it that the mighty creative and guiding power of God remains hidden from us? Why is it that we so seldom, or not at all, experience the wondrous things God can accomplish for us? It is simply because we wish to, and suppose that we can, control our lives by our own initiative; it is because we wish to live in our own strength, by our own will, by the ingenuity of our own planning. Accordingly we accept as real and valid that view of the world which fits into this outlook. This view however is one in which the world and all that happens within it appears to be controlled by law, the sequence of cause and effect; it is the world whose energies we master for our own purposes; the world which we shape according to our own designs. And so long as we see the world from this angle, it will not disclose to us the mighty power, the guidance and government of God.

Of course there is no question that the world may be understood and controlled in this way in so far as it is the world of our daily work. Nevertheless the question is whether the world understood thus is the world in its fullest reality; whether the real world has not depths which are concealed by just such a view; whether it has not another aspect which we fail to perceive so long as we equate it simply with the world of our work which, as such, stands at our disposal. And the decisive issue for us is whether we are prepared to be content to regard the world merely in terms of our daily affairs, and so to regard ourselves as the masters of the world, or whether we are going to attain the insight that we are not in reality the masters of the world and that in the last resort our initiative and resources are all in vain. If we wish to become truly aware of God as Creator, and truly to grasp His wonder-working powers; if we really long to apprehend the one astounding miracle, an event which makes us certain that God is our Creator, then this means that we long to become new creatures, that we must put away our old man, our old manner of life, our habit of living according to self-will and the strength of our own resources. In short, it means that we must recognize our old way of life to be sinful.

In the passage in which Luther says that belief in God as Creator is the central, decisive, all-embracing belief, and in which he adds that there are few "who have progressed so far as to believe in the fullest sense that our God is the God who creates and makes all things", he continues thus: "For such a man must be dead to all things, to good and ill, to life and death, to heaven and hell, and must from the depths of his heart confess that in his own strength he can do nothing."

The man who is able to confess this is also able to realize what a gift God has given to the world in Jesus Christ. Such a one encounters Jesus as the manifestation of the redeeming grace of God in this world, and the word which promises this grace to the sinner finds its way into his heart. All this is made vividly clear to us in our story. When Peter in obedience to the command of Jesus has let down his net and made the wonderful catch of fish, he falls to his knees before Jesus and says: "Depart from me, for I am a sinful man, O Lord." Jesus, however, does not reject his adoration but promises: "Do not be afraid; henceforth you will be catching men." Thus to the prostrate sinner the miracle is made known, and the real miracle is just this—that Jesus calls to His service sinful man, that He transforms the being of sinful man making him a new and pure creation.

In fact the one and the true miracle is that the word of God's saving grace which meets us in Jesus Christ creates us anew, freeing us from the fetters of ourselves and our old sinful way of life, thereby making us a "new creation". The fact that this word of grace goes on resounding, that it challenges us even to-day, here and now, with the question whether we are prepared to understand our whole life in the light of this divine word—just that is the revelation to us of God's rule as Creator.

Whoever with a willing and faithful heart hears the word of God's redeeming grace, whoever can say with Simon

164

Peter: "Depart from me, for I am a sinful man, O Lord" experiences the one true miracle, namely that God frees him from the bondage of self, and gives him the power to behold the mighty works wrought by divine action, indeed to do mighty works himself.

To behold the mighty works of God! No longer does he view the world from the same standpoint as other men. He must of course, since he is not taken out of the world, still regard it also as the world of his work, and reckon with its orderliness and its laws. It is not in the infringement of these laws that he expects to see the miracles which God effects. What is truly miraculous for him is the fact that all events which for others are part and parcel of the normal course of things, betray for him the secret working of God who is gracious to him, who shatters his being with dismay, who again and again makes him realize his powerlessness and lostness, and ever afresh brings him to the realization of the wonder of divine grace.

No doubt we are constantly pressed by the temptation to fall back again into the old pedestrian view that this world is only the world of business and affairs, which stands wholly within our control and is therefore silent, refusing to reveal the Creator. No doubt also there remains for thought the insoluble problem as to how it happens that from one point of view the world appears as the ordered world of our daily routine, and yet at the same time is the world of God's creation. In face of this contradiction, faith persistently affirms its great "Nevertheless" which can honestly be declared by anyone who has decisively encountered the love of God in Christ, the love which has redeemed him from the bondage of himself. In this situation he will surrender his thought to God, not by renouncing his reasoning powers in all those areas of life which reason can investigate and know, but by renouncing the attempt to shape his course in life by reason alone, by surrendering the presumption to solve life's mysteries. He will dare to cast himself on the self-revealing grace of God and to say: "O the depth of the riches and wisdom and knowledge of God! How unsearchable are His judgments and how inscrutable His ways!" (Rom. 11: 33)

He will be perceptive of the wonders wrought by God and he will himself do marvellous things. He hears the command of Jesus: "Put out into the deep and let down your nets for a catch." His own deeds appear to him in a new light; they are no longer the deeds which he has himself chosen to do and which he accomplishes in his own strength and by his own planning, they are rather deeds done at God's command, and hence deeds sustained and completed by divine grace. In fact he knows that he can dare to do more than human calculation is able to foresee, for he is independent of what may be attainable according to human calculations, and he commits the results of his doing into God's hands. But his action, flowing from the experienced love of God, is itself an action inspired by love, and so he has the power to bear witness to the love of God before others and to embody God's love in concrete reality. A man whose whole being and conduct is sustained by the peace and joy of love, is himself a miracle in the eyes of others. And in this sense we are all called not only to behold the wonders of God but on our part to effect wondrous things.

My friends, this is the last academic service of the present term and it presents usespecially the academic community which is to-day gathered here—with a serious question.
