This term we have been allowed to pursue our studies in peace, while so many of our fellow-students, colleagues and friends are at the front, torn from their normal work, living in stress and danger, and ensuring by the devotion of their energies and lives the peace of our quiet round of study. We think of them now in the deep bonds of fellowship and gratitude. But we must also remember that for us it is an undeserved grace of God that we have been able to carry on our work here. Ought we not to interpret this also as a miracle that has been granted us? Hence let us shoulder our responsibilities all the more seriously and bear witness by our own weak action to the mighty working of God, so that even in these distressing and anxious times the Creator's praise may ring out. "The Lord's name be praised!"

i 66

## St. Luke 17:7–10

*May 10, 1942*

"Will any one of you, who has a servant ploughing or keeping sheep, say to him when he has come in from the field, 'Come at once and sit down at table'? Will he not rather say to him, 'Prepare supper for me, and gird yourself and serve me, till I eat and drink; and afterwards you shall eat and drink'? Does he thank the servant because he did what was commanded? So you also, when you have done all that is commanded you, say, 'We are unworthy servants; we have only done what was our duty.' "

MATTHIAS CLAUDIUS once said: "The words of Christ are like a fountain which never dries up. When you draw from this fountain of wisdom, it fills up again and the second truth you derive is fuller and more splendid than the first. Thus it is with all that proceeded from the mouth of Christ, His sayings and His parables." May we prove the truth of these words to-day!

### I

Our parable is easily intelligible in itself. It illustrated the relation existing between a master and his slave (for the "servant" of the parable is a slave). The slave is the property of his master, and stands wholly at the disposal of the latter. The master has unlimited right to his service. The slave has no rights at all and is not in a position to raise any claims. All that he does he is obliged to do, and he has no reason to expect any thanks from his master.

In suggesting this, Jesus is not telling His hearers anything new, nor does He propose to give them instruction about the slave system. They already know all about it. Again, He does not intend to make a judgment on slavery or to say whether it is a worthy or an unworthy institution. He takes the material basis of His parable from life and from the social arrangements which were familiar to His contemporaries and hearers. The new and surprising thing is rather the conclusion: "So you also ..." The novelty lies in Jesus' suggestion that the relation between God and man is the same as that between a master and his slave. This was a strange idea even for the Jewish audience of Jesus. Of course they were accustomed to speak of God as the almighty all-commanding Lord, who owes account to no man, but they regarded man as not being simply God's slave, but rather God's servant, rewarded by God and entitled to such a reward, and acquiring merit by the faithful fulfilment of his obligations. Hence according to Jesus this was false. The suggestion is not only strange and surprising, but offensive and vexaticus!

Still more is the suggestion offensive to the mind of modern man; and in this connexion I am thinking not only of the people of to-day but of man as his being and mode of thinking has developed during the past few centuries since the time of the Renaissance. He is of the opinion that in contrast with earlier and cruder epochs he cherishes a purified and spiritual idea of God: he thinks that God is not an arbitrary despot, a master in command of slaves, but as man himself is conscious of his own moral freedom and dignity, God too is for him the embodiment of ethical lofty ideals, a God who is honoured precisely by the fact that man in the dignity of his freedom can proudly raise his head to God. God is not sheer power, overwhelming, coercive, shattering, but rather spiritual power exercising sway over man through the inner life of the spirit, not humbling man but rather exalting him. Man would feel it to be demeaning to recognize himself as the slave of an arbitrary God, whose action cannot in any way be called to account.

To be sure, while man realizes that he may not call God to account at the bar of his petty personal desires and needs, he yet expects that the action of God should evince its rightness by the criterion of the highest human ideals of righteousness and goodness! In this way man does call God to account. And if the process of events in the world and in personal destiny does not conform to these ideals, he quickly

i 68 infers that the whole idea of God is illusory. If he sees that evil triumphs in the world and good succumbs, then he doubts or despairs of God as being in sovereign control of fate and history. When he sees the innumerable acts of folly and madness in the world he begins to doubt the very idea of God. How can God, if there is a God, allow this or that to go on? Hence, he thinks, there is no God, there cannot be a God.

This is the characteristic judgment of modern man, voiced again and again, and to-day especially many people are speaking like this. There is no need for me to go into details. In fact, both the parable of Jesus and ruthless fate give us the same shock of offence repeatedly: it is not at all obvious to man's perception that a God of justice and goodness reigns supreme. The God of the parable is the sovereign Lord to whose caprice man is surrendered, and in relation to whom he has no rights or claims—and this too is the God who does violence to man's conscience in the tragic mysteries of human destiny. In both cases it is the same shock of offence! The only difference is that the man of to-day draws the conclusion that such a God is no God—whether he ends in indifference and resignation, or whether he draws the further conclusion that it is for human beings to take their lives in this world into their own hands in disregard of God. Let us make ourselves the masters, they say, we who are free and steadfast in purpose! In any case, man does not really surmount the obstacle implied in the offence that divine justice is not perceptible, divine goodness is not demonstrable.

But is not this parable an offence for the Christian also who wishes to submit himself humbly and sincerely to the will of God? to the God whom the gospel proclaims to be the God of grace? Is it the gospel which Jesus is preaching here? Does not Jesus speak elsewhere of God as the heavenly Father from whose providential goodness everyone may draw consolation? Does He not speak of the God without whose knowledge not a sparrow falls to the ground, who feeds the ravens and clothes in beauty the flowers of the field, and cares still more for us His children? God as the Father of men who are His children; how does such a

ı69 doctrine agree with the teaching of our parable which implies that God is the master whose slaves we are? Are we here still moving within the atmosphere of the gospel of good news?

It would be an obvious answer to say that we should not isolate this parable and view it as a detached saying. We must complete it. We must take it together with the other sayings of Jesus which speak of God as loving Father, with the words of grace and mercy. Of course we must do so. But the composite view must not be so constructed that we complete one feature by adding another, and so make for ourselves a selective picture of Jesus and His message in which one feature tones down another, in which the greatness and the power of the one is weakened by the other, so that finally there issues a picture of Jesus which is muddled and diluted; a picture which at bottom is shaped according to our own tastes and wishes. No; the composite whole must emerge in a different manner. It must arise from a process in which one saying explains another, in which we understand one word in the light of another, so that in the end every word of Jesus is seen to disclose the one and the same Lord, whose word is at one and the same time both terrifying and consoling, a dismaying and a glad message. Only then have we understood it fully.

What does this mean as far as our parable is concerned? It means this, that we must not set alongside His teaching here another message concerning the goodness and the grace of God, but that we must understand this very parable as a word of grace.

### II

But how are we to understand this paradox, namely that God precisely in so far as He is the Lord who deals with us arbitrarily as His slaves, is at the same time the Father whose loving-kindness governs us His children? How understand that just that insight which we cannot fail to express: "We are unworthy servants", is for us liberating, redeeming, saving?

We must at once remove a possible misunderstanding.

The phrase "unworthy servants" means the same as "wretched"; it does not mean servants who do their duty badly so that when we have only half fulfilled or not fulfilled at all our obligations, God is a terrifying master for us. The parable is not speaking for example of God as our Judge, before whom we stand as imperfect, impure, and sinful men. It is speaking about God as Lord, before whom we stand forth as miserable creatures, even when we have done all that was commanded us. In that this is so, it speaks a message of comfort just to those who suffer from the consciousness of their imperfection. God does not consider whether you are more or less perfect! In His sight no one is better than another, if perfection is the standard: "When you have done all that is commanded you, say, 'We are unworthy servants!' " This is what Luther means when he sings:

> " 'Tis through Thy love alone we gain  
> The pardon of our sin;  
> The strictest life is but in vain,  
> Our works can nothing win;  
> That none should boast himself of aught,  
> But own in fear Thy grace hath wrought  
> What in him seemeth righteous."1

Have we now some idea what our parable wishes to teach us, and in what way it is a message of divine grace? It is a comforting word of grace because it teaches us that we wretched human beings cannot live otherwise than by the inspiration of God's grace. It teaches us this, precisely because it shows us God as He really is, as our Lord, before whom we poor mortals can have no right, can raise no claim. It is a liberating and saving word because it frees us from the illusion that we might, because of what we are and achieve, lay claim to some justification in the sight of God. Only he who surrenders all presumption to right and claim before God, only he can live by God's grace.

This, however, seems a hard thing for man—to renounce every right in the eyes of God and to silence every claim, to cast himself utterly and unconditionally on divine favour

> **[1]** Tr. Catherine Winkworth.

and mercy. In our day, the attitude of natural man who resists dependence on the grace of God often finds expression in the saying that man has a right to live. Well, has he not such a right? After all he is not responsible for having entered the stream of life in this world, and he therefore seems to be under no obligation of gratitude to anyone for the fact of his existence, and to bear no responsibility for what he proposes to do with his life. Yet he has claims on life, he voices demands. And this is the fundamental sin of mankind-the failure to recognize that man is a creature, man's assertion of his right to live.

As a creature he should be grateful for his life to his Creator. But how can he be, when his life is one long wearisome struggle with adverse fate? How can he give thanks when his life has become a burden to him, an un-endurable torment? When he sees no meaning or sense in his life, and the meaning which he himself would like to confer on his life is destroyed by a rutliless fate? Is it not under-standable that there have been men who have cursed the day of their birth? That there have been men who have extolled as the supreme bliss never to have been born, and as the second highest bliss to die young? Certainly it is understandable that a man should love life because he feels in himself the energy and the joy to accomplish work and taste of pleasure, or when his life, however steep the pathway, is leading to success and fulfilment. But what of the man whose powers are impaired or destroyed, who because of illness or poverty must forbid himself every enjoyment? How can a man be grateful for the gift of life when his whole being "is stretched upon the rack" and bent to work which is weakening to the body and paralysing to the mind?

He will never be able to do so as long as he does not give up the belief that he has any right to life, as long as he does not realize himself to be a creature, as long as he supposes that he must himself give meaning to his life. Do you seek after God? Then you must know what the object of your search is. What is the meaning of this word "God"? To be God means to be the Creator, and to seek God means not to seek some power which rules the world according to your desires, your understanding, and your ideals, but to seek the Creator who rules the world according to His own inscrutable will. If you are seeking God you are seeking the Lord and Ruler of the world, whom you will only know when you are ready to recognize yourself as His creature, when you are ready to allow Him to be God, and to surrender yourself unreservedly to His will, even though His being is utterly incomprehensible to you and wrapped in darkness. Luther says: "Whoever refuses to suffer, can know no God as Creator, for such a one is not prepared to be nothing, that God might be his Creator." "It is the way of God first of all to destroy and humble to the dust what is in us, before He bestows on us the gift of His grace." Whoever resists this

, to him God the Creator becomes an destruction "as by fire" adverse destructive fate, and the countenance of God changes for such a one into the countenance of the devil.

God is our Creator; and all laments and accusations, all doubts and all despair, arise from the fact that the true idea of God has become lost to us and, ever anew, threatens to be dissolved. And this basic sin of denying God as Creator and asserting one's own right to life is working out its terrible consequences in every department of life; it is like a poison which is ruining both the life of the individual and of the community. From the fact that men assert and insist on their right, that they first and foremost make their claims to life instead of asking what is required of them, there arises envy and hatred, contempt for the laws of nature which sustain life, strife and distress both on a small and a big scale.

No man who exists is without wishes and longings, and no living man can be, any more than a child can have no desires in its relations with its parents. But just as it never enters the head of a true child to suppose that it must make claims on its parents and that it has a right to their kindness, just as the child waits confidently for the fulfilment of its desires as a sheer gift, so we adults must not presumptuously assert our right to a good life. We must rather be prepared for what God gives; we ought rather to realize that the highest blessing of all, namely that which confers fulfilment on our life, can always be only a gift and never the satisfaction of a claim, a due, arrogantly asserted.

And how much joy, what stores of blessing, are missed by a nation's youth which supposes that it has a rightful claim to the satisfaction of its wishes! We are of course always hearing it said: "I have a right to my body." No! You have no right to it; your body is a gift entrusted to you by the Creator, a gift which it is your duty to keep whole and pure. And whoever takes as right what should be a gift, inevitably misses the most precious things in life. Naturally readiness for a gift implies also readiness for renunciation. Only he who is prepared to renounce, to surrender all claimfulness, has the power for unhindered receptiveness. And if what he receives is not what he has desired and longed for, then the unexpected gift which is granted him will be for him an un-expected source of blessing and happiness, and he will learn to understand his very renunciation, unavoidable as it was, to be a gift of grace.

In human society there is of course an assertion of right, without which it would be impossible for men to live in community. That, however, is never a question of my right and your right, but of intrinsic rightfulness for which each one must be answerable. And human society can only be sound when the individual is concerned not so much about his own claims as about his duties and obligations. Moreover even this is far too little. Human society subsists not only on a basis of sense of obligation, but also on the basis of love and mutual trust. It can exist and thrive only when the individual is prepared to renounce and sacrifice himself for the sake of another; when its members are not nervously anxious to be uncompromising and unrelaxing in their claims, but rather are anxious to meet the wishes of others, to respect and esteem others. We ought not to approach each other with a coldly calculating manner; we need mutual confidence and trust. Confidence is not blind, but it always commits itself beyond the measure of cool calculation. Love may be rewarded with ingratitude, confidence may be misplaced and disappointed.

But if we are not prepared to accept the possibility of ingratitude and disappointment, we shall never experience the rich rewards of love and confidence.

Just as arrogance which insists on its rights corrupts the life both of the individual and the community, so also it is arrogance which in times of sore distress arouses a spirit of complaint and accusation, and prevents sorrow from bearing the fruits of grace. No doubt there are haughty and defiant men who are steeled by suffering. But are they really matured thereby? There is a defiant harshness of character which chooses self-isolation and reserve, and which conceals an inner cold-heartedness and spiritual void. It may well be that such a man is invulnerable to the blows of fate; but he is also blind to the graciousness of fate. There is also a character which is steeled by heavy sorrow and which is truly matured and strengthened thereby, a character of cheerful open-heartedness, of inner spiritual wealth; a character which gives itself freely to all, in warmth of heart which radiates itself on all with whom it comes into contact.

This is genuine power, whereas cold harshness is only apparent power and behind its reserve hides an inner weakness. Genuine strength of character does not fortify itself against fate in an attitude of proud reserve, but exposes itself to fate in the confidence that whatever fate brings can only make it grow stronger and richer. Such a man understands and accepts his destiny as a real source of grace and blessing. Moreover he has the inner freedom of spirit to enjoy the beautiful and the good which do come to him, the sunny days through which it is granted him to pass, in a spirit of serenity, without fear and without a bad conscience. For were we not resigned to whatever destiny may bring, how could we with a good conscience enjoy the good which is granted us, when all around us there is so much distress and misery? How could we, especially in a time like this, have a good conscience and inner peace, we who live at home in security (or at least the majority of us) no doubt with many cares and troubles yet at the same time free from the super-human exertions and severe sufferings to which many of our relations are exposed at the front? How could we accept this relative ease if we had not inwardly the readiness to accept bravely whatever griefs come our way? And if sorrow came and found us unprepared, could we appropriately say like Job: "Shall we receive good at the hand of God and shall we not receive evil?" (Job 2: I0.)

But who is the man who can willingly and trustfully accept and open his heart to whatever may come to him? It is the man who can wholly surrender himself to God as His Creator, and resign himself to God's dealings; who can sincerely repeat the prayer of our Lord in Gethsemane: "Not as I will but as Thou wilt" (Matt. 26: 39). It is the man who knows himself to be the slave of God his Master. It is the man whom Jesus is referring to in this parable. In another parable the poet C. F. Meyer expresses the same attitude in the words which he puts into the mouth of Michaelangelo:

"I who am a slave of passion, make me in Thy image pure and free! The first man Thou madest out of clay, I will indeed be made of sterner stuff; forsooth, Master, now Thou needest Thy hammer! Strike, divine Sculptor, I am the stone."

Because the parable of Jesus invites us to surrender ourselves in this way, it is a word of grace, a word of liberation. Because it relentlessly smites from our hands our cherished rights and claims, it frees us to receive the grace of God. In the last analysis it has the same meaning as the word: "Truly I say to you, whoever does not receive the kingdom of God like a child, shall not enter it" (Mark. Io: 15). To be God's slave and to be His child is the same thing, and to those who have the child-like spirit, the promise runs: "To such belongs the kingdom of God" (v. 14).

But it is not easy to surrender our claimfulness! Is it not asking too much of our powers? Now the message of Jesus is finally shown to be a message of grace inasmuch as it drives us to our last place of refuge, to prayer, in order that we might receive strength to do what is required. "Rogate!"

Pray!-such is the traditional title of this Sunday. The word of God speaks to us thus: "I dwell in the high and holy place, and also with him who is of a contrite and humble spirit, to revive the spirit of the humble, and to revive the heart of the contrite" (Isa. 57: 15). So then let us pray:

"Make me simple and sincere, inwardly detached, gentle and calm, penetrated by Thy peace. Make me pure in heart, that in spirit and in truth I may behold Thy luminous clarity. May my heart soar upwards as an eagle soars, living in Thee alone."

At the same time to-day is also the first academic service of this term. Let us submit all that it brings, together with the work which we shall be called on to do, to the sway of this message of Jesus; namely that we are unworthy servants who, in whatever we may accomplish, are only fulfilling our obligations, and who in the spirit of patience are prepared for whatever God may send or bestow. We are so much the more determined to tread this path of self-surrender because we are also thinking of our friends who have been torn away from their work, whether as teachers or students, who are now out there at the front and who have suffered and must go on suffering unspeakable things. Whatever problems and difficulties oppress us here are nothing as compared with the cruel destiny which weighs on them. How could they steadfastly face this destiny if not in the spirit of straightforward unquestioning self-surrender, which renounces every right and claim and humbly and trustingly leaves it to God to decide for what service He needs man and what fate He will send man? And how could we maintain our inner spiritual communion with them if we were not at one with them in the determination to bow in calmness and trust to the will of God our Creator? So linked with them in heart and mind let us say:

> "Lord, send what Thou willest,  
> Happy or painful;  
> I am content, since both  
> Flow from Thy hands."

## St. Matthew 20:1–15

*August 16, 1942*

"For the kingdom of heaven is like a householder who went out early in the morning to hire labourers for his vineyard. After agreeing with the labourers for a denarius a day, he sent them into his vineyard. And going out about the third hour he saw others standing idle in the market place; and to them he said, 'You go into the vineyard too, and whatever is right I will give you.' So they went. Going out again about the sixth hour and the ninth hour, he did the same. And about the eleventh hour he went out and found others standing; and he said to them, 'Why do you stand here idle all day?" They said to him, 'Because no one has hired us.' He said to them, 'You go into the vineyard too?

And when evening came, the owner of the vineyard said to his steward, 'Call the labourers and pay them their wages, beginning with the last, up to the first.' And when those hired about the eleventh hour came, each of them received a denarius. Now when the first came, they thought they would receive more; but each of them also received a denarius. And on receiving it they grumbled at the householder, saying, "These last worked only one hour, and you have made them equal to us who have borne the burden of the day and the scorching heat. But he replied to one of them, 'Friend, I am doing you no wrong; did you not agree with me for a denarius? Take what belongs to you, and go; I choose to give to this last as I give to you. Am I not allowed to do what I choose with what belongs to me? Or do you begrudge my generosity?" "

Our parable arouses in its hearers astonishment and indeed offence in much the same way as the parable which we considered a short while ago, namely, the parable which compares man with a slave who, whatever he does, can only do what he is obliged to do and is in no position to make claims on his master. As in that case the question which arose was: is then man's relationship to God the relationship of a slave to his master? Is man wholly surrendered to the arbitrary power of God? so here the question which we have to face is: Is not God acting unjustly if He behaves like this householder who gives no more pay to those workers who for twelve long hours bore the burden of the day and the scorching heat than to those who worked only for one hour at sunset? Equal recompense is given to unequal work! Is not this an act of injustice?

This impression is strengthened if we set beside the parable of Jesus that of a Jewish scribe which outwardly bears a complete resemblance to it. This parable speaks of a king who had hired many workers for the completion of a task. One of them distinguished himself by his industry and skill, with the result that the king took him by the hand and walked to and fro with him. At evening time the workers came and the king gave them all the same reward. Those who had worked the whole day long complained saying: "We have worked all day long and this fellow has worked for only two hours, yet he also has received full payment!" The king made answer: "In two hours he achieved more than you did in the whole day!" Thus in this instance too the same reward was given for an unequal amount of work, yet this was only so in appearance. In reality an equal amount of work was performed. Our sense of justice is thus satisfied. For in this case it is made clear why the shorter time of work was rewarded equally with the longer time; in the shorter interval as much was performed as in the longer. Here too then the principle is observed: equal pay for equal performance!

Now it is against this very principle that the parable of Jesus protests. In the parable of Jesus it is a question of equal pay for unequal work. In order to understand this we must first of all consider what kind of a reward is in question. It is the reward which God gives to men. In His parable Jesus is not of course concerned to expound and teach basic social principles as to how employees should be treated, He is concerned to show how God acts towards men. It would certainly be foolish to demand that we men should behave towards each other as God behaves, but it is equally foolish to require that God should be determined in His action by the principles which guide us. In order to illustrate the character of God's action, Jesus narrates this story of the householder and the labourers in the vineyard. In any event the householder behaves quite correctly in so far as he faithfully gives to his labourers the agreed payment at the due time. When those who were first hired complain that they do not receive more, he is quite justified in his reply: "Friend, I am doing you no wrong: did you not agree with me for a denarius?" What proper answer can the grumbling workman give? It is quite evident that the householder is right; the labourer can raise no just claim.

And that is the first thing we are meant to learn from this parable: man can raise no just claim in the sight of God. God gives us what is our due, and no one has the right to complain and require more. But the disposition to think that we can make claims on God clings to us men and so oftentimes we are accustomed to look at others who are faring better than ourselves, and to complain: by what means have they deserved better than we? Are there not many of these lucky people who have toiled far less than ourselves? who have endured far less trial and vexation? Yet they are to fare better than we! With what justification? May we not claim as much? Or else we moan: why am I in particular struck by evil which is spared others? Yet they are no better than I! In fact it is just those whose conduct is less exemplary who so often escape! It is not seldom that thoughts and feelings of this kind sway men's minds.

In fact it is this trend of thought which governs the Jewish parable that has just been quoted. Its aim is to comfort man with the doctrine that fundamentally God acts in accordance with justice, i.e. justice as measured by the standards of human justice, which rewards like performance with like recompense. This parable rests on the conception that God rewards the achievements of men, and hence must give the same recompense to the same degree of merit. Now

I8o it is this very conception which Jesus opposes, and He wishes to replace it by a different understanding of God. And what is His conception? Just that of God's sovereign freedom. God is free over against all human claims! "I choose to give to this last as I give to you. Am I not allowed to do what I choose with what belongs to me?" says the householder in the parable, and the remark is intended to illustrate God's absolute freedom. Man must bow before the transcendence of this divine freedom and not ask why. God is sovereignly free and unfettered by human claims; and whoever dares to speak of God or to reckon with God must be clear about this. Man is subject to the free will of God and has no right to make claims against God.

Thus far our parable teaches us nothing other than the parable about the master and his slaves. But it does not teach us this lesson alone; there is something decisively important which it adds. What is the real sting of the offence which the grumbling workmen feel? They are not of course offended because they have been badly treated, because their pay has been reduced or kept back. The sting lies rather in the fact that the others who have worked less than themselves receive none the less the same reward. And on what grounds does it rest that the employer gives these latter the same recompense? On the grounds of his sheer kindness and generosity. He shames the grumblers by his question: "Do you begrudge my generosity?" Did not they whom no one had hired need a day's wages just as much as they who were first hired? Were they not also men who suffered hunger and thirst? Did they not likewise want money for the needs of their life and of their families? Were they to be made to suffer because up to evening they had found no remunerative work?

The fact is of course that the grumblers had not for one moment considered from this point of view the behaviour of their employer; they had considered it only from the standpoint of their own advantage. Must they not inevitably feel ashamed? Ought they not rather to have rejoiced on hearing that their comrades, thanks to the kindness of their master, were to receive just as much as themselves so that they might have the wherewithal to sustain their own life and that of their families? Yes, these surly fellows could not but feel ashamed, and confess that the freedom of their master was exercised just in his generosity. And thus the parable is meant to teach us the unity of God's freedom and love.

Freedom and love belong integrally together in God and form a unity in His nature. No doubt we can imagine a freedom which manifests itself apart from love; a freedom of arbitrary and harsh power. But we cannot conceive any form of love and generosity, even amongst us men, existing without freedom. For we can only speak of a gift flowing from kindness when the gift is given not as a consequence of any external compulsion, not under the pressure of some socalled moral or social exigence, but indeed in perfect freedom, in conditions where the gift might not have been made at all, and where no claim on it exists. The activity of love can take place only where there is freedom. And were we to organize the whole complex of human relationships on a basis of right and law, to the exclusion of true freedom, so that everything which a man receives would rest on some just claim, then we should be banishing from the world the spirit of generous love, and so we should be excluding the very thing which imparts to the human soul real nobility and animates man with happiness and joy. Kindness is always undeserved. And what rejoices man's heart is precisely what he is given as a sheer gift, i.e. the bestowal of a gift which he has not deserved.

So it is too in man's relation with God. If we think of God only in the light of our claims, we shall never experience the goodness of God, for His goodness presupposes His freedom. And God's graciousness to man is always undeserved. For this reason those people who go through life obsessed by the feeling of their rightful claims are blind to the prevenient goodness with which God meets them. The man who always supposes that the things which are given him are only his just due, who takes the good that comes his way as though he had a right to it, fails to perceive God's overflowing generosity, and misses the best in life, the sentiments of joy and gratitude and contentment. For such a man is of course never satisfied, and always thinks that his claim exceeds what is actually given him. God's goodness implies His freedom; and as His goodness is inexhaustible, exceeding all the goodness of man, so also is His freedom unlimited, transcending all human freedom. Hence if we are to experience God's goodness we must be prepared also to acknowledge His freedom, and to silence all claimfulness. For this reason Jesus in His parable emphasizes God's freedom so sharply as to arouse at first a shock of offence in His hearer: "Am I not allowed to do what I choose with what belongs to me?"

Now of course when we speak in these terms of God's goodness and freedom many tormenting questions arise. Is God's goodness in reality experienced by all men? Is it always only their own fault if they fail to perceive how the gracious goodness of God is active in their own lives? For example, did the labourers who were hired first experience the kindness of their master? There are certainly many sad and tragic destinies in which it is not easy to trace any manifestation of God's kindness, and indeed there is much human distress in face of which heart-searching questions spring to our minds. Even to those who ask these questions the words of Jesus contain a message of comfort; but our parable does not examine such questions directly. It proposes to teach us only one thing, namely, the unity of God's goodness and freedom. And we should not allow our attention to be diverted from its main purpose by other questions which receive an answer elsewhere.

However, if we probe more deeply the teaching implicit in our parable we perceive that indirectly it also contains some answer to these vexing problems; namely the answer that, if we forget God's freedom, we shall never be able to become aware of His goodness. And there is a further point: whoever fails to acknowledge God's sovereign Freedom shows thereby that he has not a kind heart himself. To have the vision of God's unconstrained goodness requires from man that he himself should be willing to exercise goodness.

### III

Let us look once more at the disposition of these grumblers in the parable. If anyone perchance asks: did they experience God's goodness, did it not rather come to the men who were hired last? the very question betrays a wrongness of outlook. Is it not always a great and splendid thing to see God's goodness in action? Should we not be ready to rejoice whenever we see a manifestation of God's generosity, whether it be I myself or someone else who is its object? Yes, that is just the point; are we able to feel joy in the good which comes to others? For of course this capacity to rejoice with others is the sign of a noble and kind disposition.

"Do you begrudge my generosity?" asks the householder in the parable. Should not the surly fellow have been ashamed as he realized that he ought to have been pleased to think that his comrades were being so generously treated? Instead of this his heart was filled with a petty envy when he saw that another man was getting better treatment than himself. And do we suppose that a heart like this could be capable of perceiving God's sheer goodness when he himself becomes the object of it? No! such a man will never be aware of it, even when generous treatment is meted out to him, for he will not be capable of confessing with shame: "Lord, I am not in the least worthy of all the faithfulness and mercy which Thou hast shown to Thy servant!" He will rather take the gift as though he merited it. Still more will such a man be incapable of perceiving God's kindness in the midst of adversity and sorrow. The awareness of God's kindness to us implies that we ourselves are kind and generous in disposition.

This is certainly not the only word of comfort which can be spoken to those who are in trouble and sorrow. But it is a word which must be said to them too. They must be told: if you hope and long for the experience of God's goodness then you yourselves must be ready to exercise goodness. In your lament over your own distresses there must not be mingled any note of complaining that others are better treated than you; there must not be intermixed with your sorrows any suggestion of mean envy. Rather, if we wish to experience joy ourselves, we must be ready to rejoice with others. And it is remarkable how, in our joy over the good fortune of others, our own troubles find some alleviation. If we are able to rejoice with others in their happiness, we inwardly participate in the good which has been bestowed on them. Then we are permitted to see the manifestation of God's goodness, and to become aware of God's goodness in its manifestations is always an enheartening and strengthening experience. And it is just the man who can see God's goodness reflected in the lives of others, who has also eyes to see it when it comes to himself. But if we cannot rejoice in the blessings which enrich other lives, then we make ourselves blind to those which come to ourselves.

Was this not in fact the case with those men who were first hired? Had they not essentially a great advantage over those who were not hired until eventide? Certainly they had borne the burden of work the whole day through. But could this fact be viewed from one side only, namely, work as a torment and burden? Was it not possible to look at the situation in another light also? Those who were hired later were not idlers and loafers, but simply workless men. Is it the case that the unemployed have so much better a lot than those who are at work? Should not rather those who are in work feel obliged to give thanks and rejoice because they are able to work?

Perhaps I may be allowed to relate a small incident in my own experience. My father was for many years the parson of a village in the district where I was brought up. When I returned to this village some years later, I fell into conversation with an old man who years before had listened to my father's sermons. He told me that there was one sermon in particular which he had never forgotten. It was the sermon preached on the occasion of a harvest festival and in a year when, owing to very bad weather, the harvest had been a failure. In the course of his sermon my father had said: "If the farmers were to say in present circumstances— Why should we give thanks, seeing that after all our labour we have gathered in hardly anything?' then the answer must be: 'Give thanks for the fact that you were able to work!' "

That is certainly a very simple thought, but the fact that just this thought had remained so firmly in the old man's memory shows how a quite simple thought can often strike people with surprise and shed light on their situation. "Give thanks that you were able to work!" How many a sick person would count himself fortunate if only he could work! And to-day above all it should be clear to us when we recall that so many of our friends who have been torn from their work would give much if only they were allowed to carry on with their normal job at home. Which of us can really doubt that the very fact of our being allowed to work is in itself a proof of God's goodness to us? And seeing that we are permitted to work, are we going to lament and complain that there are other people who are better off than ourselves? Often we do not realize how well off we in fact are!

I believe that to-day especially we need to take the lesson of our parable to heart. To-day a new social order is taking shape in our midst; and we are asked to be serious about it so that all inequalities in our social life, all class warfare, may disappear and a new community of the people based on social equality and equality of opportunity may be successfully established. Has our parable anything to say in opposition to this idea? Nothing at all! We have in fact already suggested that our text is not concerned to impart teaching about the social order. On the contrary it is quite right that in our human communal life we should be careful to achieve an order of society that is based on justice, and in which there will be given to every man who is capable of work and glad to work equal opportunities of prosperity and advancement. However here, as so often, evil disguises itself

I 86 as good; the claimful spirit of self-seeking and envy masks itself as the requirement of justice when in fact, instead of the right principle "No one should be worse off than I myself" is set up the false principle "No one should be better off than I myself"! Such is the language of self-seeking and envy.

And this selfish envious spirit is too easily inclined to forget that it is impossible to establish complete equality among men, that there will always be diversities among them which are quite simply grounded in the diversity of human endowment, gifts, and powers, and that no human organizational technique is capable of eliminating the differences which fate decrees. But the mystery of our destiny lies in the hands of God. And we often make the mistake of requiring from God what we should require from ourselves as regards the structural arrangements of human society. This we do when we compare ourselves with others and feel discontented because they are faring better than ourselves. Such behaviour is the mean and envious behaviour of the labourers in the parable. What this parable requires of us is freedom from envy, and the capacity to rejoice in the good which others enjoy. In point of fact we cannot for the most part make a true judgment as to whether others are really better off than ourselves. We see the outward aspect of things and we often do not see how much inner sorrow and distress may be concealed beneath a fair outward show.

And finally we forget so very easily that all the craft and the effort involved in human organizing is in vain without the blessing of God: "Unless the Lord builds the house, those who build it labour in vain. Unless the Lord watches over the city, the watchman stays awake in vain" (Ps. 127: 1). Immersed as we are in our work and our effort to achieve things, we forget that God's sovereign freedom also controls and disposes our human exertions and that in His freedom He can bring our work to prosperity, or lay it in ruins. And so long as we are faring well, we tend to ascribe our prosperity to our own skill and ability, and blind ourselves to God's goodness which wills to encounter and confront us both in prosperity and adversity.

> "I know that Thou art the eternal source,  
> The fountain of all goodness and all grace,  
> Whence flow to men, in never-failing course,  
> All joy and blessings from the heavenly place.  
> What are we, and what have we,  
> On this whole earth below  
> That springs not, Father God, from Thee  
> Who dost all good bestow?"

Who can sincerely speak in these terms? Only the man who acknowledges God's sovereign freedom, who opens his heart to the overflowing and free goodness of God, and does so by actively exercising kindness and generosity himself. The eyes of such a one are opened to the abounding grace of God which reigns over all things and prevails in all things. Thus too our parable, if we give heed to it with willing ears, will be seen as a gift of God's sovereign goodness disclosed in Jesus; a gift which shames us and frees us from the calculating coldness of our hearts. And so then let us take to heart the truth enshrined in the poet's words:1

> "Thou, like every child of man,  
> In this world hast no rights:  
> All the creatures God has made  
> Are guests at a strange hearth.  
> God is master in His house  
> And, as He wills, His gifts imparts."

> **[1]** Paul Gerhardt.  
> 188

## St. John 16:22–33

*May 30, 1943*

"So you have sorrow now, but I will see you again and your hearts will rejoice, and no one will take your joy from you. In that day you will ask me no questions. Truly, truly, I say to you, if you ask anything of the Father, he will give it to you in my name. Hitherto you have asked nothing in my name; ask, and you will receive, that your joy may be full.

"I have said this to you in figures; the hour is coming when I shall no longer speak to you in figures but tell you plainly of the Father. In that day you will ask in my name; and I do not say to you that I shall pray the Father for you; for the Father himself loves you, because you have loved me and have believed that I came from the Father. I came from the Father and have come into the world; again, I am leaving the world and going to the Father."

His disciples said, "Ah, now you are speaking plainly, not in any figure! Now we know that you know all things, and need none to question you; by this we believe that you came from God." Jesus answered them, "Do you now believe? The hour is coming, indeed it has come, when you will be scattered, every man to his home, and will leave me alone; yet I am not alone, for the Father is with me. I have said this to you, that in me you may have peace. In the world you have tribulation; but be of good cheer, I have overcome the world."

Our text has been appointed for to-day which is Rogation Sunday. "Rogate" means "pray". It is intended to show us the right way of prayer as Jesus taught it to His disciples in the hour of His farewell discourses. I do not mean to say that He actually instructed them in methods of prayer, but rather that He promised them that one day they would be able to pray aright and would in fact pray aright; one day, or more

'. What day is meant? This is the precisely "on that day" question we must first ask if we wish to understand what kind of prayer is being alluded to. For if it is granted us to experience "that day", then the right mode of prayer will spontaneously arise.

What day is meant by "that day"? The first verse of our text-certainly very familiar to many of us from listening to Brahms' Requiem-runs: "So you have sorrow now, but 1 will see you again and your hearts will rejoice, and no one will take your joy from you." These words were spoken before the event of Christ's passion, and the day of reunion which Christ promises is Easter Day. But it is Easter Day in a quite special context of meaning. The phrase "on that day" occurs twice in our text and we must remember that this expression held a very special and secret meaning for primitive Christianity. The expression was used without further elucidation

, i.e. the day of and its implication was "the day of the Lord" His return, the day on which this world would come to an end and a new heaven and earth would spring into being; the day on which would be fulfilled the promise: "Behold, I make all things new!" And the meaning of Jesus' words is that the "day" which spells an end to this world has through Easter Day mysteriously become a present reality for believers. The believer through his faith to some extent anticipates the dawning of that "day" on which all things will be made new. How is this possible? The "day" when all creation will be renewed is anticipated by the Christian believer who is himself regenerated in his personal being, as Paul says: "Therefore if any one is in Christ, he is a new creation; the old has passed away, behold, the new has come" (2 Cor. 5: 17).

Hence the first thing which our text teaches us is this: to be a Christian, to believe, means to have anticipated the time span of this world, to stand already at the terminal point of this age. For such a one true prayer is already a reality. But the promise of the gift of prayer is preceded by the promise of the gift of joy, of a joy "which no one will take from us"

. This implies a joy which is no longer threatened by the world, because the believer is as it were already

Igo removed from the world and has overcome it, as is suggested in the first letter of John: "This is the victory that overcomes the world, our faith" (5: 4).

Perhaps in these days we understand better than formerly the longing which the early Christians felt for "that day" the day spelling the end of time, the "dear last day" as Luther used to call it. Of this day Jesus says: "In that day you will ask me no questions", by which He means that then all tormenting questions will be silenced, all oppressive problems solved. In fact it is the very essence of joy that, when it envelops our being, questioning ceases; all becomes clear for us, self-explanatory. In an exposition of the words of Jesus about the lilies of the field and the birds of the air, Kierkegaard describes the latter as the great "teachers of joy" for the simple reason that they ask no questions and know no cares.

And is it suggested that this day, the "day" on which we shall no more ask questions, is to become for us a present reality? Is that joy which no one can take from us destined to be ours, we people who—especially just now—are filled with questioning and anxious care and every sort of distress? To be sure, gleams of joy and sunshine are granted us now and then. But we know too, as Hölderlin says: "Joy flashes across our path from time to time, but it does not properly belong to mortal beings." And we also understand the cry of the poet who in the moment of fulfilled joy implores death quickly to snatch him away: "Now am I blessed! pierce my heart now!" (C. F. Meyer); for the poet knows that the moment of joy will vanish, it will fade into the grey light of common day, life will once more be filled with questioning and anxious care.

### II

What then is the joy which no man can take from us? It is no joy connected with anything which this world and life within it can bestow, even the most precious and highest things of this world. It is a joy imparted by what lies beyond this world; and we experience it only when we stand beyond this world, when in our experience we have already foreshadowed "that day"

> ' the last day of earthly time.

No doubt we all long for a joy which no man can take from us. But do we wish for it in all seriousness? If so, then we must realize this: that we are aspiring to a joy which does not cling to this world. We must become clear about this noteworthy fact, namely that no one can tell us what is the true object of such joy. No one can tell us at what, on this level, we can and should rejoice. For everything that might be named as its object would still belong to this old world. And if for many centuries the pious have dreamed of such joy in their fanciful pictures of the heavenly city with its golden streets, and of the choirs and songs of the new Jerusalem where the blessed live for evermore, we must say that all such pictures are mere images and symbols for a joy that is inexpressible. For the ineffable joy which no man can take from us, no object can be named in human terms and concepts.

Or may perhaps one ideal object of such joy be formulated? Yes, there is one thing we can say: it is the joy of freedom—of freedom from the world, the joy that the questions and cares of the world can no longer concern us in our innermost being, because we no longer belong to the world. It is the joy of a freedom that may perhaps be under-stood if we reflect that once a message of understanding and forgiving love freed us from a heavy inner burden which we dragged around with us, a burden which lay oppressively on our minds and spirits and which tainted with a touch of bitterness even our happiest hours. What joyful relief it was for us when a word of divine forgiveness effaced our past and delivered us from its dead weight! We then rejoiced—-but at what? At nothing in particular, at nothing specific, simply over the fact that we were free. Or perhaps it has been our experience that we were once entangled in an unendurable situation, whether innocently or guiltily, and there came a day which set us free, whether by our own decision or by a favourable juncture of circumstances; the past sank away from us and we were free; we experienced a joyful sense of deliverance. We may have experienced such a thing ourselves, or if not, we can easily imagine how a person feels who can breathe again because he has found freedom from the past which fettered his spirit.

Hence we may perhaps easily understand, in the light of such human experience, the freedom implied by "that day" which ultimately frees us from all those things with which this world binds and burdens us. Does there not steal over us at times a longing for such freedom? A longing to be cleansed from all that stains and clouds our being? A longing for a unity of being through deliverance from all that confuses and divides our spirit? And that longing which sometimes we feel for the vanished past of our childhood, with its artless delights, is it not a longing for freedom from the confusions of this world?

But do we seriously desire such freedom? We must confess: this freedom is in its roots freedom from ourselves. For all with which the world exercises its hold over us, good and ill, pleasure and pain, gains its binding power from ourselves; from the fact that we have our wishes, our sick desires, our projects and plans, which the world sometimes meets with alluring hopes of fulfilment, sometimes crowns with a measure of success, sometimes severely suppresses and destroys. It is from the well of our own being that vexing questions and anxieties arise, because it is we who generate desires and structures of life. If "that day" is ever to come for us, the day on which we shall ask no further questions, then it must come as the day on which our self-will, the motivating force of our wishes and plans, grows silent; as the day on which the world for us sinks into nothingness.

But do we wish this? Or should we not rather ask: ought we to wish it? Do we not stand immersed in this world with all the obligations of duty too? In relationships which we do not regard as externally compulsive, but which we joyfully affirm? Relationships whose binding force springs from our gifts and endowment? And do we not above all stand in personal relationships with our own families and friends, with whom we are linked with the ties of love and trust, and for which we bear responsibility? Are not these obligations arising from our tasks in life and from our relations with our own, part and parcel of our very life? Ought we to seek release from them in order to gain divine freedom? At the present time should we repudiate our anxieties about our relations and friends who are fighting for us in dire distress and danger? Ought we to forget the sorrow that we bear in our hearts concerning those who have been taken from us? Our compassion with so many a one who is deeply afflicted? Would not the attainment of a freedom in which we are no longer moved by all this, amount to a subtle technique of withdrawing from the network of human relations, of retiring within oneself in a state of self-chosen isolation and seclusion, of rigid insensibility—we might even say inner deadness?

This cannot be what is meant by the words of Jesus. Otherwise He would not have been able to add that on "that day" we are still to pray and address our petitions to God. No! His promise concerns precisely those who stand involved in the complex relations of life, for in His highpriestly prayer He prays to God for His own: "I do not pray that Thou shouldst take them out of the world, but that Thou shouldst keep them from the evil one" (John 17: 15). It is not from the encounters of life that we should seek freedom but from ourselves; from the fact that in all these encounters we seek our own interests, holding fast to ourselves. Freedom from ourselves means a radical surrender of ourselves to God, and hence readiness for all that God sends us: openness to all the encounters of life.

### III

But one thing is certainly true: if we wish to experience the

', i.e. if we wish to joy of freedom which marks "that day". attain inner freedom from self as those who no longer belong to this world and yet are open to all the encounters of life, then we must be able to be alone in the presence of God. We must be ready to enter into a solitariness in which the world

194 fades away, in which all relationships, even the most binding and the dearest, are loosed and where we stand confronted by God alone. We must come into the presence of God bared and naked, divested of all to which we would fain cling, stripped of the rags with which we would cover ourselves, so that we might win an understanding and consciousness of our true being. This is a terrible, stark loneliness frightening to the natural man; and the God who meets us in such loneliness may wear to us the appearance of the annihilating power of death. We must be ready to endure His consuming glance, and to be nothing in His sight. We must be prepared as it were to leave the gaily-coloured and noisy world and to wander into the wilderness; for what R. M. Rilke says in his Book of Hours is indeed true of God: "Thou art the miracle in the desert which strikes lost and lonely exiles."

Let us remind ourselves once more of this: "that day" which spells freedom is also according to the word of Jesus Easter Day, the Day of Resurrection, the day which follows on the crucifixion. "You have sorrow now" —it is the sorrow of the passion, of the cross, by which all desires and hopes are destroyed. "But I will see you again and your hearts will rejoice." The triumphant certitude of the resurrection is to be bestowed on the grief of the cross. And only he who is ready to enter with Jesus into the dread hour of God-forsakenness on the cross, will also be allowed to share in the joy of the Risen Saviour. To share in the cross of Christ, however, means to surrender our wishes and plans to the will of God, so that we place under the shadow of the cross all that concerns us, both our hopes and our work. This means that we enter the ultimate solitude of man before God, and, as Paul says, that we decree the sentence of death on ourselves, so that we put our trust not in ourselves but in God who raises the dead (2 Cor. I: 9).

From such solitude we must return to our normal life; but the fact that we have tarried there and ever and again tarry there, must stamp our life with a distinctive character. If our time of withdrawal is honest and genuine, then we

195 shall return to the normal round of life more open and free than ever before, yet with the readiness to resign and sacrifice ourselves. It is such a spirit which shows that we have truly

", which proves that we are really experienced "that day" those who no more belong to this world. The spirit of readiness for renunciation and sacrifice! Will it not always be painful to see that which we had considered as our task in life threatened, suppressed, ruined? Will it not always be deeply painful to be wrenched from those with whom we are bound with the ties of love? Were these things not painful, they would not present a true sacrifice.

It might no doubt seem enticing to seek a position in life, the opportunity of which is provided by the Roman Catholic Church in its monastic system. There, man from the outset and once for all severs the connexions which bind him to the life of the world, so that nothing more can inwardly affect or wound him. Let us moreover be on our guard against too easily despising such an institution; for us Protestants it may always serve as a warning sign that we too as Christians are required to sever ourselves in spirit from the world, and to stand before God in naked solitariness. But we feel bound to add that this requirement is not truly fulfilled in monasticism. In monasticism ultimate freedom through self-surrender to God is not attained, because such freedom must prove itself in complete openness to the encounters of life. When man withdraws himself utterly from these encounters, he is betraying a secret fear of them and there is no fear in freedom.

If we wish to retain a complete openness towards the encounters of life, we shall of course also be ready for the pains and sorrows which are inseparable from all true sacrifice. But we shall be without fear, for we shall realize that pain and sorrow constantly lead us into the solitariness of confrontation by God where the miracle of genuine encounter with God occurs, where the certitude of the Easter faith rises triumphant; out of the darkness of death we pass into the light of life. And so in the midst of all pain a secret joy is characteristic of the Christian spirit.

ıg6

But perhaps all that I have been saying is only a matter of fine phrases? Is such Christian existence, an existence in both pain and sorrow, an existence which is no longer rooted in this world, is it at all possible or is it merely a beautiful fantasy? Is not the promise of resurrection from the dead a perplexing and mysterious message for us?

Jesus anticipates such questions: "I have said this to you in figures" or more precisely translated "in enigmas". For to him who does not dare to believe, the promise is a conundrum, a hollow sounding word. "The hour is coming when I shall no longer speak to you in figures, but tell you plainly of the Father." This does not mean that later Jesus Will say something new and different from what He has already said; it does mean that these puzzling words will seem transparently clear and true. This is shown straightaway by the remark of the disciples. Before Jesus says anything different from what He has already said, namely that He came from the Father and is returning to the Father, they remark: "Ah, now you are speaking plainly, and not in any figure!" The disciples speak thus from a faith which anticipates the fulfilment of the promise.

And what is Jesus' reply? "Do you now believe? The hour is coming, indeed it has come, when you will be scattered..." Hence He appears to attach but little importance to the confession of faith. But no, we should not understand it in that way! What we are to learn is rather this: a quick impulsive confession of faith may mean very little. Solid faith grows only in the experiences of life and especially in the experience of despair. And to this experience of despair the final word of His message applies: "I have said this to you, that in me you may have peace. In the world you have tribulation; but be of good cheer, I have overcome the world."

This is no cheap word of comfort, as though Jesus' own traversing of death and resurrection had spared us all sorrow and despair. It is rather a comfort and a warning at the same time— warning which must lead to comfort, to the only comfort possible; that we should take up our pain and despair as a sharing of the cross of Christ, that we should take it into the heart of our solitariness before God, and so gain a part in Christ's victory over the world.

Hence to experience "that day" means: in following Christ to take up His cross and to penetrate to that depth of life where we are alone in God's presence, and thus to become as one who no more belongs to this world. To such a one the right way of prayer is granted.

What then is the right way of prayer? We need not speak much longer on this theme, for the fact is that we have been speaking of it essentially all the time! "If you ask anything of the Father, He will give it to you in my name. Hitherto you have asked nothing in my name." The right way of prayer is prayer in the name of Christ. The disciples had prayed much and often already before the passion and Easter, but not 'in His name". They had prayed: "Thy kingdom come, give us this day our daily bread" and all that belongs to this context. They are not now to offer a different prayer, but they are to pray differently. "In the name of Jesus" means in the light of His cross and His victory over the world. No doubt they are meant to pour forth to God the sorrows and the desires of their heart, but as men who are inwardly prepared for renunciation and sacrifice, and who are therefore without fear or dismay. Can then the promise that God will grant us our requests simply mean that we can count on the fulfilment of all our wishes? Certainly not! Yet in a deeper sense, yes. For if we pray in His name, that is, in freedom from the bondage of self, then in all that we ask for we are at bottom praying for encounter with God, for the life which God bestows. To pray in the name of Jesus means to pray as Jesus prayed in the garden of Gethsemane, to accompany every prayer by the qualification: "Nevertheless not my will, but Thine, be done."

For such as have experienced "that day", prayer of this character is a matter of course. For these are they who have penetrated to that centre of life where they are alone in the presence of God, and this loneliness itself becomes for them a prayer. True prayer in the name of Jesus implies that with Him we enter consoledly the region of solitary encounter with God, in whose sight we are aware of standing and by whom we allow ourselves to be confronted. "In the world you have tribulation; but be of good cheer, I have overcome the world." This solitariness of the soul can itself become a prayer, a cry of the heart for God:

"It may be that I pierce through rugged mountain sides, through harsh veins, like ore, alone; so deep I delve that I see no end, no distant view; all has become near, all nearness hard as stone. Yet I am not experienced in the ways of sorrow, and this entombing darkness makes me small. But Thou art; may the weight of Thy glory burst upon me, the weight of Thy whole hand smite me, and I with all my treasure fall prostrate before Thee."1

Of such prayer it is true to say: "In prayer we rise to Him as from the bands of death. Sorely our heart needed His breath which breathed around us." 2

Whoever practises such prayers goes back to the round of daily life, comforted and thankful that with all the impulses of his heart he may open himself to God without reserve. Such a one will be accompanied by the prayer of solitude in all life's encounters; it will impart to him that freedom and joy in the inspiration of which he will no longer need to ask questions.

In conclusion let us think of those words of Paul: "Three times I besought the Lord about this... but He said to me 'My grace is sufficient for you, for my power is made perfect in weakness.' " Was this an answer to his prayer or not? Paul understood it to be an answer: "I will all the more gladly boast of my weaknesses, that the power of Christ may rest upon me. For the sake of Christ, then, I am content with weaknesses, insults, hardships, persecutions, and calamities;

> **[2]** A. v. Arnim.

> **[1]** R. M. Rilke.

for when I am weak, then I am strong" (2 Cor. 12: 8-10). Here we see at work the freedom of him who, as a new man in Christ, no more belongs to this old world which passes away. Here we see manifested freedom from fear and dismay through the fullness of self-surrender to God. Have we got so far? Oh that we might reach this stage of Christian devotion! And if we do not dare to join in the triumphant words of Paul, then let us echo from our hearts the plain words of Matthias Claudius:

> "Lord, grant us to behold Thy majesty  
> And trust not in the world that fades away,  
> Nor boast ourselves on earth of vanity:  
> Make us and keep us pure in heart, we pray.  
> Like joyous children may we be,  
> Walking Thy ways, our hope in Thee!"

## St. Matthew 5:3–10

*December 12, 1943*

"Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven.

"Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted. "Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth. "Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied.

"Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy. "Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God. "Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God.

"Blessed are those who are persecuted for righteousness' sake, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven." THe essential meaning of the beatitudes is their implication of a promise. For this reason they form a most suitable text for the season of Advent; for the Advent message is the message of a promise. A promise for those who are oppressed by adversity and cry aloud for help; for those who "walk in darkness" and long for light. To all such the promise rings out: "Blessed are ye!" "Salvation will be yours!" For to them the future is to bring help and healing; they are to be comforted and filled; they are to see God; the kingdom of heaven is theirs.

Do we belong to the circle of those to whom these promises apply? Among the beatitudes there is one by which we feel particularly moved to-day, and which we wish had been addressed directly to ourselves: "Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted!" For who is there among us who to-day is not a mourner, longing for some comfort? Perhaps some of us have recently heard these words sung to the music of Brahms' Requiem and were moved to tears. But may we isolate any one of the beatitudes and apply it specially to ourselves in this way? They belong integrally together and one of them is a message for us in particular only if all are. Certainly we are all mourners; but are we also those who are poor in spirit, who hunger and thirst after righteousness, who are merciful and pure in heart?

### I

Let us first examine the individual beatitudes and consider whether we are among those addressed.

"Blessed are the poor in spirit." What does it mean to be poor in spirit? It means to realize one's poverty in the presence of God. No doubt in virtue of our essential nature as men we are all poor in the sight of God, helpless and frail, weak and perishable mortals. The point is, however—do we realize this fact? Do we realize it, not in the sense of theoretical knowledge, but in such a way that this awareness penetrates our whole being and informs our whole way of life?

To be poor in spirit means not to conceal the essential poverty of our human nature by the apparent wealth which a man may acquire. It may be a question of outward prosperity; as Luther says, to be poor in spirit means "a heart which does not cleave to property and wealth, but, even when a man has it, it is for him as though he had it not". Whether we are spiritually poor in this sense may be tested by consideration of another comment of Luther: "It is not that a man is asked to leave house and home, wife and child, and wander through the land to be a burden to others ... but the meaning rather refers to the Christian who can in heart leave house and home, wife and child, even though he actually remains with them, sups with them, and serves them out of love, as God has commanded; and yet his disposition is such that, when need arises, he can for the fulfilment of God's will abandon it all at any time. If you are so minded, then you have left all, so let not your heart cleave to earthly things, but remain pure from greed, and free from too much attachment to this world... and in fact a rich man may wish to be spiritually poor yet he is not for that reason permitted to throw away his goods, unless necessity forces him to do so, in which case he is prepared to abandon them in the name of God; and then it is not that he wants to abandon house and home, wife and child, he would rather cling to them so long as God permits and serve God thereby, yet he is also ready for resignation when God wills to take them from him." To-day we are all involved in a situation in which we must be prepared for God to take away from us what He gave, and for many of our friends and relations this has already happened. How stands it with us? Are we resigned to God's will?

We may also be tempted to conceal our essential poverty by the wealth of our mind and moral dispositions, by the consciousness of what we are able to achieve, in fact by the consciousness of our courageous readiness for self-sacrifice; by the pride which trusts in one's own strength of will and moral resources, by the sense of security which a man wins through purposeful work and devotion to those values which lend to a human life dignity and distinction. But has such pride, such self-security, any longer its convincing power in these days when our world, in which we dwelt with a sense of pride and security, is falling into ruins before our eyes? Does not such an attitude now stand out as the arrogance of man who forgets that he is poor, that he is futile?

"Blessed are the poor in spirit"—the word is intended to comfort the poor who realize their poverty. But it does not comfort them by reinterpreting their poverty as riches; it does not mean: in the awareness of your poverty resides your wealth! There is an attitude of spiritual superiority which derives from the consciousness of penetrating the essential vanity of all things; there is a spirit of sceptical irony which looks with a scornful compassion on the busy and tumultuous affairs of men; there is a condescending type of humour which surveys the bustle of the human scene with cold detachment. It is true that of all human attitudes such irony and humour stand nearest to the Christian faith. But the spiritually poor are not they who from a safe distance ironically or humorously contemplate the course of events; for in such an attitude there still lurks the pride of man who does not need God and for whom the promise: "Yours is the kingdom of heaven" has no meaning. The poor in spirit to whom the kingdom of heaven is promised are they who look forward to the future which God will bring about-though not, of course, a worldly future, not a brilliant compensation for all they have lost. No, something quite different, something which transcends the things of this world: "What no eye has seen, nor ear heard, nor the heart of man conceived, what God has prepared for those who love Him."

The poor in spirit are they who wait upon the Lord; they who wait for God's future, because they know that here and now nothing can give them satisfaction, those who by such an attitude of waiting on God have inwardly freed themselves from their attachment to the here and now; such stand in an attitude of waiting which silences all desires.

Blessed are they who wait! This is also the meaning of the second beatitude: "Blessed are those who mourn, for they shall be comforted." Yes, this is the message which we are to-day most eager to relate to our own position. But again it is not sorrow as such to which God's message of healing and deliverance applies; it is a question of understanding rightly the meaning of this word. All sorrow and pain is intended to make us aware how insecure is the ground on which we stand; in every individual case of distress and pain there is but manifested the deep underlying world agony which envelops humanity as a whole. In every loss that we suffer we see the evidence of that power making for transience, the power of death to which we are all subject. Do we under-stand our sorrows in this light? For the most part they strike us as something strange and offensive, astonishing and frightening. We say, bewildered, "No, that cannot be!" and fail to recognize that it is but the common human lot. In spite of all that is beautiful and uplifting, human life, as the ancient poet Pindar says, is but a shadowy dream. We go our way as over a surface of ice, which at any moment may break.

Here again there is an attitude which is similar to the Christian one, and yet differs from it. It is the attitude of the old Greek sage, who, when the death of his son was announced to him, replied: "I realized that I had given birth to a mortal being." And the stoic sage advises us as follows: "What evil is there if, while kissing your little child, you whisper: To-morrow you may be dead? Or similarly in speaking to your friend: To-morrow either you or I will have vanished, perhaps, and we may never see each other again?" Certainly by this awareness we can train ourselves to survey as from a distance whatever happens to us, to remain in our deepest being unmoved by any human tragedy, so that when fate strikes us we are impervious and invulnerable to its blows. But these stoically-minded people are not they who mourn and whom Jesus extols as blessed. The fact is that they do not take upon themselves the burden of pain and sorrow; rather they flee from it by making themselves insensible and impassive. No, those are blessed who willingly embrace sorrow, who bear pain; who are not affrighted as if something most strange and offensive had befallen them, but who realize that in their pain they are sharing in the unfathomable pain which pierces all humanity to its depths, and who by such realization are freed to direct their gaze to the future; who wait for God's consolation. "God will wipe away every tear from their eyes, and death shall be no more, neither shall there be mourning nor crying nor pain any more, for the former things have passed away" (Rev. 21: 4). How this will come to pass, we can have no idea; but the very renunciation of understanding is an essential part of the attitude of waiting.

There follows the beatitude: "Blessed are the meek, for they shall inherit the earth."
