We pass it over; for it does not fit into the context, since it does not speak of the frailty, the pain and the longings of humanity; moreover textual tradition makes it seem likely that the verse was added later. The subsequent verse, however, does speak of the longings of humanity: "Blessed are those who hunger and thirst for righteousness, for they shall be satisfied."

These words can be easily misunderstood, since by the word "righteousness" we normally understand a certain virtue, that of impartiality or integrity, moral blamelessness of life. Understood on these lines, the beatitude would have the same meaning as the words of Goethe: "Him who ever manfully toils, we can redeem." In the Biblical context, however, righteousness has a different sense; those are praised as blessed whom God justifies, whom God acknowledges, whom God accredits. The promise is addressed to those who suffer not merely from the frailty and pain of human life in general, but from their own sense of inferiority and unworthiness. The reference is to those who are oppressed by the feeling that they never can wholly be what they would like to be, that neither before others nor before God can they appear and present themselves as what in their deepest being they truly are, that their essential being is always neutralized by weakness and guilt, that they can never give themselves as they really intend, and so always suffer from the feeling of being misunderstood. In fact is there not in all of us this yearning desire to be able to give clear and effective expression to our essential being and our deepest will, to stand forth in the sight of all and in God's sight as we feel that we might be? Yes, this desire is latent in all men. Yet often it is suppressed or distorted into a tense and nervous striving to bring one's merit to light, to thrust oneself forward by achievement or pose, by a convulsive effort to make an impression on others, and in that impression to find a mirror of one's true self.

Oh that we were freed from this urge! That we could express ourselves spontaneously as we are! That our being could unfold itself effectively and purely, and give a true reflection of itself in the eyes of all! No doubt there is also an attitude of resigned abandonment of effort, of contempt for human judgments in general, the withdrawal of man into himself in proud self-isolation from his fellows. And this attitude may well suggest a certain strength of soul, and compel our respect. But the beatitude is not meant for it. Christ's blessing is addressed to those who wait for God Himself to bestow upon them that for which they long, for God to free them from the frustrations which shackle them, as He meets them and acknowledges them in their true

206 being. It is for those who realize that nothing can be forced and who wait patiently for God, as the Psalmist says, "to bring forth their vindication as the light" and their "right as the noonday" (Ps. 37: 6).

LII

Thus it is those who wait upon God who are extolled in the first group of beatitudes. Four beatitudes follow which suggest how a genuine attitude of waiting bears fruit and must become a living power in man's life. For those who know how to wait upon God, the being of man is released from the present, from the given, from its connexion with what he has and is. It turns towards the future, towards what God will grant, what God will make of man. Thus man is released for all the encounters which God will send him and in which God's future will come to meet him.

"Blessed are the merciful, for they shall obtain mercy." This does not need many words of explanation; we need only point out that the word translated "merciful" has in truth a wider meaning; it implies not only compassion but (in general) goodness, active beneficent love. Blessed are they whose heart is inspired by such creative goodness. They shall obtain mercy. Yes, they are privileged to perceive the mercy of God active towards them in that they are allowed to experience how the man who is not embittered despite poverty, pain, and unfulfilled longing, the man who is readily accessible to the pain and sorrow of his neighbourhow such a one easily bears his own sorrows.

"Blessed are the pure in heart, for they shall see God." Who is pure in heart? What is meant is the disposition of honesty, of inner truthfulness, which refuses to impose either on itself or others, which hates all pretence and falsity; the honourableness and openness of disposition which has no unconfessed ulterior motives, which will not adopt a pose but offers itself in spontaneous naturalness to others. What we call purity in a more specialized sense is included in this disposition. For all impure thoughts and sick passions ever

207 go hand in hand with furtiveness. And when they do dare to display themselves with cynical frankness, then their victim is in truth play-acting with a spurious contorted heroism, and deceives himself at least. But the Christian who is free with the freedom of waiting for God, finds all such pretence and mummery repugnant.

"Blessed are the peacemakers, for they shall be called sons of God." The peacemakers are those who are always ready to maintain peace or to lay the foundations of peace; those who, with their tranquil trust in God, are so inwardly detached from what they possess or control that they are not anxiously concerned about their rights, nor do they obstinately insist on their rights. Their inner disposition is so noble that they are ready to relax their claims; they can smilingly let slide what to others seems immensely important, and with a smile and a shrug they can bear to be called weak.

And finally: "Blessed are those who are persecuted for righteousness' sake, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven." Those who are inwardly detached from the transitory will be able with their whole power to devote themselves to the things that are abiding, to the beautiful, the true and the good, and in so doing they will be without anxiety about any suffering that may befall them. In the service of the eternal and the unseen they gladly take upon themselves the burden of hatred and persecution. Whenever it is a question of God or the world, they are unhesitating in making a decision which brings them freedom.

What is promised to the merciful, to the pure in heart, to the peacemakers, to those who are persecuted for righteousness' sake, is always the same: they shall obtain mercy, they shall see God, they shall be called the children of God, the kingdom of heaven is theirs. All this has the same meaning. God prevents them and holds them in His everlasting love, draws them into fellowship with Himself, and bestows upon them that which brings fulfilment to their lives.

We have suggested that the four last beatitudes show how the attitude of genuine trust in God proves itself as a living

208 force. Prayerful waiting upon God bears fruit in man's inner detachment and freedom, with the kindness and honesty which flow from it; in man's peaceableness of disposition and readiness to suffer for the good. And now conversely it should be said that all this is only possible on the basis of genuine trust. These fruits of the spirit are not something deliberately cultivated or forced, they grow naturally in the soul of the man whose life is rooted in his trustful waiting on God.

HII

The one who waits! Thus may we characterize in general terms the life of the Christian; the description fits him just as does that which qualifies him as the viator, the pilgrim. In this connexion the thought of Jung-Stilling occurs to our minds: "Blessed are the homesick; for they shall return to their homes!" The Christian is essentially a pilgrim who is engaged in a journey, who has turned his back on the old world and is directing his steps to the future which God will create. And for the same reason we may describe him as the one who waits.

But is not this attitude characteristic of all men as such? Is not every man at heart filled with discontent about the present and expectation of the future? Is it not part of man's essential being never to be able to say to the present moment: "Tarry, I pray, thou art so beautiful!" Is it not essentially human to be ever occupied with a tense expectation of the coming moment that shall bring us satisfaction? No doubt we often deceive ourselves about this, and many a time it may seem to us that we experience a moment of fulfilment to which we would like to hold fast. But if such a feeling does not rest simply on the suppression and temporary oblivion of our cares, which will soon assail us again, then in a really fulfilled moment it is only apparently so. For such a moment always contains the promise of a future, and fills us precisely with tense expectancy.

We human beings are all of us expectant. The difference between us is, however, whether we picture the future in fantasies born of our desires and dreams, or whether our waiting for the future is so radical that we renounce all such dreams and yet are cheerful in our confident waiting. Do we step forward with confident trust into a future which is shrouded in darkness for us, because our hope is set on God's future, which surpasses all that we can desire or conceive?

To be a Christian means to be one who waits for God's future. Hence for the Christian perhaps all seasons are essentially an Advent season. For Advent is characterized above all by this note of expectation. Yes, so it is, and yet it seems good to distinguish the season of Advent in the round of the Church's year as the special time of expectation. It is intended to remind us sharply of what we so easily and so often forget, namely that, as Christians, we are expectant. But also it should make us more certain of the fulfilment of the promise, for it leads us on to that feast which is the very symbol of fulfilment, the Feast of the Holy Nativity. Is it merely a symbol of fulfilment? Or is it also already fulfilment itself, in so far as fulfilment is possible in the context of our earthly pilgrimage?

Yes, it is certainly fulfilment provided we celebrate the feast in its true sense, by opening our hearts to the message of the divine love which is rooted in the birth of Jesus Christ. It depends on us, whether Christmas will signify for us a real fulfilment. The more difficult it may seem to us this year to keep Christmas with the joy normally associated with it, so much the more should we remind ourselves that the true Christian joy is promised to those who wait upon God. And so all the heaviness which lies on our hearts may serve to make us realize that we are the expectant ones who trust in God. Thus we shall be prepared to receive the true Christmas joy which will transform our human expectancy into a trustful, glad and consoled waiting for the gift of God's future.

210

## 2 Corinthians 4:6–11

*June 17, 1945*

For it is the God who said, "Let light shine out of darkness" who has shone in our hearts to give the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Christ.

But we have this treasure in earthen vessels, to show that the transcendent power belongs to God and not to us. We are afflicted in every way, but not crushed; perplexed, but not driven to despair; persecuted, but not forsaken; struck down, but not destroyed; always carrying in the body the death of Jesus, so that the life of Jesus may also be manifested in our bodies. For while we live we are always being given up to death for Jesus' sake, so that the life of Jesus may be manifested in our mortal flesh. THe cruel distress of the times through which we are living fills our hearts with anxious questioning as we wonder how we are to master things; it awakens in our minds desires and hopes, it drives us to plan and scheme in the effort to surmount our difficulties. "Master things", "surmount our difficulties"—such phrases we all no doubt understand primarily in the sense of succeeding in putting our troubles behind us, of pulling through and extricating ourselves from our adversities; of building a better future, if not for ourselves, at least for our children. And of course such anxieties, such considerations and hopes ought to have a place in our minds; but our text teaches us that anxieties and hopes on this plane should not be the first and most important thing with us, that the true victory over our distresses is an inner victory; not a future victory but an effectively present one. The point is not how we are to remove our trials but how we are to bear them steadfastly; not how we are to banish them, but how we are to control and deal with them; not how happy we shall be when they are passed, but how in the very

2II midst of them we may have the peace which passes under-standingthis must be the first question.

Whoever succeeds in achieving this present spiritual victory will also be the most capable in working at the task of building for the future; for such a one will not be in turns excited and depressed, but will accomplish his work with inner serenity and cheerfulness of spirit. But who is it, who conscious of living on this victorious level can echo the words of Paul: "We are afflicted in every way, but not crushed; perplexed, but not driven to despair"?

1

The spiritual strength of Paul springs from the fact that he lives in two worlds; not only in the visible world of change and decay, of tears and death, that world in which we are "afflicted in every way" and "perplexed"

, but also in the invisible world in which there is no fear and no despair. From this invisible world he derives the power to master all those pains and trials which flow from the visible and temporal. In fact we may go further and add that the trials of the visible and temporal scene serve to make him all the more certain of invisible realities: "We have this treasure in earthen vessels, to show that the transcendent power belongs to God and not to us."

The fact that man lives in two worlds is not a distinctively Christian insight. Everywhere and at every time in which man has transcended his anxiety about his bodily existence, about the material security of his life, has opened the "eye of the soul" and in inner spiritual vision has caught a glimpse of the world of the spirit, then he has lived in two worlds and then two worlds have dwelt in his breast. At such moments the world of the spirit, the world of beauty, truth and goodness shines upon him in its inspiring splendour and instils into him the sense of utter obligation towards it. He learns to understand that it is not the exigencies of the passing day, the question of the useful and profitable which constitutes his essential and true being, but solely the question of what is good and noble. He learns that it is not power but right which lends to human society its salutary order and dignity. And he comes to experience the joy that is granted him when he uses the power of thought and insight not only in the service of the practical ends of life, but also and above all when he uses and trains them to grasp the truth, to under-stand the world as it really is and himself as he really is. He learns to know the joy of brightening the prosaic hues of everyday life with the radiance of artistic achievements, of decorating life with the garlands of the beautiful.

But man lives at the same time in the visible world which is ruled by sheer hunger and brutal power. Nature seems to conspire against the world of the spirit. Material needs, want and sickness, rob the spirit of its freedom and subdue it to the world of the physical. The question as to what is good is superseded by the question as to what is useful. The understanding of truth and knowledge is exploited by the technology of practical life aims. The hours of uplifting joy in the contemplation of beauty grow few, and art no longer serves the delight and strengthening of the spirit but is prostituted in the service of entertainment and distraction. And we must go further and say that human society itself seems to be hostile to the life of the spirit. Right cannot be maintained without power, and how often does power come before right! How much better fare those people who are concerned with questions of the profitable rather than the good! So often does the lie triumph over truth, ambition over the tranquil surrender to the recognition of what is true and the ideal of beauty. . Thus man is constantly pressed by the temptation to abandon that life in two worlds which so often tears his breast and instead to follow purely practical life aims. He is faced by the decision: is he to yield to the absolute obligations with which the world of the spirit confronts him and in its service to shoulder a double burden of torment and trial? Is he to preserve the faith that he lives his true essential life only in the world of the spirit? At all times there have been men—and even before Christ-who in the claim of the spirit have heard the voice of God; men for whom there was no doubt that only faithful devotion to the invisible world of the spirit could endow life with dignity and nobility; men who bore within themselves the consciousness of an inner peace of which neither the fierce hostility of nature nor the wickedness of man could rob them. And there were also men who spoke of these things in paradoxes similar to those of Paul. The description of the true sage as the Stoic Epictetus describes it can be an admonishment and summons even to the Christian:

> "In sickness and yet blessed,  
> In dangers and yet blessed,  
> In death and yet blessed,  
> In exile and yet blessed,  
> In disgrace and yet blessed."

### II

This description of the true sage is of course not yet the description of the true Christian as the words of Paul picture it for us. Yet there exists between the two ideals a characteristic affinity; it is the affinity between idealism and Christianity. A dangerous affinity, we may and indeed must say! For it has often led to a confusion between Christianity and idealism, and for this reason, especially in recent times, it has caused the Church to attack idealism. But to-day above all it would be disastrous, just because of this danger, to fail to recognize this inner kinship. Let us consider. Was it not one and the selfsame time in which Nietzsche's cry rang out: "God is dead!" and in which faith in the world of the spirit died in our culture? The gospel addresses itself to man; but what alone makes man truly human is his participation in the world of the spirit. And as, in the Pauline phrase, the Mosaic

, so also 1s law was formerly a "custodian until Christ came" idealism. Naturally, as the Judaic law was misused by man to enable him to establish his own righteousness in the sight of God, the same possibility of abuse exists with regard to idealism.

There has been much reflection about the nature of the relationship between Christianity and idealism, and to-day especially we should ponder these matters anew. Just now we will only say, quite briefly: the gospel spells the rejection of the world of the spirit if man sees in that world a structure which he himself has raised, the expression of his own creative power and perfection. But the gospel signifies the confirmation of the world of the spirit, if man recognizes in that world the divine authority under which he stands, and if he does not attempt to hide the fact that he can never satisfy the claim of this authority. And this not only in the sense of his realizing that he is never yet what he should be, but also in the sense of his realizing that he is always already what he should not be. He is guilty, not only in the sense that ever increasing obligations exceed the measure of his fulfilment, but also in the sense that he has rendered himself guilty by showing himself to be unworthy of these high duties. He is constantly frustrated not only by the imperfection of all things earthly in which the ideal can never find pure expression, but also by his own doing, by his own past which makes him unclean in the face of the holy. His conscience accuses him. He belongs to two worlds: his true and better self belongs to the world of the spirit, but that fact may not and cannot justify him or quieten the accusations of his conscience so long as he is bound to confess that this his true self has not really become his own but ever hovers before him as an unattained ideal. His empirical ego is in fact the battle-ground of the struggle between the powers of the spirit and the lower impulses. His ego is rent in twain; it lacks unity and purity. It needs cleansing, purification from the stains of the old man, emancipation from its disharmonies, regeneration; in a word, it needs divine grace.

And it is just this which has become luminous for Paul in the face of Jesus Christ: the grace of God which has made him a new creature. "For it is the God who said, 'Let light shine out of darkness'

who has shone in our hearts to give the light of the knowledge of the glory of God in the face of Christ."

As the new man in Christ, Paul of course remains involved

215 in the two worlds as long as he leads his life on earth. But this life has acquired for him a new rich meaning: life in the spirit is also life in the grace of God, and so in a new sense too he has become victorious over life in the visible world.

### III

To-day there is a quite special reason to enlarge on the theme that the gospel signifies a confirmation of the world of the spirit, that grace does not cancel the claim of the ideal but brings out its validity, and at the same time bestows the power to live in obedience to this claim in inner harmony of life. The reason is that in recent years we have learnt what an inner, and then later, outer catastrophe befalls a people when it loses its faith in the ideal world, and rejects the duty of obedience to that world; when in fact the ideal and the spiritual are replaced by false doctrines.

For this is the meaning of what has happened to our nation! We have been told that it is not right but power which establishes order in the life of a people and of the world; that the sole meaning of right is what is expedient for the nation. The ideal of humanity has been despised; the moral law and the claims of conscience have been depreciated as the discoveries of a slavish disposition. The only valid obligation, we have heard, arises not from the world of the spirit, but from blood and soil, and is nothing else than the will to self-assertion and power springing from the race. And just as the sphere of the right and of the moral law was destroyed, so was also the sphere of truth. We have been taught that there is no such thing as absolute truth, and that every insight is only a vision of things determined by the unconscious impulse and will of the blood. There should be no impartial search for truth, no investigation undeterred by whatever its findings happen to be; all knowledge is to be harnessed to the service of the will to power. All knowledge must serve political and technological purposes. Finally the sphere of beauty was also destroyed. Art was no longer considered as the embodiment of the eternal ideal of the beautiful, but as a creation of the racial will and sentiment; and instead of leading to feelings of exaltation and the sense of the sublime, it was enrolled in the service of political propaganda.

I do not now say all this in order to launch a cheap accusation against a system which has collapsed, but in order to warn all men to open their eyes and see on what a precipitous slope modern life in general has been moving. The false doctrines of the "national-socialist" system are only an extreme radical consequence of that development which has been taking place in recent decades, in fact during the whole of the past century. The belief in spiritual values has faded more and more. A nationalistic will to power has increasingly controlled political thought. Considerations of utility, of practical advancement and profit, have increasingly moulded education, the choice of a profession and the conduct of life in general. The question of what a young man should learn has been more and more considered from the point of view: can it be of use to him later in life? That has been asked rather than such primary questions as: Will it develop his mind? Will it give to his life a content independent of practical success, a content which will guard him against temptation and raise him above the vexations and disappointments of life? How often has there been talk of a knowledge which is remote from the practical concerns of life! It easily occurs to everyone that knowledge deviates from life and chases vain fantasies. But to whom does it occur that life itself may often in one way or another stray from the truth which in the realm of knowledge is honoured as guide?

A life which is successful from the practical point of view is far from being a life which deserves to be called a genuinely human life. And knowledge is not remote from life simply because it yields no results of apparent practical consequence, and because its exponents appear in the eyes of the so-called virtuosos of life to be unpractical dreamy men. Aloofness from the world may also be an honour if the world voices the criticism! And in the concerns of unpractical knowledge true human life may be better preserved than in the life of business acumen. But what a falling off there has been in our faith in the spirit, in the spirit as the power which alone confers on man his nobility, which makes him inwardly happy even in the midst of outward want and distress!

And the life of faith, of religion, what of that? How often have we heard the criticism: what have the Churches done for the life of the people, the life of civilization? As though the meaning of the Church, of faith, were to achieve some practical end within the sphere of the visible world! As though it were not the very essence of religion to be for men an asylum, a sanctuary, to which they might withdraw from the tumult of the visible world in the quest of meditation and devotion, of quiet and prayer! A sanctuary to which man might turn for refuge from the sound and fury of the everyday world, a sanctuary in which God draws near to him, and in whose stillness he derives the power to live in the unseen, eternal world; the power in the inspiration of which he can say: "We are afflicted in every way, but not crushed; perplexed, but not driven to despair; persecuted, but not forsaken; struck down, but not destroyed."

### IV

Have we in fact pursued this as a conscious aim? Have we troubled to fit into our lives such times of quiet and meditation, which stand out from the routine and bustle of our daily work? Have we admitted to ourselves that we are the denizens of two worlds and have we asked ourselves in which of these worlds our true home lies? Do we desire to be at home in this world of the spirit, to be rooted in the world of grace? That is the whole point.

Our time-and here lies its blessing-may well restore to us the consciousness that we live in two worlds, a consciousness both painful and consoling. Certainly the visible world constantly tends to crowd out the invisible from our minds; the inner world of the spirit is ever in danger of being stifled by the external world. But all the more is it our duty to believe in the invisible world of the spirit and of grace, to cling to faith in it. If we live in the pain and distress of these days without that inner treasure which the eternal world of the spirit and of grace bestows, then we are lost and must despair. But if we realize that our highest and truest life consists in this inner treasure of spiritual life, then in all our darkness we have a light, in all the agony of our time we have a solid ground of hope. Do we wish with all our hearts to belong to this world of the spirit and of grace?

A period of history in which the spirit has been despised and the word of God's grace reviled lies behind us. The world of the spirit has once more gained an opportunity of ex-pansion, and the word of God's grace may be freely proclaimed. And even now we occasionally have the shaming experience of hearing people complain, in face of the sufferings which we are still undergoing and must undergo for some time to come, that we were better off in the days of the old régime and should be better off now if it were still in force. We meet people who would be ready to exchange the intellectual freedom which has been restored to us, the freedom from falsehood and infamy, for material well-being. This attitude is surely the lowest stage to which a man can sink. No! "Man shall not live by bread alone but by every word that proceeds from the mouth of God" (Matt. 4: 4).

But the truth is that distress and want have hit people so hard that we understand when they despair, and we do not wish to condemn them harshly. And our challenge: do we wish to live in the world of the spirit?—might well be put as follows: are we able to live in that world? Is not the life of so many of our people who have lost all their possessions and their loved ones, but a hollow worthless shell? Are not all their thoughts and abilities so fully claimed and absorbed by the effort to secure the barest necessities of life that no time or power is left them to live in the world of the spirit? Are we not threateningly faced by the coming winter which will surely bring us cold and hunger?

The essential answer to these questions is that in order to live the interior life of the spirit no special time or ability is required. Of course suffering and distress may be so acute that time and resource fail for peaceful pursuits whether in the realms of knowledge or art. But everyone can live the life of the spirit who remains faithful to the good and the true; and this we can do whatever the distress and hardship of the present hour. Anyone may experience the strengthening power of the spirit who fulfils his duties with such loyalty. It must not be the self-seeking urge to snatch as much as possible for oneself, to save one's own skin at all costs, which determines the course of our daily life, but rather the sway of honesty and kindness in our hearts even in the face of cruel hardship. How much easier could men make their own lot if they met each other in the spirit of friendliness and good will, were prepared to allow to the other fellow his due, and to bear with patience the misunderstandings which are unavoidable! How unnecessarily hard men make life for themselves by their impatience and bitterness, their unfriendliness and lovelessness! But whoever remains faithful to the life of the spirit will always find such as share his faith. Such faith is the basis of true fellowship; and fellowship in the life of the spirit sustains and strengthens by its inner bonds, and is also effectively manifested in outward helpfulness.

### V

There is still one final thing to be said. We have asked the question: can we, in the midst of the distress and hardship which weighs on our life, still remain anchored in the world of the spirit and of grace? Paul's answer is: not only are we able to do so, but it is this very suffering itself which leads us to depend on the world of grace. Let us note this fact: the first verses of his paradoxes link the antitheses between thisworldly realities and the life of the beyond by means of the conjunction "but":

> "We are afflicted in every way but not crushed,  
> Perplexed but not driven to despair,  
> Persecuted but not forsaken,  
> Struck down but not destroyed."

Then, however, the conjunction "but" is replaced by a "so that": "Always carrying in the body the death of Jesus, so that the life of Jesus may also be manifested in our bodies. For while we live we are always being given up to death for Jesus' sake, so that the life of Jesus may be manifested in our mortal flesh." And in these verses there is another thing to be observed: all that Paul has previously named, affliction and perplexity, persecution and assault, is now included under one heading: "Always carrying in the body the death of Jesus." That is to say that he understands all our sufferings in the light of the cross. For Paul the cross of Christ is not simply an historical event belonging to a remote past, but an event which, initiated in Christ, penetrates the whole of human history; it is the event in which God pronounces this whole world here below, with all its pride and pageantry, all its cruel distress and pain, to be as nothing. This dying of Christ is repeated wherever a man understands that his suffering is meant to serve the purpose of reminding him that this present world is provisional only and ultimately doomed to destruction. And the resurrection life of Christ is actualized wherever man takes up the cross of Christ, which means that he refuses to cling to this-worldly realities, that he lets them slide in order that God may bestow upon him the life of the transcendent world of the spirit.

Hence all our suffering is meant to cause us to become inwardly released from the fascination of the this-worldly and the visible, to become wholly abandoned to the self-bestowing grace of God-that grace which wills to confer upon us true eternal life. This is the life of which we can have no conception; for we can conceive only a life according to the realities of this world. Nevertheless we are permitted to glimpse something of that higher life just because we live here in two worlds, because, while through the body we are involved in the visible, we may raise our hearts and minds to the world of the spirit and of grace. "But we have this treasure in earthen vessels, to show that the transcendent power belongs to God and not to us." What so often appears to us as a torment, indeed as a curse, namely, the fact that we live in two worlds, is in truth a grace; for by it we learn that with all our power "nothing is accomplished"—we learn to obey the first commandment in which all other commands are included, and which requires that we should

. So let us con- "fear, love and trust God above all things" clude with the words with which Luther in the Larger Catechism sums up his comment on the first commandment:

"Hence let us learn well the first commandment, where we see how God will tolerate no presumption, nor trust in any other thing, and demands of us nothing further than a hearty confidence in all that is good; hence that we walk uprightly and straightforwardly on our way in life, and use the wealth which God gives us no further than an old cobbler uses his needle, bodkin and thread, or as a guest uses his inn, food and bed, solely for his temporal need, each man in his place according to God's ordinance; only let nothing on earth become our master or idol."

Hence let us say: "Not to us, O Lord, not to us, but to Thy name give glory" (Ps. 115: I). Let us try to silence our restless tormented hearts with their desires and claims, that the power of God may truly control our lives.

> "Let nothing vex you  
> With lasting sorrow.  
> Be still, O heart;  
> As God ordains,  
> So be content,  
> And bear thy part.  
> "Why bear to-day  
> To-morrow's care?  
> The One above  
> All things controls,  
> Supplies thy needs,  
> In love.  
> "In all thy ways  
> Be steadfast,  
> And rest  
> In what God wills;  
> There lies thy peace,  
> That is the best." 1

> **[1]** P. Fleming.

## Lamentations 3:22–41

*June 23, 1946*

The steadfast love of the Lord never ceases, his mercies never come to an end; they are new every morning; great is thy faithfulness. "The Lord is my portion," says my soul, "therefore I will hope in him."

The Lord is good to those who wait for him, to the soul that seeks him. It is good that one should wait quietly for the salvation of the Lord. It is good for a man that he bear the yoke in his youth.

Let him sit alone in silence when he has laid it on him; let him put his mouth in the dust—there may yet be hope; let him give his cheek to the smiter, and be filled with insults.

For the Lord will not cast off for ever, but, though he cause grief, he will have compassion according to the abundance of his steadfast love; for he does not willingly afflict or grieve the sons of men.

To crush under foot all the prisoners of the earth, to turn aside the right of a man in the presence of the Most

High, to subvert a man in his cause, the Lord does not approve.

Who has commanded and it came to pass, unless the Lord has ordained it? Is it not from the mouth of the Most High that good and evil come? Why should a living man complain, a man, about the punishment of his sins?

Let us test and examine our ways, and return to the Lord! Let us lift up our hearts and hands to God in heaven.

> "The steadfast love of the Lord never ceases,  
> his mercies never come to an end;  
> they are new every morning,  
> great is thy faithfulness."

WHAT a sublime faith expresses itself in these words! Do we not shrink from repeating them? Do we begin each day with an expression of thanksgiving that the mercy and faithfulness of God have once more irradiated our lives? Or do the clouds of bitter anxiety and grief dull our horizon every morning? Do we feel that the pain and suffering of our people, the evidence of which we see everywhere around us, the want and the misery which each day renews, stifle any note of thanksgiving and preclude all joyful trust? "It is the goodness of the Lord that we are not utterly perished." Yes, but perhaps in our case the situation is one of irremediable ruin.

Is it then that the world in which we live is different from that which the pious singer knew? Are the catastrophes which have overtaken us terrible beyond anything in his ex-perience? With the consequence that we must be silent where he could still speak gratefully of God's mercy and faithfulness? Is that really so? Consider how he goes on to speak of his God:

"Thou hast wrapped thyself with anger and pursued us,

slayıng without pity; thou hast wrapped thyself with a cloud so that no prayer can pass through. Thou hast made us offscouring and refuse among the peoples. "All our enemies rail against us; panic and pitfall have come upon us, devastation and destruction; my eyes flow with rivers of tears because of the destruction of the daughter of my people. My eyes will flow without ceasing, without respite, until the Lord from heaven looks down and sees." (3: 43-50.) Is it not as though these words were spoken directly out of the crisis in which we are living, out of the heart of our own misery? The same note is struck throughout the whole Book of Lamentations. It begins:

> "How lonely sits the city  
> that was full of people!  
> How like a widow has she become,  
> she that was great among the nations!" (I: I.)

And it continues:

"All her people groan as they search for bread;

> they trade their treasures for food  
> to revive their strength.  
> Look, O Lord, and behold,  
> for I am despised.'  
> 'Is it nothing to you, all you who pass by?  
> Look and see  
> if there is any sorrow like my sorrow  
> which was brought upon me,  
> which the Lord inflicted  
> on the day of his fierce anger.' " (1: 11, 12.)

"In the dust of the streets

> lie the young and the old;  
> my maidens and my young men  
> have fallen by the sword;  
> in the day of thy anger thou hast slain them,  
> slaughtering without mercy." (2: 21.)

"The tongue of the nursing cleaves

> to the roof of its mouth for thirst;  
> the children beg for food,  
> but no one gives to them." (4: 4.)

"Our inheritance has been turned over to strangers,

> our homes to aliens.  
> We have become orphans, fatherless;  
> our mothers are like widows.  
> We must pay for the water we drink,  
> the wood we get must be bought.  
> With a yoke on our necks we are hard driven;  
> we are weary, we are given no rest." (5: 2-5.)

It is this very God of fierce anger and mercilessness whom the poet praises as the God whose mercies and faithfulness are renewed every morning! What gives him the strength to do so, or the justification to do so? Is not his confidence perhaps a matter of artistry or of neurotic self-deception? He sees all the pain and suffering of the present in the light of a great hope. The ground of his hope is: "The Lord is my portion," says my soul: "therefore will I hope in Him."

"The Lord is my portion"; that means, I confess my faith in the Lord, in my God. He is my portion, which is to say: if I am confronted by the choice and we are all confronted by this choice) —here reigns God in all His inscrutability and terror, God in the majesty of His sovereign will, with His inexorable command, and irrevocable decrees—there, the sway of the world with all that it can give, the possibilities which entice my desire and set a goal to my strivings; if I am confronted by this choice, then "the Lord is my portion" it is the Lord whom I choose. I choose the Lord although then I am building only upon faith and hope and have nothing visible and tangible in my hands; although then I can foresee and calculate nothing, and, humanly speaking, stand wrapped in complete darkness; although then, with all my plans and activities, I must ever make the reservation:

> "As God shall guide, so will I walk,  
> Resigning all self-will."

The leading image in this poem was furnished by an ancient narrative concerning Moses. It was not granted to Moses to see God face to face; as the glory of God passed by, he was permitted to see only God's back:

> "As God shall guide, so will I walk,  
> Though hard and thorny be the way:  
> No man shall see His face and live—  
> Our sunset is His dawn of day."

Luther says in his commentary on the Letter to the Romans: "It is always the case that we understand our own work before it is accomplished; God's work, however, we only understand after it has been accomplished" (II, 205, 5ff.). "For it is necessarily so, that God's work is concealed, and is not understood when it comes to pass. It is always hidden under a form which is the opposite of our human conception" (11, 204, IIfl.). "What is our good, is concealed from us and in fact so deeply concealed that it is disguised under its opposite. Thus our life is hidden under the form of death; the love which concerns us, under the form of hate; honour under disgrace, salvation under ruin, ascendancy under exile, heaven under hell, wisdom under folly, righteousness under sin, power under weakness; and in general every assurance of the good under the refusal of just that good we desire, so that sheer faith in God may come first" (II, 219, 16ff.). "Whoever is wise, accepts God's gifts with patience and joy; but the highest insight is needed if our wisdom is not to focus itself on what lies exposed to our view (in that case we should have to despair), but on what is future, unknown and not exposed to our gaze" (II, 206, 27ff.). Hence this is the choice and decision which lie before us. But whoever can say: "The Lord is my portion", can also continue: "The Lord is good to those who wait for Him, to the soul that seeks Him" (3: 25).

Oh that we might succeed at one stroke in deciding aright this either-or! Then at one stroke the burden of our anxieties would drop from us. Free and unencumbered could we traverse our trials and distresses, inwardly freed from self-will, and no evil could any longer affect us in our inmost self. There may well be people who succeed in doing so, who in a childlike spirit of confidence pass calmly through all terrors, as in the fairy story the lucky child passes cheerful and unharmed among roaming wild beasts which can do him no hurt, because he is magically protected.

But how few there are whose childlike faith as a magic armour protects them from all the assaults of the devil, from doubt and bitter anxiety and fear! We others, who are by far the greater number-must we not ever strive with might and main to secure this magic armour and to appropriate this faith? Must we not repeatedly renew the decision, and win an ever new victory over ourselves as we determine that the Lord shall be our portion? To us is addressed with special force the verse: "It is good that one should wait quietly for the salvation of the Lord" (3: 26). "Wait quietly"- that is what we must learn to do. Such patience implies that we shall ever renew our decision, confess our faith in the Lord, turn away from ourselves and from the world, and hope in "the salvation of the Lord".

227

"Wait quietly"-this attitude however must be manifested in the quite concrete circumstances of our daily life, and precisely now in the crisis through which we are passing. We stand amid the ruins of a world that has collapsed, and we have to build it afresh. Not only is the outer structure of our world shattered—our cities and houses, our resources for trade and production, but the spiritual capital on which a people must live has also been swept away and annihilated: morals, the consciousness of right, and the rule of right itself. Everyone who is called to co-operate in some degree (and to a certain extent we are all so called) realizes what heavy work lies before us. It is work which brooks no delay; but amid all the energy required, and the urgent haste to put our hands to the plough, there is one thing of which we must again and again sharply remind ourselves. Patience is necessary! not patience in regard to our duties, but patience and inner calm regarding our expectations. Not patience as concerns what we have to demand of ourselves, but patience about what we demand of the future.

When here in Marburg a few weeks ago Professor Franz Bohm gave a lecture on the decline of law and right in the Third Reich, and in conclusion turned his thoughts to the future that is to emerge, he ended with the warning: "Have patience!" When in Whitsuntide week foreign experts met German professors to discuss together the crushing problems of international trade we had to listen to the repeated warning issued by foreign friends that we must exercise patience. Too much has happened, too much has been shattered and laid in ruins for our world to be put right again at one stroke and as if the terror through which we have passed had not taken place.

An old tag runs: "A heavy load of impatient zeal is not so powertul as to counterbalance a dram of dire misfortune." And not only is impatience useless but actually mischievous, harming ourselves who are so impatient, who in the illusions we foster can only be dooming ourselves to disappointment; who then, dismayed and in a mood of hopeless resignation, will lose all courage and will no longer wish to co-operate

228 even though the task before us is so urgent and vital. "It is no good, nothing is of any use now!" But have patience, friend! If we warn people thus, how often are we told: "Yes, it is all very fine for you to talk! You are not so badly placed as I!" I think now of the many among us who have lost their jobs and their money. Who could fail to think of them with compassion and not understand their natural impatience? And yet for them too the answer is: patience! Not as a friendly human warning, for then they might justifiably retort: "It is easy for you to talk!" But it is the warning of God's word itself: you must try to bear patiently the load placed on your shoulders.

Impatience only makes things worse and leads you to embittered and foolish thoughts. Impatience is harmful too for the community. How much anger and abuse of others arise from our impatience! On all sides we hear the complaint: "Nothing has become any better than it was formerly! In fact we were better off then than now! then there was more to eat and drink, more tobacco to smoke!" What blindness of impatient folly! As though things could anyhow be other than they are! As though we had not to bear the necessary consequences of our madness and delusions, and bear them bravely so that we might go forward again! In queues, in the bustle of business life and in offices, in the tramshow often is there a lack of patience and courtesy, and in consequence what mutual recriminations, in a spirit of bitterness and hatred! And how many people show an ungrateful and loveless insensibility to the far greater burdens and misery of others! Moreover how much better off are we than those unfortunate ones who, driven from their homes, are seeking refuge with us from East Germany! No doubt we are involved in difficulties in attempting to provide them with shelter, but only patience will enable us to deal effectively with the situation.

And another point. Consider how the spirit of angry impatience leads people astray into downright wrongdoing, into ruthless egoism, and the exploitation of whatever advantages a person may iniquitously come by. "Let others

229 go hungry if only I have enough!" This frame of mind leads to unscrupulous black market traffic and pure robbery.

Such are the fruits of impatience, of the incapacity to bear our burdens and to wait, to lend a hand patiently so that in time the evil may diminish. Impatience leads only to a worsening of our troubles. It poisons the atmosphere. And what we need above all is an atmosphere that is antiseptic, an atmosphere of mutual confidence, mutual helpfulness, composure, cheerfulness and humour. How cleansing and salutary it is if in the press and turmoil where each one has to bear his share of inconvenience, there is to be found someone who manages to preserve his composure, and with a touch of dry humour can make a witty remark. Now, such an atmosphere of trust and helpfulness and humour springs from the spirit of patience.

Again, impatience is mischievous in regard to our relations with our conquerors. There is no doubt about it that their hand lies heavy upon us. But it will only become lighter when we win their confidence. In fact in proportion as we have gained it, we have noticed their readiness to meet us halfway and their good will in endeavouring to ease our lot. By impatience we can only aggravate our evil situation in the world. How cheap are the wild accusations which fly to and fro! How cheap and how foolish! Were we patient we should not always be thinking of what must ever seem so unpleasant and deplorable when foreign troops have occupied our soil. Nor should we be only thinking of the worse things that have been spared us (for things might have been much worse) but we should consider above all the positive help that has been given us, and that readiness to meet us which has been manifested. again and again. The spirit of patience affords us calm of mind for reflection and so the ability to cleave to the good rather than become obsessed by the evil. Impatience forgets the misery that we ourselves have brought upon the world and the suffering we have caused to so many peoples. Will they so easily forget these miseries? We need patience in order to regain their confidence.

Furthermore, impatience blinds to the true situation in regard to ourselves; it deprives us of the power of self-criticism. In our text we read:

> "Why should a living man complain,  
> a man, about the punishment of his sins?" (3: 39.)

Or more precisely translated:

> "What has a living man the right to complain of?  
> It is his sin that each one should lament!"

What then is the ultimate source of the distress and suffering in which we are involved? Is it ultimately the fault of the conquerors that so many of us are without homes and shelter? Are they responsible for the fact that we go hungry? We do not propose in this place to discuss in all its breadth and scope the vexed question of guilt. It is certain, however, that not every one of us is guilty to the same degree, and that there are many innocent people who have been swept into this tide of bitter pain and suffering for which they are in no wise responsible. It is also certain that the question of guilt bears differently on German youth than on the older generation; for the latter the situation is much more oppressive because on them lies the responsibility for youth, and for the young our common distress is much more terrible than for us older people.

It is not our business here to discuss in general terms the question of guilt in so far as this is a political question, a question to be treated as between human beings. There is only one thing we have to say on the point here and now: it is that only patience and inner calm of mind can and should render possible an objective consideration of the matter. We shall never come to an understanding with each other if bitterness and passion prevail. Having said that, we must add: we stand before the sight of God, and in the eyes of the Most Holy no one of us is pure. Everyone is in some way a participant and somehow responsible when in our nation things are not as they should be. And if in our suffering we cry out and complain with embittered and indignant hearts, then we should heed those words which we do not like to hear:

23I

"What has a living man the right to complain of?

> It is his sin that each one should lament!"

But of course this glance which we turn backwards and within, in honest self-examination, should free us to look bravely towards the future also. Self-criticism frees us from ever complaining about our circumstances and other people; it gives us the calm and patient frame of mind to make an objective judgment about men and situations, and it renders us competent to deal with the duties which are incumbent on us.

> "How precious it is to live in patience,  
> and to wait quietly for the salvation of the Lord."

Have we then, in this disposition of mind, the right to hope that our sufferings will one day come to an end, that the lot of our nation will be changed? That once again, truthfulness and integrity, right and justice, will come to prevail in Germany, and that we shall be able to meet each other again and hold intercourse in mutual trust and friendliness? That once more we shall be able to do work that will prosper, and have sufficient to eat? That we shall no longer have to freeze in the bitter cold and that each will have a roof over his head?

Yes certainly, to hope in the Lord means all this. For all these things may we hope if we use patience and tranquillity—patience and calm of mind which cleanses the mental and spiritual atmosphere, and imparts unwearied courage and energy for action.

But it also means a great deal more! And this leads us back to the principle that to be patient means constantly to confess one's faith in the Lord, to decide for Him, to say: "The Lord is my portion." This again means to choose Him at all costs, even though the choice should entail for us the loss of all other things. It means to make the ultimate decision suggested by the words of the Psalmist: "Whom have I in heaven but thee? and there is nothing upon earth that I desire besides thee. My flesh and my heart may fail,

232 but God is the strength of my heart and my portion for ever" (Ps. 73: 25f.).

Does not this, however, imply that we must be ready to renounce all hope? To lay as it were in sacrifice at the feet of God all our hopes that our lot may be transformed? Yes, it does in fact mean that. And is not this the same thing as is suggested in Dante's poem by the inscription which is seen over the gate leading to hell: "Abandon all hope, ye who enter here!" Is then the way to God the way to hopelessness and hell?

Yes and no. The way to God leads not to hell but through hell, or, in Christian terms, through the cross. It leads us not to hopelessness but to a hope which transcends all human hope; and we must silence all human hope, if that divine hope is to dawn for us.

We must make this clear to ourselves: for man as he is, laden with wishes and plans, with longings and hopeand this means for us all, we who form our dream pictures as to how our life should go according to our desire and will— for all of us the way to God is the way into that darkness which for us means hell. There are moments in our life when from our human point of view there seems no difference between God and the devil, when God, quite incomprehensibly to us, destroys our dearest and loveliest; and not only that which forms the goal of our desires, and brings the greatest happiness into our lives, but often too those things of which we think we may say that they have been initiated and built up by the aid of His spirit, and in the spirit of the good and noble. The breath of the Lord can sweep away our all in a second and for our eyes there is nothing left but a comfortless waste. That is the meaning of God: His majesty annihilates whatever stands independently. His word is a word that slays.

This hell we must traverse; before the life of the resurrection stands the cross. "It is the essence of God," says Luther, "first to destroy what is in us before He bestows on us His gifts. ... We are not able to understand His works and His plans until our plans vanish and our activities are stilled;

233 until, in the vision of God, we have become quite quiescent (passivi) both as regards our inner and outer life."1 "This is certainly harsh and painful. For that the soul should be denuded of all its motions of understanding and willing, signifies its entrance into darkness, as it were, the darkness of destruction and death; and from this extremity it shrinks violently."2 "This happens however through the faith in which man surrenders his understanding to the word of the cross, in the power of which he renounces himself and his all, becoming dead to himself and to all things. And thus he lives for God alone, for whom all live, even the dead." 3 "For God saves none but the sinner, He instructs none but the foolish and unwise, He makes none rich but the poor, He makes none living but the dead."4 "For him who refuses to take upon himself the suffering of the cross, God, the Creator, does not exist at all; for such a one refuses to become as nothing that the Creator might be manifested to him."5 Then only do we love God "when there is nothing visible, tangible for us any more, neither within nor without, to which confidence, love or fear could turn, but solely He who is above all things, the invisible, the unapproachable, the incomprehensible, God. This means, however, when man is torn away into the heart of darkness, and does not know what he should love, when he only knows what he should not love; when he repudiates all that he can know and experience, and longs only for the One whom he does not yet know."6 "Thus hope bears us away into the unknown, the hidden, the innermost darkness. It knows not for what it should hope; it knows only for what it must not hope."

Should anyone reply: "When Luther speaks thus about man's being led into the realms of darkness, he has in mind only very special life experiences; it does not happen to all men that their way leads through suffering!" I do not know whether any one to-day is thinking on these lines, to-day when all of us are involved in suffering and deprivation, and when even those who are better off than most have

> **[1]** Rom. II, 203, 4M.

> **[2]** 11, 206, 14f.

> **[3]** II, 245, 12ff.

> **[4]** II, 252, 18ff.

> **[5]** II, 135, 24ff.

> **[7]** II, 202, 5ff.

> **[6]** II, 138, 28ff.

to bear their cross. But if anyone really supposes that there is a way to God which does not pass through the experience of utter tranquillization and submergence of which Luther speaks, that there is any man whom God does not constantly remind, through experience, that he is essentially nothing, then we must answer: such a one is only cherishing illusions; he entirely fails to see the realities of life; and even though his own life may pass outwardly without storm and stress, he is blind to what God is ever showing him if only he will see it. Is there after all any life without disillusionment and suffering? And can a so-called happy man, in whose life the measure of suffering is trifling, close his eyes to the suffering of others? must he not also suffer under the afflictions which strike down his fellow men? And does not death face us all, casting its shadow before it? No, if anyone imagines that there is a way to the living God which does not pass through the heart of darkness, then without realizing it he is already involved in the deepest darkness from which there is no issue—unless it be that he comes to realize that he is treading the way of darkness.

May it be that the language of Luther has become strange to us and difficult to understand? In that case, what he says may be perhaps more intelligibly expressed for us in a poem of Eichendorff:

> "What we see is ever changing,  
> Daylight sinks to sunset glow:  
> Anguish with desire is mingled,  
> Death o'ershadowing all we know.  
> "Furtive as a thief in darkness  
> Sorrow glides across our way.  
> Look thy last on all things lovely':  
> Let them go; thou canst not stay.  
> "What delight on earth were lett us,  
> How could man his tears restrain  
> Cursing life that gave him being,  
> Didst not Thou in heaven reign?  
> "Thou it is, who gently breakest  
> These fair shelters we invent,  
> That eternal light may wake us;  
> Therefore do I not lament."

The poems of Eichendorff may seem to us to-day to be an echo from a happier, now vanished, world. But this poem shows that in the last resort it is not a question of a greater or lesser degree of good fortune, and that even in a so-called prosperous age a serious-minded man sees how our life is shadowed by death, and that the way to God leads through the "dark night of the soul": "Thou it is who gently breakest the fair shelters we build, that eternal light may break upon us" —this is the very same truth that Luther's words sharply enforced.

And is all that I have been saying a Christian sermon about the grace of God? Most surely; for the message of divine grace does not suggest that the cross will be spared us, but rather that the cross itself is grace; that God slays in order to make alive. And real genuine patience of disposition consists in this—in our readiness to hear, in the "no" by which God negates our desires and our will, His secret "yes". And in this sense let us take to heart the truth of the following words in our text:

"It is good for a man that he bear the yoke in his youth. Let him sit alone in silence when he has laid it on him; let him put his mouth in the dust— there may yet be hope; let him give his cheek to the smiter, and be filled with insults. For the Lord will not cast off for ever, but, though he cause grief, he will have compassion according to the abundance of his steadfast love; for he does not willingly afflict or grieve the sons of men.

> To crush under foot  
> all the prisoners of the earth,  
> to turn aside the right of a man  
> in the presence of the Most High,  
> to subvert a man in his cause,  
> the Lord does not approve.  
> "Who has commanded and it came to pass,  
> unless the Lord has ordained it?  
> Is it not from the mouth of the Most High  
> that good and evil come?" (3: 27-38.)

Let us then receive in patience, as from God's hand, what He sends us, and address our hopes to the world of unseen realities. It is just in so doing that we shall win true inner freedom for the urgent tasks of the present, courage to accomplish the work which in the distress of our days is laid upon us. For then no disillusionment can paralyse our strength; then we have become unassailable. Patience which, abandoning earthly hopes, nevertheless continues to hope, bears within itself the seeds of fulfilment. The way into the darkness of death is already lightened by the dawn of resurrection life. If we decide for God, we shall become inwardly aware that God has already decided for us. Then we shall not only "rejoice in our hope of sharing the glory of God", but, "more than that, we rejoice in our sufferings, knowing that suffering produces endurance, and endurance produces character, and character produces hope, and hope does not disappoint us, because God's love has been poured into our hearts through the Holy Spirit which has been given to us" (Rom. 5: 3-5).

In order that this experience may truly be ours, let us now heed the final words of our text:

> "Let us test and examine our ways,  
> and return to the Lord!  
> Let us lift up our hearts and hands  
> to God in heaven!"

237

## St. Mark 13:31–33

*July 25, 1950*

"Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away.

"But of that day or that hour no one knows, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son, but only the Father. Take heed, watch; for you do not know when the time will come." THIs is the final service of the term. It is an hour of farewell, in which we bid goodbye to the term and all that it has brought us in the way of tasks completed. I hope also that it has enriched us with new joy, new impressions and new insights, with knowledge that has been inspiring and also perhaps a little frightening; in any event I hope that we have been both stirred and encouraged. For many also it is an hour of farewell to the place in which we have worked together, to the town of Marburg and its surrounding country. And the more our life is consciously or unconsciously moulded and coloured by the character of the environment in which we move, so much the more does our departure from it strike for us a solemn and significant note. Above all it is a time in which we bid farewell to each other, we who have been linked together as learners and teachers; and while for some it means only a temporary departure which will be followed by another term of work together, for many too it is a final farewell or at any rate a farewell for some considerable time, broken at most by occasional meetings later on.

This farewell service should unite us once again as we consider together and ask ourselves what such an hour as the present ought to mean in the light of God's word. Perhaps it may surprise us that in the Bible we do not easily find any message bearing directly on those moments in our life which to us are so important, as for example many an event in the circle of family or bourgeois life, or the alternation of the seasons, or even such an occasion as the end of a term. The word of God wishes to strike us on a deeper level of life than that of the natural feelings and thoughts which stir our minds in a moment of farewell; feelings of joyous thankfulness and sorrowful goodbye, of hopes for the future or even anxieties about the future-thoughts and feelings which certainly have their rightful place in our lives but which receive their true meaning only as we bring them into vital connexion with that deeper level of life in which God's word strikes home to us and finds us.

God's word tells us in the first place that such an hour of farewell as the present implies nothing of particular significance in God's sight. Its meaning lies rather in the fact that it brings home to our minds the truth that our whole life is a constant process of bidding goodbye. It warns us to consider that we live immersed in the flux of time, and it teaches us to ask where eternity lies.

"Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away." "Heaven and earth will pass away" —which means that at some time or other they will come to an end, for they are essentially transitory and at bottom they are passing away all the time. "But of that day or that hour no one knows, not even the angels in heaven, nor the Son, but only the Father"; that is, the day and the hour when this term will be reached. Do we then need to know it? Whether this end be near or far, the terrible thing is really not that our surrounding world will pass away, but that we ourselves who are framed and enclosed by it are frail perishable mortals; that our lives will reach their term, when heaven and earth will in any case pass away for us, even though they continue to subsist for a time as far as coming generations are concerned. Nor do we know the day and hour of our own end. "Ultima latet"—the last hour is hidden from our knowledge-this inscription on an old clock applies not only to the world in general but to us individuals in particular.

"Lord, so teach us to number our days that we may get a heart of wisdom" (Ps. 90: 12). Thus the Psalmist sings, and

239 in that other psalm, whose solemn words ring forth so impressively in the German Requiem of Brahms, we read: "Lord, let me know my end, and what is the measure of my days; let me know how fleeting my life is! Behold, thou hast made my days a few handbreadths, and my lifetime is as nothing in thy sight. Surely every man stands as a mere breath! Surely man goes about as a shadow! Surely for nought are they in turmoil; man heaps up, and knows not who will gather!" (Ps. 39: 4-6.)

But the fact that our days will come to their natural term is not the only solemn and fearful thought. There is also the further fact that the whole texture of our life is stamped by the mark of transitoriness. There is another inscription on a timepiece found by the Duke of York in a monastery at Amalfi and which he noted in his diary: "dies nostri quasi umbra super terram, et nulla est mora"—i.e. "Our days are like the shadow which moves over the face of the earth, and suffer no delay."

"Linger, and you'll regret it: time has sped

> How soon! day stands not still, nor comes again.  
> How soon the earth's resplendent colours fade!  
> The poplar sheds her silver leaf, how soon!"1

—so writes the ancient poet, and likewise the modern:

"This is a fact which none fully ponders, and far too awesome for weak complaining: the hours glide on, all fades in veils of mist."2 Everywhere and at all times, men have known and been poignantly aware of this underlying truth of human life. Heathen as well as Jews and Christians have felt it, and the voices of the Old Testament, together with those of the heathen poets in the ancient east and classical antiquity and of the poets of modern times, form one vast choir singing in unison to this theme.

Now what follows from this? Firstly, the fact that it was not first the Christians, but before them the Jews and heathen too who sadly realized that the swift flight of time warns us of its preciousness, of the preciousness of the immediate

> **[2]** v. Hoffmannsthal.

> **[1]** Tibullus, 1, 4, 27-30.

present, the moment in which we stand. This realization, however, may be diversely interpreted:

"And I commend enjoyment, for man has no good thing under the sun but to eat, and drink, and enjoy himself, for this will go with him in his toil through the days of life which God gives him under the sun" (Ecclesiastes 8: 15). "Go, eat your bread with enjoyment, and drink your wine with a merry heart. ... Let your garments be always white; let not oil be lacking on your head. Enjoy life with the wife whom you love, all the days of your vain life..." (9: 7-9). Thus speaks the so-called preacher, Solomon, and the very same note is struck in the Carpe Diem of Horace and our own "Gather ye rosebuds while ye may!"

All this, however, remains on the surface. Let us listen rather to the words of Pascal which he writes in the second section of his Pensées where he treats of the "Wretchedness of man without God", and in which he constantly speaks of the manifold distractions (divertissements) which absorb men's minds, of that spirit of frivolity which loses time because it loses the present moment:

"Never do we live whole-heartedly in the present. We anticipate the future, as though it were too slow in coming, as though we wished to hasten its advent; or else we recall the past, as though to arrest it in its swift flight. We are so foolish that we allow our thoughts to stray in periods of time which are not really ours, and we do not think of the sole point of time which veritably belongs to us. We are so stupid that we dwell on a past which no longer subsists, and thoughtlessly miss the time which is present with us. The truth is that the present is normally offensive to us. We refuse to face it because it vexes us; and if it is pleasant we idly deplore its passing. We try to keep it by merging it with the future, and we fondly think we can arrange matters which do not lie within our control, with a view to a point of time which we have no certainty of ever reaching. Let each one examine his thoughts. He will discover that they are all concerned either with the past or the future. We hardly ever think of the present; and when we do think of it, it is only to shed its light on the future. The present is never an end for us. Thus we never live, but only hope to live; and since we are always preparing ourselves to be happy, it is inevitable that we never in fact are happy."1

What is it however that lends weight and meaning to our present, which, in spite of its swift passage, nevertheless lifts it out of the flux of time to fuse it with eternity? Might we answer: the fact that the present is to be used for the fulfilment of a task? But if this task is nothing more than the realization of a plan, the accomplishment of some piece of work, then the present is seen only from the standpoint of a future which will come and go; it is not yet seen in the light of eternity.

Or should we say that the present is determined by an unconditional obligation, that of the beautiful, the true and the good? That the present is the moment when we are faced by the call of duty, of responsibility? And is it not really the case that if we understand our present as confronting us with duty to be done and responsibility to be fulfilled, then we are freed from the melancholy of a retrospective glance into the past, as also from the anxious anticipation of the future? Is not the call to responsible action a call which comes to us from eternal horizons, a call which rescues our here-and-now from the inexorable flight of time, and sheds upon it the radiance of the eternal? Which gives abiding reality to what in itself is nothing?

I believe that we may and must say so much. And this call of eternity which delivers us from time into the here-andnow of responsibility, is understood by the Christian faith to be also the call of God. Nevertheless this is not the final and true answer which faith gives to the question: how is eternal reality imparted to our present?

For if we content ourselves with this answer, are we not eluding the mystery and the full scope of the problem of transience? Is our life entirely a matter of responsible action and the fulfilment of duty? Is it not also and to an equal degree a matter of passive reception and suffering? Is it not

> **[1]** Pensées, 172.

also a life of encounter with others, who enrich and inspire us, who give us joy and dower us with love, or who test and train us through contradiction, and by compelling us to prove the genuineness of our thinking and willing? Is not our life also a life of pregnant destiny—harsh and cruel at times, at times also joyous and inspiring?

All this which gives content and substance to our life, passes away. And does not the curse of transience render it all vain and meaningless? Is life an exciting drama but a drama which dies away into nothingness? Or has it all an eternal import, or can it have such? Do not these questions press sorely on our minds when death snatches from us those to whom we were linked in the bonds of love? Do they not vex also to-day when we have seen the disappearance of so much that we thought great and beautiful and valuable, to-day, when we are caught up in a crisis which makes almost everything seem questionable, and which threatens with imminent destruction what hitherto seemed to give our life content and meaning? And can we truly comfort by such reference to duty and the call of responsibility those countless thousands whose lives have apparently become meaningless through loss of home and shelter, through servitude and misery, through want and hunger? Indeed, is it not the fact that for many people this very transitoriness of things has become their solace? That life for them has become dreamlike:

"The most frightening dream is accompanied by a secret feeling that it all means nothing, howsoever we sweat with fear."1

And finally, what are we to say in face of the realization that we fail to fulfil this summons of eternity? Are we not aware that we have often failed to obey it? Has not our time so often been misspent and wasted through our own fault? How seldom has been, how seldom is, our present time a genuinely fulfilled time, determined and enriched by obedience to the eternal will! Certainly the summons to responsibility and the call of duty are meant to give our

> **[1]** Hebbel.

present an eternal meaning; but does it not tax our inner resources to fulfil that meaning? And are we not presuming too much of ourselves in supposing that by our strength alone we can impart to our life an eternal quality? But is not the summons of God's eternal will not merely a demand but also the promise of grace?

"Heaven and earth will pass away, but my words will not pass away." These words imply something further than the call to responsible existence; they imply also the call of grace which bestows upon our present that quality of eternal life in which we must live. How does this come about?

In the first place, because these words, sounding from eternity, remind us of that truth which of course we can become aware of in other ways: "Heaven and earth will pass away!" These words also emphasize our transience and ultimate nothingness; but the characteristic thing about them, what distinguishes them from the words of human insight and wisdom, is just this: that while in themselves they warn us of our transience and perishability, they are also words of promise. It is as if in the "no" which negates our existence is concealed God's secret "yes". These words declare to us: if our aspiration to eternity is genuine, then it is not to be satisfied by the possibility that the fleeting, to which we would fain cling, may receive permanence, but conversely by our surrendering it. They do not however suggest that in a mood of resignation we should surrender the aspiration itself. They declare rather that the confession of our transitoriness and perishability and the surrender of the fleeting things of time are precisely the ways of grasping the eternal; or better, of allowing it to become to us as a gift. They teach us to know God as the One who "gives life to the dead and calls into existence the things that do not exist" (Rom. 4: 17); they bid us pronounce the death sentence on ourselves by refusing to place our trust in ourselves, by trusting rather in God who raises the dead (2 Cor. I: 9). As creatures we are called to recognize God as the Creator. But to come to have faith in God as the Creator we must, in the words of Luther, be dead to all things, to good and ill, to life and death, to heaven and hell, and from the depth of our hearts recognize that in our own power we can avail nothing. It is the very nature and way of God first to destroy and annihilate what is in us before He bestows on us His gift.2

This is the truth which Jesus expresses not only in the words: "For whoever would save his life will lose it; and whoever loses his life for my sake, he will save it" (Lk. 9: 24) —but furthermore in His cross.
